\begin{exercise}
A grammar is 
\definend{right linear}\index{right linear}\index{grammar!right linear}\index{grammar!linear} 
if every production rule has the
form \mbox{\ntrm{N}\produces \trm{x}\ntrm{M}}, where the right side has
a single terminal followed by a single nonterminal.
With this right linear grammar
we can associate this nondeterministic Finite State machine.
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{1.25in}
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} \trm{a} A

A \produces{} \trm{a} A \syntaxor \trm{b} B

B \produces{} \trm{b} B \syntaxor b
\end{grammar}
\end{minipage}
\hspace*{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm68.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give three strings from the language of the grammar
and show that they are accepted by the machine.
\item Describe that language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is a derivation of \str{abb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S
        \derives \trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    This is a derivation of \str{aabb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S 
        \derives \trm{a}A
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    And, this is a derivation of \str{abbb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S 
        \derives \trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    This verifies that the first is accepted by the machine.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{abb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
    The second is here.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{aabb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{abb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
    The third is similar.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{abbb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bbb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
  \item The language of the machine and the grammar is 
    $\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\suchthat i>0, j>1}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Decide whether each problem is solvable or
unsolvable by a Turing machine.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_{DFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the deterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\item $\lang_{NFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the nondeterministic machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\end{exparts}
% (Assume that $\FSM$ is described by using bitstrings 
% to encode the transition function.)
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each of these is solvable.  
  For each we will sketch an algorithm.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Clearly we can write a simulator for deterministic Finite
  State machines.
  This simulator is uniform in that it takes in the $\Delta$ table and
  the input string.
  By Church's Thesis since we can write it on a modern computer, we
  can write one for a Turing machine.
  When given an input~$\sigma$, the machine will consume it in
  no more than $\sizeof{\sigma}$ transitions, and so 
  the simulation is sure to halt.
  Then we can just check whether the ending state is an accepting state.
  \item   
  The section body discusses how to simulate a nondeterministic computation,
  by dovetailing (that is, time-slicing).
  Or, we can use the powerset algorithm to convert it to a deterministic 
  machine and then simulate that.
  We can then answer questions about whether this machine accepts 
  a given input~$\sigma$ just as in the prior item.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Given a nondeterministic Finite State machine without $\varepsilon$ transitions
% and with two accepting states, show how to add $\varepsilon$
% transitions so that the extended machine will have only one accepting state,
% without changing the recognized language. 
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% \zcpageref{par:NFSMAccepted} has a description of
% when an input is accepted by a
% nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$ without $\varepsilon$ transitions.
% Here is a definition that more formal, and constructive,
% meaning that you could follow it to
% write a program to compute whether the string is accepted.
% We define the \definend{extended transition function} 
% $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{\powerset(Q)}$
% giving the set of states that the machine will be in after starting in the
% given state and consuming the given string.
% (So \zcref{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree} illustrates the
% computation of $\hat{\Delta}(q_0,\str{00001})$.)
% Where the input string is empty
% $\sigma=\emptystring$,
% for each state $q\in\FSM$ let $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q}$.
% Where the input string is not empty,
% peel off the final character, $\sigma=\sigma_0\concat x$ where $x\in\Sigma$,
% and for each $q\in Q$ define $\hat{\Delta}(\sigma)$ by:
% \begin{equation*}
%   \hat{\Delta}(q,\sigma)=
%     \hat{\Delta}(q_{i_0},x)\cup\hat{\Delta}(q_{i_1},x)\cup \,\cdots\,\hat{\Delta}(q_{i_k},x)
% \end{equation*}
% where $\Delta(q,\sigma_0)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\, \ldots\,q_{i_k}}$.
% Then where $F_{\FSM}$ is the set of final states,
% the language of the machine is 
% $\lang(\FSM)=\set{\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
%   \hat{\Delta}(q_0,\tau)\cap F_{\FSM}\neq\emptyset}$.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item For the machine of \zcref{ex:NDFMFromDiscussion},
%   find $\hat{\Delta}(q,\emptystring)$ for each $q\in Q$.
% \item Find $\hat{\Delta}(q,\str{0})$
%   and $\hat{\Delta}(q,\str{1})$ for each $q\in Q$.
% \item Find $\hat{\Delta}(q,x)$ for each $q\in Q$ and $x\in\B^2$.
% \item Continue until you find
%   $\hat{\Delta}(q,x)$ for each $q\in Q$ and $x\in\B^5$.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:DefnEpsilonClosure}
  % \zcref{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure} illustrated following $\varepsilon$ transitions.
  % We can give a rigorous definition.
  % For each state $q\in Q$ we will define the
  % \definend{$\varepsilon$ closure} $E(q)\subseteq Q$.
  % The definition is inductive, so we get a sequence of sets
  % $E_0(q)\subseteq E_1(q)\subseteq \cdots E_k(q)=E(q)$.
  % The intiution is that $E_i(q)$ is the set of states reachable
  % from~$q$ by traversing $i$ or fewer $\varepsilon$ edges.
  % Formally, the definition's base step is that for each state, $E_0(q)=\set{q}$.
  % The inductive step starts with $E_i(q)=\set{q_{i_0},\,\ldots q_{i_j}}$
  % for some $i\in\N$ and produces $E_{i+1}(q)$
  % \begin{equation*}
  %   E_{i+1}(q)=E_i(q)\cup \Delta(q_{I_0},\varepsilon)\cup\,\cdots\,
  %                        \Delta(q_{i_j},\varepsilon)
  % \end{equation*}
  \begin{exparts*} 
  \item For the machine of \zcref{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure},
    for each $q\in Q$ produce $E(q,0)$,
    $E(q,1)$, $E(q,2)$, and~$E(q,3)$.
    List $\hat{E}(q)$ for each $q\in Q$.
  \item Do the same for \zcref{exer:MFSMWithEpsilonTrans}'s machine.
  % \item This machine has loops of $\varepsilon$'s.
  %   \begin{center}
  %     \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm31.pdf}
  %   \end{center}
  %   Does the definition just keep going around; what is $\hat{E}(q)$ for each
  %   $q\in Q$?
  % \item Prove that for every machine, $\hat{E}(q)$ is 
  %   defined for every $q\in Q$.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Here is the list.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$E(q,m)$}} 
                         &\tabulartext{$m=0$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$1$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$2$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$3$}  
  \\ \cline{2-5}
  $q_0$        &$\set{q_0}$ &$\set{q_0,q_3}$ &$\set{q_0,q_3}$ &$\set{q_0,q_3}$  \\
  $q_1$        &$\set{q_1}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$  \\
  $q_2$        &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_2}$   \\
  $q_3$        &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
So, $\hat{E}(q_0)=\set{q_0,q_3}$, $\hat{E}(q_1)=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$,
$\hat{E}(q_2)=\set{q_2}$, and~$\hat{E}(q_3)=\set{q_3}$. 
\item This is similar.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$E(q,m)$}} 
                         &\tabulartext{$m=0$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$1$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$2$}  
                         &\tabulartext{$3$}  
  \\ \cline{2-5}
  $q_0$         &$\set{q_0}$ &$\set{q_0}$     &$\set{q_0}$     &$\set{q_0}$ \\
  $q_1$         &$\set{q_1}$ &$\set{q_1,q_2}$ &$\set{q_1,q_2}$ &$\set{q_1,q_2}$\\
  $q_2$         &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_2}$     &$\set{q_2}$     &$\set{q_2}$   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
So, $\hat{E}(q_0)=\set{q_0}$, $\hat{E}(q_1)=\set{q_1, q_2}$,
and $\hat{E}(q_2)=\set{q_2}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% A deterministic Finite State machine is basically a nondeterministic one;
% turn it into one by taking the transition function table and making each
% entry a one-element set.
% What happens if you then do the construction to create a deterministic
% machine from it? 
% \end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{nondeterminism|)}







% ==================================================
\section{Regular expressions}\index{regular expression|(}
\margingraphic{0.15\textwidth}{automata/pix/kleene.jpg}{\label{fig:kleene}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cole_Kleene}{Stephen Kleene 1909--94}}\index{Kleene, S!picture}
In 1951, S~Kleene\footnote{%
  \citetext{Pronounced KLAY-nee}{His son Ken Kleene, wrote,
  ``As far as I am aware this pronunciation is incorrect in all known languages.
  I believe that this novel pronunciation was invented by my father.''
  \autocite{fodc:Kleene}}.
  Like Turing, he was a student of Church's.} 
was studying a
\citetext{mathematical model of neurons}{\autocite{wiki:ArtificialNeuron}}.
These are like a Finite State machine in that they 
do not have scratch memory.
% Because of that connection, his work is related to what's here. 
He noted patterns in the languages that such machines recognize.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa15.pdf}
\end{center}
For example, this Finite State machine 
accepts strings that
have any number of \str{b}'s
followed by at least one~\str{a},
optionally then followed by some number of repetitions 
of a pattern of at least one \str{b} and then
at least one~\str{a}.

He introduced a way to describe the languages that makes it
convenient to express 
constructs such as `any number of'.


\subsection{Definition}
% A regular expression is a string that describes a language.
% \footnote{%
%   We will call the languages `regular' in that they have regularities.
%   The expressions describe the languages.}
We will start with 
a few examples,
using $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\ldots \str{z}}$.

%<*ex:RegexOne>
\begin{example}
The regular expression \re{p(a|e|i|o|u)t}
describes the language of strings that start with~\str{p},
\citetext{have a vowel in the middle}{%
  Most speakers of American English cite the vowels as `a', `e', `i',
  `o', and~`u'.  
  See \autocite{Bigham}.},
and end with~\str{t}.
That is, this regular expression
describes the language consisting of five words, 
$\lang=\set{\str{pat}, \str{pet}, \str{pit},\str{pot}, \str{put}}$.
\end{example}

The \definend{alternation}\index{alternation} operator `|'\footnote{%
  \protect\citetext{This symbol is called `pipe'.}{%
  Other notations for alternation are `$+$' and `$\cup$'.}}\index{pipe@| pipe alternation operator} 
denoting `or',
and the parentheses to provide grouping, 
are not part of the strings being described;~they
are \definend{metacharacters}.\index{metacharacter}

Besides alternation and parentheses,
the regular expression in that example also includes concatenation since
the language consists of strings where the initial \str{p} 
is concatenated with a vowel,
which in turn is concatenated with \str{t}.
%</ex:RegexOne>

%<*ex:RegexTwo>
\begin{example}
The regular expression \re{ab*c}
describes the language
of words begin with an~$\str{a}$, followed by any number of $\str{b}$'s
(including possibly zero-many $\str{b}$'s), and ending with  
a~$\str{c}$, that is, $\lang=\set{\str{ac}, \str{abc}, \str{abbc},\, \ldots}$.
Thus,~`$*$'  %`$\kleenestar{}$'
means `repeat any number of times, 
including possibly zero-many times'.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexTwo>

% \begin{example}
% For the machine that opened this subsection, the
% regular expression~\re{a*ba*} describes the language that the 
% machine recognizes.
% \end{example}

% We first describe the syntax of regular expressions.

\begin{definition}\index{regular expression} \label{def:RESyntax}
%<*def:RESyntax>
Let $\Sigma=\set{x_0,x_1,\ldots\, x_n}$ 
be an alphabet not containing % the meta\-characters
`\re{)}', `\re{(}', `\re{|}', or `\re{*}'.
A \definend{regular expression}\index{regular expression!syntax}
over~$\Sigma$ is a string in the language of this grammar.\footnotemark{} %

\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{reg-exp} \produces{} \ntrm{char} \syntaxor \ntrm{alter} \syntaxor  \ntrm{concat} \syntaxor  \ntrm{star} 

\ntrm{char} \produces{} $\emptyset$ \syntaxor \emptystring
                    \syntaxor $x_0$ \syntaxor $x_1$ \syntaxor \ldots{}\; $x_n$

\ntrm{alter} \produces{} \trm{(} \ntrm{reg-exp} `\trm{|}' \ntrm{reg-exp} \trm{)}

\ntrm{concat} \produces{} \trm{(} \ntrm{reg-exp} \ntrm{reg-exp} \trm{)}

\ntrm{star} \produces{} \trm{(} \ntrm{reg-exp} \trm{)*}
\end{grammar}
%</def:RESyntax>
\end{definition}\footnotetext{%
  As with other grammars in this book,
  here we use the pipe symbol~\re{|} as a metacharacter
  to collapse rules with the same left side; see the first and second lines.
  But this symbol also appears in regular expressions.
  For that usage,
  this grammar wraps it in single quotes, as \protect\smash{`\protect\trm{|}'}.}
In words, any of the single character strings 
$\emptyset$ or \emptystring{} or $x_0$, \ldots{} or $x_n$ 
are regular expressions. 
And, where $R_0$ and $R_1$ are regular expressions then so also
are \re{($R_0$|$R_1$)}, and \re{($R_0R_1$)}, 
and \re{($R_0$)*}. 
Thus examples of regular 
expressions over $\Sigma=\set{ \str{a},\str{b} }$ are these four:
\trm{(}\str{ab}\trm{)}, and \trm{(}\str{a}\trm{|}\str{b}\trm{)},
and \str{b}, and \trm{(}\str{a}\trm{(}\str{b}\trm{)*)}.
(Below we will discuss conventions for omitting some parentheses.)

That definition gives the syntax of regular expressions, the rules for
the structure of valid strings.
As to their semantics, 
what they mean,\index{regular expression!semantics}
a regular expression describes a language.
%<*discussion:RESemantics>
The language described by the
one-character regular expression \re{$\emptyset$} is
the empty language, $\lang(\emptyset)=\emptyset$.
The language described by the regular expression consisting
of only the character~\re{$\emptystring$}
is the one-element language containing only the empty string, 
$\lang(\varepsilon)=\set{\emptystring}=\set{\text{`\,'}}$.
If the regular expression consists of a single character 
from the alphabet~$\Sigma$
then the language that it describes contains only one string, which
consists of only that single character, 
as in $\lang(\str{a})=\set{\str{a}}$.

Now for the semantics of the operations.
Start with regular expressions $R_0$ and~$R_1$
describing languages $\lang(R_0)$ and~$\lang(R_1)$.
The alternation of two regular expressions \re{($R_0$|$R_1$)} 
describes the union of 
their languages, $\lang(R_0)\cup\lang(R_1)$.
Concatenation \re{($R_0R_1$)}
describes concatenation of the languages,
$\lang(R_0)\concat\lang(R_1)$.
And, the Kleene star\index{Kleene star!regular expression} 
\re{($R_0$)*} describes the star of the language 
$\kleenestar{\lang(R)}\!$.
%</discussion:RESemantics>

\begin{example}
Consider the regular expression \re{(ab)} over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.  
It is the concatenation of the regular expression~\re{a} with 
the regular expression~\re{b}.
The first describes a single-element language $\lang(\str{a})=\set{\str{a}}$
and likewise the second describes $\lang(\str{b})=\set{\str{b}}$.
Thus $\lang(\str{(ab)})$ is another language with only one element,
$\lang(\str{(ab)})=\lang(\str{a})\concat \lang(\str{b})=\set{\str{ab}}$.
\end{example}

\index{regular expression!operator precedence}
As formally defined, the syntax rules call for lots of parentheses.
We cut down on the annoyance by adopting a convention for operator
precedence:~star binds most tightly, then concatenation, and then
the alternation operator,~\re{|}, binds least tightly.

\begin{example}
Instead of \re{(a(b(a)*))} we write \re{aba*}.
The precedence rules give that the star applies only to the \str{a} before it,
as in \re{ab(a*)}.
(If concatenation bound more tightly than star then \re{aba*} would instead mean
\re{(aba)*}.) 
The described language is
$\lang(\re{aba*})
  =\set{\str{ab}, \str{aba}, \str{abaa}, \str{ab}\str{a}^3\!,\ldots}
  =\set{\str{ab}\str{a}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}  \label{ex:RegexABOrCStar}
The  precedence conventions do not eliminate the need for parentheses.
Compare \re{b|c*} and \re{b*|c*} and \re{(b|c)*}.
For the first, because \re{*} binds more tightly than \re{|},
the \str{c} is bound to the star and so 
it means `one \str{b} or any number of \str{c}'s'.
Similarly the second means
`any number of \str{b}'s or any number of \str{c}'s'.
So we need parentheses to express the third,
`any number of repetitions of picking \str{b} or~\str{c}',
whose language is
$\lang=\set{\emptystring, \str{b}, \str{c}, 
             \str{bb}, \str{bc}, \str{cb}, \str{cc}, \ldots}$.
\end{remark}

We next see a few common constructs.
These examples use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.

%<*ex:RegexThree>
\begin{example}
The language consisting of strings of \str{a}'s whose 
length is a multiple of three,
$\lang=\set{a^{3k}\suchthat k\in\N}=\set{\emptystring,\str{aaa},\str{aaaaaa},\,\ldots}$, 
is described by \re{(aaa)*}.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexThree>

Note that the empty string is a member of that language.
A common mistake is to forget that star includes 
the possibility of zero-many repetitions.

\begin{example}
% <*ex:RegexFive>
Any finite language is described by a regular expression.  
We can just list all of its strings:~the
language % over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$ 
$\set{\str{abc}, \str{bbc}, \str{cbc}}$
is described by the regular expression \re{(abc)|(bbc)|(cbc)}.
%</ex:RegexFive>
\end{example}

\begin{example}
%<*ex:RegexSix>
As well, to match any character we can list them all.
The language of the prior example consists  
of three-letter words ending in \str{bc}, which we can describe
with the regular expression \re{(a|b|c)bc}.
%</ex:RegexSix>
\end{example}

%<*ex:RegexFour>
\begin{example}
We can use~\re{$\emptystring$} to mark things as optional.
Thus \re{a*($\emptystring$|b)} describes the language 
of strings that have any number
of \str{a}'s and optionally end in one~\str{b},
$\lang=\set{\emptystring,\str{b},\str{a},\str{ab},\str{aa},\str{aab},\ldots}$.
Similarly, to describe the language consisting of words with 
between three and five $\str{a}$'s,
$\lang=\set{\str{aaa}, \str{aaaa}, \str{aaaaa}}$, we can 
use \re{aaa(\emptystring|a|aa)}.
\end{example}
%</ex:RegexFour>

% \begin{example}
% The language
% $\set{\str{b},\str{bc},\str{bcc},
%              \str{ab},\str{abc},\str{abcc},
%              \str{aab},\,\ldots}$
% has words starting with any number of $\str{a}$'s
% (including zero-many \str{a}'s),
% followed by a single~$\str{b}$, and then ending in fewer than three
% $\str{c}$'s. 
% To describe it we can use 
% \re{a*b(\emptystring|c|cc)}. 
% \end{example}

Also see \zcref{extra:Regexs} for extensions that
are widely used in practice.



% -----------------------------------------
\subsection{Kleene's Theorem}\index{Kleene's theorem|(}
The next result justifies our study of regular expressions because it shows 
that they describe the languages of interest.

%<*th:KleenesTheorem>
\begin{theorem}[Kleene's theorem]\index{Kleene's theorem} 
  \label{th:KleenesTheorem}
  A language is recognized by a Finite State machine if and only if that
  language is described by a regular expression.
\end{theorem}
%</th:KleenesTheorem>

We will prove this in separate halves.
The proofs use nondeterministic machines but since we can convert
those to deterministic machines, the result holds for them also.

%<*lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine}
  If a language is described by a regular expression
  then there is a Finite State machine recognizing that language.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine>

% The proof follows the steps suggested in \zcref{ex:NFSMJoinParallel}, 
% \zcref{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}, and \zcref{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}.

\begin{proof}
  Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  We will show that for any regular expression~$R$ over $\Sigma$
  there is a machine with alphabet~$\Sigma$
  accepting exactly the strings matching the expression.
  We use induction on the structure of regular expressions.

%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachinei>
  Start with regular expressions consisting of a single character.
  If 
  $R=\re{$\emptyset$}$ then  $\lang(R)=\set{}$ and
  the machine on the left below recognizes this language.
  If $R=\re{$\emptystring$}$ then $\lang(R)=\set{\emptystring}$
  and the machine in the middle recognizes it.
  If the regular expression is a character from the alphabet
  such as $R=\re{\str{a}}$ then the machine on the right works.
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachinei>
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm32.pdf}
    \hspace{5em}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm33.pdf}
    \hspace{5em}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm34.pdf}
  \end{center}

%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiia>
  We finish by handling the three operations.
  Let $R_0$ and~$R_1$ be regular expressions. 
  The inductive hypothesis
  gives a machine $\FSM_0$ whose language is described by
  $R_0$ and a machine~$\FSM_1$ whose language is described by $R_1$.

  First consider alternation, $R=\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}$. 
  Create the machine recognizing the language described by $R$ by
  joining those two machines in parallel:~introduce
  a new state $s$ and use $\varepsilon$
  transitions to connect~$s$ to the start states of $\FSM_0$ and $\FSM_1$. 
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiia>
  See \zcref{ex:NFSMJoinParallel} in the prior section.

%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiib>
  Next consider concatenation, 
  $R=R_0\concat R_1$.
  Join the two machines serially:~for
  each accepting state in $\FSM_0$, make
  an $\varepsilon$ transition to the start state of $\FSM_1$
  and then convert all of the accepting states of $\FSM_0$ to be
  non-accepting states. 
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiib>
  See \zcref{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}.
  
%<*pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiic>
  Finally consider Kleene star, 
  $R=\re{($R_0$)*}$.
  % As in \zcref{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar},
  For each accepting state in the machine~$\FSM_0$,
  make an~$\varepsilon$ transition to
  the start state and then
  make the start state an accepting state. % even if starting state is start state
%</pf:KleenesThmRegexToMachineiic>
  See \zcref{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
Building a machine for the regular expression \re{ab(c|d)(ef)*}
starts with machines for each of the single characters. 
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm71.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm72.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}\ldots{}\hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm76.pdf}}
\end{center}
Put these atomic components together
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm77.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm78.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm81.pdf}}
\end{center}
to get the complete machine.
\begin{center}
\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm82.pdf}
\end{center}
This is a nondeterministic machine with $\varepsilon$~transitions.
To get a deterministic machine use the powerset construction.
\end{example}

%<*lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex}
  Any language recognized by a Finite State machine 
  is described by a regular expression. 
\end{lemma}
%</lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex>

Our strategy starts with a Finite State machine and eliminates its states
one at a time.
Below is an illustration, before and after pictures of part of a larger machine,
where we eliminate the state~$q$.
\begin{center}
\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm35.pdf}
\hspace{3em}
\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm36.pdf}
\end{center}
In the after picture the edge is labeled~\re{ab}, with more
than just one character.
For this proof we will generalize transition graphs to allow
edge labels that are regular expressions.
As we eliminate states, we keep the recognized language of the machines the same.
We will be done when what remains is two states with one edge
between them. 
The desired regular expression will be 
that edge's label.

Before the proof, one more illustration.
Start with the machine on the left.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm64.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm65.pdf}}
\end{center}
The proof goes as on the right,
by introducing a new start state, $e$,
and a new final state,~$f\!$.
Then the proof eliminates $q_1$ as below.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm66.pdf}}
\end{center}
Clearly this machine recognizes the same language as the
starting one.

\begin{proof}
  Call the machine~$\FSM$.
  If it has no accepting states then
  the regular expression is \re{$\emptyset$} and we are done.
  Otherwise, we start by transforming $\FSM$ to a new machine,
  $\hat{\FSM}$, that has the same language and that is set up for
  the state-elimination strategy.

  First we arrange that
  $\smash{\hat{\FSM}}$ has a single accepting state.
  Create a new state $f$ and
  for each of $\FSM$'s accepting states make an $\varepsilon$~transition to~$f$
  (by the prior paragraph there is at least one such accepting state
  so $f$ is connected to the rest of $\hat{M}$). 
  Change all the accepting states to non-accepting ones 
  and then make~$f$ accepting.
  Clearly this does not change the language of accepted strings.

  Now introduce a new start state,~$e$.
  Connect it to~$q_0$ with an $\varepsilon$~transition,
  again leaving the language of the machine unchanged.
  (Including~$e$ in $\hat{\FSM}$ allows us to uniformly eliminate 
  each state in~$\FSM$ when we say below, 
  ``Pick any~$q$ not equal to $e$ or~$f$.'')

  Because the edge labels are regular expressions, we can arrange
  that from any~$q_i$ to any~$q_j$
  there is at most one edge, since
  if $\FSM$ has more than one edge then in~$\hat{\FSM}$ 
  we can use alternation,~`\re{|}', to combine the labels.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm39.pdf}}
    \hspace{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm40.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Do the same with loops, that is, cases where $i=j$.
  These adjustments do not change the language of accepted strings.
  
  The last part of transforming to $\hat{\FSM}$ is to
  drop states that are useless in that they don't affect which strings are
  accepted.
  If a state node other than~$f$ has no outgoing edges then
  omit it, along with the edges into it.
  The language of the machine will not change because this state
  is not itself accepting as only~$f$ is accepting, and
  cannot lead to an accepting state since it doesn't lead anywhere.
  Along the same lines, 
  if a state node  is unreachable from the start~$e$ then
  drop that node along with its incoming and outgoing edges.
  (Dropping useless states has some technical aspects.
  For instance, omitting a no-outgoing-edges node along with its incoming edges
  can result in another node now having no outgoing edges, which in turn needs 
  the same treatment.
  But these machine have only finitely many nodes and so this omitting process
  must eventually finish. 
  For a definition of unreachability see \zcref{exer:UnreachableStates}.)
  
  With that, $\smash{\hat{\FSM}}$ is ready for state elimination.
  Pick any~$q$ other than $e$ or~$f\!$.
  Below are 
  \citetext{before and after diagrams}{%
    This digram is derived from \autocite{HopcroftUllman}.}.
  By the setup work above, $q$ has at least one incoming edge and at least
  one outgoing edge.
  So there are states $q_{i_0}$, \ldots{} $q_{i_j}$ with an edge leading
  into~$q$, 
  and states $q_{o_0}$, \ldots{} $q_{o_k}$ that receive an edge leading out
  of~$q$. 
  In addition, $q$ may have a loop.
  (A fine point is that 
  possibly some of the states shown on the
  left of each diagram equal some shown on the right.
  For example, possibly $q_{i_0}$ equals~$q_{o_0}$, and
  the edge on the top of each diagram is a loop.)
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm37.pdf}}
    \hspace{2.5em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm38.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Eliminate $q$ and the associated edges by making the replacements
  shown on the after diagram.
  (If an edge is not present then don't include any regular expression in
  the replacement.
  For instance, if there is no $R_{\ell}$ loop then the right's top edge should
  be \re{$R_{i_0,o_0}$|$R_{i_0}R_{o_0}$}.)
  By construction, for any two states in the after machine,
  $q_i$ and~$q_o$, in passing from the before diagram to the after, the set 
  of strings taking the machine from the first to the second is unchanged.
  Thus languages of the before and after machines are equal.
  
  Repeat this elimination procedure until the only states left are
  $e$ and~$f\!$. 
  The desired regular expression is on the sole remaining edge.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
  We want a regular expression describing the 
  language of the machine~$\FSM$ on the left below.
  Introduce $e$ and~$f$ as on the right.
  There are no useless states so this is $\hat{\FSM}$.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm48.pdf}}
    \hspace{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm49.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  We will eliminate $q_2$, then~$q_1$, then~$q_0$
  (this choice is arbitrary;~we could have instead done any other order).
  For the first, in the notation used in the proof's diagram, $q_1=q_{i_0}$
  and $q_0=q_{o_0}$.
  The regular expressions are $R_{i_0,o_0}=\re{b}$,
  $R_{i_0}=\re{a}$,
  $R_{\ell}=\re{b}$,
  and $R_{o_0}=\re{b}$.
  Elimination leaves the resulting machine with an arrow from 
  $q_{i_0}=q_1$ to~$q_{o_0}=q_0$ labeled
  \re{$R_{i_0,o_0}$|($R_{i_0}R_{\ell}$*$R_{o_0}$)},
  which is \re{b|(ab*b)}.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm50.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  
  Next,~$q_1$.
  There is one node giving an incoming arrow,
  $q_0=q_{i_0}$,
  and two nodes associated with outgoing arrows, $q_0=q_{o_0}$ and~$f=q_{o_1}$.
  (Note that $q_0$ is both an incoming and outgoing node; this is the
  fine point mentioned in the proof.)
  The regular expressions are:
  there is no arrow for $R_{i_0,o_0}$,
  $R_{i_0,o_1}=\re{\emptystring}$, 
  $R_{i_0}=\re{a}$,
  $R_{o_0}=\re{b|(ab*b)}$,
  there is also no arrow for $R_{\ell}$,  
  and $R_{o_1}=\re{$\varepsilon$}$.
  Eliminating $q_1$ means that the next machine
  has an arrow from $q_{i_0}=q_0$ to $q_{o_0}=q_0$ labeled
  \re{$R_{i_0,o_0}$|($R_{i_0}R_{\ell}$*$R_{o_0}$)},
  which is \re{a(b|ab*b)}.
  It also means that the machine
  has an arrow from $q_{i_0}=q_0$ to $q_{o_1}=q_f$ labeled
  \re{$R_{i_0,o_1}$|($R_{i_0}R_{\ell}$*$R_{o_1}$)},
  which is \re{\emptystring|(a\emptystring)}.
  
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm51.pdf}}    
  \end{center}
  
  Now for the final step.
  The sole incoming node is $e=q_{i_0}$
  and the sole outgoing node is $f=q_{o_0}$.
  As well, $R_{i_0}=\re{$\varepsilon$}$,
  $R_{o_0}=\re{$\varepsilon$|a$\varepsilon$}$,
  and
  $R_{\ell}=\re{a(b|ab*b)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm52.pdf}}    
  \end{center}
  This regular expression describes the language of the starting machine
  (we can simplify it; 
  for instance, 
  in the final parenthesis we can replace \re{a$\varepsilon$} with~\re{a}).
\end{example}
\index{Kleene's theorem|)}


% .........................
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
Decide if the string~$\sigma$ matches the regular expression~$R$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\sigma=\str{0010}$, $R=\re{0*10}$
\item $\sigma=\str{101}$, $R=\re{1*01}$
\item $\sigma=\str{101}$, $R=\re{1*(0|1)}$
\item $\sigma=\str{101}$, $R=\re{1*(0|1)*}$
\item $\sigma=\str{01}$, $R=\re{1*01*}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
Each one illustrates the match using underbars.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Yes.
    One match is 
    $\underbracket{\str{00}}_{\re{0*}}\underbracket{\str{1}}_{\re{1}}\underbracket{\str{0}}_{\re{0}}$.
  \item Yes
    This works: 
    $\underbracket{\str{1}}_{\re{1*}}\underbracket{\str{0}}_{\re{0}}\underbracket{\str{1}}_{\re{1}}$.
  \item No.
    To match a string with a \str{0}, that~\str{0} must be the final character.
  \item Yes.
    A match is 
    $\underbracket{\str{1}}_{\re{1*}}\underbracket{\str{01}}_{\re{(0|1)*}}$.
  \item Yes; this is a case where the star means `zero' in `zero or more'.
    The concatenation of the empty string \emptystring{} with the string
    \str{01} equals the given string \str{01}.
    So we can write \str{01} as $\emptystring\concat\str{01}$ and 
    recognize this match: 
    $\underbracket{\emptystring}_{\re{1*}}\underbracket{\str{0}}_{\re{0}}\underbracket{\str{1}}_{\re{1*}}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  For each regular expression produce five bitstrings that match
  and five that do not, or as many as there are if there are not five.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{01*}
  \item \re{(01)*}
  \item \re{1(0|1)1}
  \item \re{(0|1)($\emptystring$|1)0*}
  \item \re{$\emptyset$}
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
Five that match are \str{0}, \str{01}, \str{011}, \str{0111}, 
and~$\str{0}\str{1}^4=\str{01111}$.
Five that do not are \emptystring, \str{1}, \str{10}, \str{11}, 
and~\str{100}. 
\item
Five matching strings are \emptystring, \str{01}, \str{0101}, \str{010101},
and~$(\str{01})^4\!$.
Five that don't match are \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{00}, \str{10}, and~\str{11}.
\item 
There are only two 
matches, \str{101} and~\str{111}.
Five that do not match are \emptystring, \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{00}, \str{01},
and~\str{10}.
\item
Five matches are \str{0}, \str{01}, \str{00}, \str{1}, and~\str{10}.
Five that don't match are \emptystring, \str{001}, \str{0001}, \str{101},
and~\str{1101}.
\item Matches:~none.
Non-matches:~\emptystring, \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{00}, \str{01},
and~\str{10}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a brief plain English description of the language
for each regular expression.
\begin{exparts*}
\item \re{a*cb*}
\item \re{aa*}
\item \re{a(a|b)*bb}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The language consists of strings with some number of \str{a}'s 
  (that number may be zero), followed by a single~\str{c},
  followed by some number of \str{b}'s.
  \item The language consists of strings of at least one~\str{a}.
  \item Each string in this language starts with an~\str{a}, followed by
  any number of \str{a}'s and \str{b}'s, and ends with two \str{b}'s.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each string in
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$ that is of length 
  less than or equal to~$3$,
  decide if the string is a match.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{a*b}
  \item \re{a*}
  \item \re{$\emptyset$}
  \item \re{$\emptystring$}
  \item \re{b(a|b)a}
  \item \re{(a|b)($\emptystring$|a)a}
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \figureref{fig:StringsMatchRegularExprs} shows the table.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{r|*{6}{c}}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ } &\re{a*b} &\re{a*} &\re{$\emptyset$}
        &\re{$\emptystring$} &\re{b(a|b)a} &\re{(a|b)($\emptystring$|a)a}  
      \\ \cline{2-7}
      \emptystring &\false &\true &\false &\true &\false &\false  \\[1ex]
      \str{a}      &\false &\true &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{b}      &\true &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\[1ex]
      \str{aa}     &\false &\true &\false &\false &\false &\true  \\
      \str{ab}     &\true &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{ba}     &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\true  \\
      \str{bb}     &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\[1ex]
      \str{aaa}    &\false &\true &\false &\false &\false &\true  \\
      \str{aab}    &\true &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{aba}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{abb}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{baa}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\true &\true  \\
      \str{bab}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
      \str{bba}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\true &\false  \\
      \str{bbb}    &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false &\false  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{String matching test.}
    \label{fig:StringsMatchRegularExprs}
  \end{figure}
  Observe that the regular expression \re{$\emptyset$} never matches.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A friend says to you, ``The point of parentheses is that you first do inside the
parentheses and then do what's outside.
So Kleene star must mean `match the inside and repeat'.
So I think that \re{(0*1)*} should match
the strings \str{001001} and~\str{010101}, 
but not the strings \str{01001} and~\str{00000101}, 
because you can't write those two as $\sigma^n$ for any substring~$\sigma$.''
Straighten them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  First, \re{(0*1)*} matches \str{01001} because
  we can decompose it into
  $\str{01001}=\str{01}\concat\str{001}$, and both
  \str{01} and~\str{001} match \re{(0*1)}.
  Second,  \re{(0*1)*} matches \str{00000101} because
  $\str{00000101}=\str{000001}\concat\str{01}$, and both of 
  those match \re{(0*1)}.
  Thus, Kleene star means more like
  ``repeatedly match the inside.''

  In terms of the definitions, the language of the
  star is the star of the 
  language:~$\lang(\kleenestar{R})=\kleenestar{(\lang(R))}$.
  The definition of the latter is about decomposition, 
  $
  \kleenestar{\lang(R)}
    =\set{\sigma_0\concat\cdots\concat\sigma_{k-1}\suchthat \text{$k\in\N$ and $\sigma_0,\ldots,\sigma_{k-1}\in\lang(R)$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% Using regular-expressions.rkt
% (let ([s '("001001" "010101" "01001" "00000101")]
%         [r '(#rx"^(0*1)*$")])
%     (test-strings-against-regexes s r))

%  001001 #t
%  010101 #t
%  01001 #t
%  00000101 #t

\begin{exercise}
The person behind you in class says, ``I don't get it. 
I got a regular expression that I am sure is right.
But I looked in the answers and the book got a different one.''
Explain what is up.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Regular expressions are not unique.
  For instance, here are two regular expressions for the language 
  of strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ that have a substring
  with at least three \str{a}'s:~\re{(a|b)*aaa(a|b)*}
  and \re{(a|b)*aaaa*(a|b)*}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Produce a regular expression for the language of bitstrings
  that have a substring consisting of at least three consecutive \str{1}'s.
\begin{answer}\verified
  One is \re{(0|1)*111(0|1)*}.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language, give five strings that are in the language and five that 
are not.
Then give a regular expression describing the language.
Finally, give a Finite State machine that accepts the language
(a nondeterministic machine is acceptable).
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\lang_0=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^{2m}\suchthat m,n\geq 1}$
\item $\lang_1=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^{3m}\suchthat m,n\geq 1}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
For each, many sets of five strings are possible answers.
We list here one set out of many.
\begin{exparts}
\item Here are five members of~$\lang_0$: 
\str{abb}, \str{aabb}, $\str{a}\str{b}^4\!$,  $\str{aa}\str{b}^4\!$,
 $\str{a}\str{b}^6\!$.
Here are five that are not: \emptystring,
\str{a}, \str{b}, \str{ab}, \str{abba}, \str{ba}. 
A regular expression is \re{aa*bb(bb)*}.
Here is a (nondeterministic) Finite State machine that accepts 
this language.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1039.pdf}
\end{display}
\item 
Five members of~$\lang_1$ are: 
\str{abbb}, \str{aabbb}, $\str{a}\str{b}^6\!$,  $\str{aa}\str{b}^6\!$,
 $\str{a}\str{b}^9\!$.
Five that are not are: \emptystring,
\str{a}, \str{b}, \str{abbbb}, \str{abbba}, \str{ba}. 
A regular expression is \re{aa*bbb(bbb)*}.
This is a nondeterministic Finite State machine that accepts~$\lang_1$.
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1040.pdf}
\end{display}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Give a regular expression for the language over
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$
  whose strings are missing at least one letter,
  that is, whose strings are either without any \str{a}'s,
  or without any \str{b}'s, or without any \str{c}'s.
\begin{answer}\verified
\re{((a|b)*)|((a|c)*)|((b|c)*)}  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
% \begin{exercise}
%   Produce a regular expression for the language of bitstrings
%   such that the number of \str{1}'s is a multiple of four.
% \begin{answer}
%   For the number of \str{1}'s to be a multiple of four, take the
%   \str{1}'s in groups of four, as in \re{(0*10*10*10*1)*0*}.  
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}  % HMU p 88
Give a regular expression for each language.
Use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of strings starting with \str{b}. 
\item The set of strings whose second-to-last character is \str{a}.
\item The set of strings containing at least one of each character.
\item The strings where the number of \str{a}'s is divisible by three.
% \item The strings with at most one set of consecutive \str{a}'s.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{b(a|b)*}
\item \re{(a|b)*a(a|b)}
\item The two characters could come in the order 
$\sigma=\ldots{}\str{a}\ldots{}\str{b}\ldots{}$ or the order
$\sigma=\ldots{}\str{a}\ldots{}\str{b}\ldots{}$\sentencespace
So this works:
\re{(a|b)*((a(a|b)*b)|(b(a|b)*a))(a|b)*}.
\item The \str{a}'s must come in triplets: \re{(b*ab*ab*a)*b*}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression to describe each language over
the alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of strings starting with \str{aba}.
\item The set of strings ending with \str{aba}.
\item The set of strings containing the substring \str{aba}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \re{aba(a|b)*}
  \item \re{(a|b)*aba}
  \item \re{(a|b)*aba(a|b)*}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression to describe each language over $\B$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of strings of odd parity, where the number of \str{1}'s is
  odd.
\item The set of strings where no two adjacent characters are equal.
\item The set of strings representing in binary multiples of eight.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item There must be at least one~\str{1}, and more \str{1}'s must come in 
  pairs:~\re{(0*10*1)*0*10*}.
  \item The regular expression \re{(\emptystring|1)(01)*(\emptystring|0)}
  will do.
  In particular, it matches the empty string as well as the string~\str{0}
  and the string~\str{1}.
  \item A multiple of eight in binary ends in three \str{0}'s:~\re{(0|1)*000}.
  That expression matches binary numbers with leading \str{0}'s and won't match
  \str{0}.
  If you don't like that then instead use \re{(1(0|1)*000)|0}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression to describe each language over
the alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Every \str{a} is both immediately preceded and immediately followed
  by a~\str{b}.
\item Each string has at least two \str{b}'s that are not followed by an
  \str{a}.
% \item Each string has no more than one pair of consecutive \str{a}'s
%   and no more than one pair of consecutive \str{b}'s.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is an answer: \re{((ba)*bb*)*}.
  Notice that it matches the empty string, and that it matches \str{babab}
  and \str{bbbbab} and \re{b}.
  \item \re{(a|b)*((bba*bb)|(bbb))(a|b)*}
  % \item \re{}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Give a regular expression for each language of bitstrings.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item The number of \str{0}'s is even.
  \item There are more than two \str{1}'s.
  \item The number of \str{0}'s is even and 
   there are more than two \str{1}'s.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Here the \str{0}'s come in pairs:~\re{(1*01*01*)*1*}.
    Note that it matches \emptystring{} as well as~\re{1}.
  \item Here are three \str{1}'s without 
    Kleene star's: \re{0*10*10*1(0|1)*}.
  \item We can start with the expression from the prior item
  and for the \str{0}'s, substitute the expression from the first item.
  \begin{display}
    \re{((1*01*01*)*1*)*1((1*01*01*)*1*)*1((1*01*01*)*1*)*1(((1*01*01*)*1*)|1)*}
  %     (1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*(1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*(1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*((1*01*01*)*(1|010)1*)*
  \end{display}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Give a regular expression to describe each language.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
    $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
      \text{$\sigma$ ends with the same symbol that it began with, and $\sigma\neq\emptystring$}}$    
  \item
    $\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}\str{a}^j\suchthat
      \text{$i$ and $j$ leave the same remainder on division by three}}$
  \end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \re{(a(a|b)*a)|(b(a|b)*b)}
  \item \re{((aaa)*b(aaa)*)|(a(aaa)*ba(aaa)*)|(aa(aaa)*baa(aaa)*)}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regular expression describing each language over $\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The strings representing a binary number that is a multiple of two.
\item The bitstrings where the first character differs from the final
  one.
\item The bitstrings where no two adjacent characters are equal.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This requires the number to have a leading \str{1}: \re{1(0|1)*0}.
  To also allow the number $0$ we can use the expression \re{(1(0|1)*0)|0}.
\item \re{(1(01)*0)|(0(10)*1)}
\item \re{$\emptystring$|0|1|(0(10)*(1|\emptystring))|(1(01)*(0|\emptystring))}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Produce a Finite State machine whose language equals the language described
by each regular expression.
\begin{exparts*}
\item \re{a*ba}
\item \re{ab*(a|b)*a}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item A nondeterministic Finite State machine is 
on the left in \figureref{fig:FSMForStarab}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex04.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex05.pdf}}
  \caption{Finite State machine for \re{a*ba}.}
  \label{fig:FSMForStarab}
\end{figure}
If you prefer a deterministic machine, use the one on the right.
\item A nondeterministic machine is in \figureref{fig:FSMForaabStara}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex06.pdf}}
  \caption{Finite State machine for \re{ab*(a|b)*a}, which describes the same language as \re{a(a|b)*a}.}
  \label{fig:FSMForaabStara}
\end{figure}
Note that \re{b*(a|b)*} collapses to \re{(a|b)*}, so that is what the
machine handles.
We have given a way to convert a nondeterministic Finite State
machine to a deterministic one,
so if you prefer a deterministic one then take the above nondeterministic 
machine as an abbreviation. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:UnreachableStates}
Part of the proof of \zcref{lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex} involves
unreachable states.
Here is a definition.
Given a state~$q$, construct the set of states reachable 
from it by first setting $S_0=\set{q}\cup\hat{E}(q)$, 
where $\hat{E}(q)$  is the $\varepsilon$~closure.
Then iterate:~starting with the set~$S_i$ of states that are
reachable in $i$-many steps, for each $\tilde{q}\in S_i$
follow each outbound edge for a single step and also include the elements of
the $\varepsilon$~closure.
The union of~$S_i$ with the collection of the states reached in this way 
is the set~$S_{i+1}$.
Stop when $S_i=S_{i+1}$, 
at which point it is the set of ever-reachable states.
The unreachable states are the others.
For each machine, use that definition to find the set of unreachable states.
\vspace{0.5ex}\par\noindent % little vertical room for graphic
\begin{exparts*}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm60.pdf}}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm61.pdf}}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  We will construct the reachable states, and then the unreachable ones are
  the others.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The set of reachable states is $S_2=S_3=\cdots=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$.
  So the set of unreachable states is $\set{q_3}$.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|c}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$i$}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$S_i$}} 
    \\ \hline 
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  \\
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  \item The set of reachable states is $S_2=S_3=\cdots=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$.
  Thus the set of unreachable states is $\set{q_2,q_4}$.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|c}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$i$}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$S_i$}} 
    \\ \hline 
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$  \\
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \recommended
\begin{exercise}
This grammar for regular expressions reflects the operator
precedence rules. 
% alternative to \zcref{def:RESyntax}.

\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{reg-exp} \produces{} \ntrm{concat} \syntaxor \ntrm{reg-exp} `\trm{|}' \ntrm{concat} 

\ntrm{concat} \produces{}  \ntrm{simple} \syntaxor \ntrm{concat} \ntrm{simple}

\ntrm{simple} \produces{} \trm{(} \ntrm{reg-exp} \trm{)} \syntaxor \ntrm{simple} \trm{*} \syntaxor $\emptyset$ \syntaxor \emptystring \syntaxor  $x_0$ \syntaxor \ldots{} \syntaxor $x_n$
\end{grammar}
  Derive and construct the parse tree for each regular expressions over
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}, \str{c}}$.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item \re{a(b|c)}
  \item \re{ab*(a|c)}  
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  \figureref{fig:ParseRegularExprsI} has the derivation and the parse tree.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{minipage}{0.5\linewidth}
  \begin{tabular}{r@{}l}
  \synt{reg-exp} &\derives \synt{concat}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \synt{simple}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{reg-exp} \str{)}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{reg-exp} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \synt{simple} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{(} \synt{simple} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{(} \str{b} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{(} \str{b} `\str{|}' \synt{simple} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{(} \str{b} `\str{|}' \str{c} \str{)}
  \end{tabular}      
    \end{minipage}
    \hspace{2em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex03.pdf}}
    \caption{Derivation and parse tree for \re{a(b|c)}.}
    \label{fig:ParseRegularExprsI}
  \end{figure}
\item 
  \figureref{fig:ParseRegularExprsII} shows the derivation and the parse tree.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{minipage}{0.5\linewidth}
  \begin{tabular}{r@{}l}
  \synt{reg-exp} &\derives \synt{concat}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \synt{simple}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{reg-exp} \str{)}  \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{reg-exp} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \synt{concat} \synt{simple} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \synt{simple} \synt{simple} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \synt{simple} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \synt{simple} \str{*} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{b} \str{*} \str{(} \synt{concat} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{b} \str{*} \str{(} \synt{simple} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{b} \str{*} \str{(} \str{a} `\str{|}' \synt{concat} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{b} \str{*} \str{(} \str{a} `\str{|}' \synt{simple} \str{)} \\
    &\derives \str{a} \str{b} \str{*} \str{(} \str{a} `\str{|}' \str{c} \str{)} 
  \end{tabular}
    \end{minipage}
    \hspace{2em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex02.pdf}}
    % \hspace{3em}
    % \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/regex/regex02.pdf}}
    \caption{Derivation and parse tree for \re{ab*(a|c)}.}
    \label{fig:ParseRegularExprsII}
  \end{figure}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\autocite{GATE21}
Let $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$ be recognized by a deterministic 
Finite State machine having exactly $k$ states.
One of these must also be accepted by such a machine with $k$ states, while for
the other that is not necessarily right.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts*}
\item The complement, $\setcomp{\lang}$
\item $\lang\cup\set{\str{01}}$  
% \item $\lang^k$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
The first is recognized by the same $k$-state machine, but with the accepting 
states made nonaccepting and with the nonaccepting made accepting.
The second will not be recognized by a $k$-state machine 
in the case that $\lang=\emptyset$ and $k=1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Fix a Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
  Kleene's Theorem shows that the set of strings taking $\FSM$ from the
  start state~$q_0$ to the set of final states is regular.
  \begin{exparts}
    \item
      Show that for any set of states $S\subseteq Q_{\FSM}$, final or not, 
      the set of
      strings taking $\FSM$ from~$q_0$ to one of the states in~$S$
      is regular.
    \item
      Show that the set of strings taking $\FSM$ from any single
      state to any other single state is regular.
  \end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We can leverage Kleene's theorem for these.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Transform the machine~$\FSM$ to a new machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by making
  the set of final states of~$\hat{\FSM}$ be~$S$.
  \item Transform the machine~$M$ to~$\hat{\FSM}$ by making
  the start state be the given first single state, and also 
  make the set~$S$ of the prior item contain only the ending state.
  Then apply the argument given for the prior item. 
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix an alphabet $\Sigma$.
Show that the set of languages over $\Sigma$ that are
described by a regular expression is countably infinite.
Conclude that there are languages over $\Sigma$
not recognized by any Finite State machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We will show this in two halves:~that the set of languages is infinite, 
  and that it is at most countable.
  Recall that, as defined in \zcref{sec:Strings},
  alphabets are nonempty and finite.

  We first show that the number of languages is infinite. 
  Let $x$ be an element of~$\Sigma$.
  The one-element language $\lang_1=\set{x}$ is described by the
  one-character regular expression $R_1=x$. 
  The one-element language $\lang_2=\set{xx}$ is described by the
  two-character regular expression $R_2=xx$.
  In general, $\lang_k$ is a one-element language that is different from
  all $\lang_{j}$ for $j\neq k$, and is described by a regular expression
  $R_k$ with $k$~characters. 
  That makes infinitely many different languages described by 
  regular expressions over~$\Sigma$.

  We finish by showing that the set of languages is countable.
  Every regular expression is associated with only one language and so
  if we show that there are countably
  many regular expressions then we will be done.
  A regular expression is a string over
  $\Sigma\cup\set{\str{)},\str{(},\str{|},\str{*}}$,
  which is a finite set.
  So for any number~$n\in\N$ there are finitely many regular expressions
  of length~$n$.
  Recall Chapter~II's \zcref{th:UnionFiniteNumberCtbleSets}, which
  shows that the countable union of countable sets is countable.
  Because a finite set is countable, the union over all~$n$ of the length~$n$
  regular expressions is therefore countable.

We finish by showing that the set of all languages over~$\Sigma$,
the set of all~$\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, is uncountable.
First, $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is countably infinite
because there are countably many $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ of length
zero, of length one, etc.. 
The set of all languages~$\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is the power set 
of~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and so has cardinality greater than the
cardinality of~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, which makes that set uncountable.
  So there are languages that
  are not described by any regular expression.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  An alternative proof of
  \zcref{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine},
  the \definend{subset method},\index{subset method}\index{Finite State machine!subset method} 
  goes from a given regular expression to an associated machine by
  reversing the steps of \zcref{lem:KleenesThmMachineToRegex}.
  Start by labeling the single edge on a two-state machine with

  \begin{center}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/30726}
\end{credit}
Nondeterministic Finite State machines can always be made to have a single
accepting state.
For deterministic machines that is not so.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that any deterministic Finite State machine that recognizes the
  finite language
  $\lang_1=\set{\emptystring,\str{a}}$ must have at least two accepting states.
\item Show that any deterministic Finite State machine that recognizes 
  $\lang_2=\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\str{aa}}$ must have at least three
  accepting states.
\item Show that for any $n\in\N$ there is a regular language
  that is not recognized by any deterministic Finite State machine
  with at most~$n$ accepting states.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  Because it accepts the input~\emptystring{} the initial state~$q_0$ must
  be an accepting state.
  If~$q_0$ were the only accepting state then 
  this machine accepts \str{a} by virtue of a loop from~$q_0$ to itself,
  which would make the machine accept~\str{aa}.
  That is not in the language~$\lang_1$, so there must be at least one
  other accepting state.
  \item 
  Because it accepts the input~\emptystring{} the initial state~$q_0$ must
  be an accepting state.
  If~$q_0$ were the only accepting state then 
  this machine accepts \str{a} by virtue of a loop from~$q_0$ to itself,
  which would make the machine accept~\str{aaa}.
  That string is not a member of the language~$\lang_2$ so there must be at 
  least one accepting state other than~$q_0$.

  If the machine accepts \str{aa} by being in state~$q_0$ then that involves
  a loop, so the string \str{aaaa} would also be accepted by this machine.
  But that string is not in the language~$\lang_2$, so the 
  extended transition function on this input, $\hat{E}(\str{aa})$, 
  is not equal to the state~$q_0$.
  Similarly, if $\hat{E}(\str{aa})$ were equal to $\hat{E}(\str{a})$ then
  there would be a loop from this state to itself on input~\str{a}, and
  so the machine would also accept \str{aaa}.
  But the machine does not accept \str{aaa} because that string is not in the
  language~$\lang_2$, so there must be a third accepting state. 
  \item This is the natural extension of the prior two items.
  Consider $\lang_n=\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\ldots,\str{a}^n}$.
  If $\hat{E}(\str{a}^n)$ were equal to $\hat{E}(\str{a}^i)$ for any $i<n$
  then there would be a loop and so the machine would accept additional
  strings. 
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{regular expression|)}




% =================================================
\section{Regular languages}\index{regular language|(}\index{language!regular|(}


% We have seen that
% deterministic Finite State machines, nondeterministic Finite State
% machines, and regular expressions
% all describe the same set of languages.
% \citetext{The fact that we can describe this set in many 
%   different ways}{\autocite{SE:noperm}.} 
% argues that there is something natural and important about it.\footnote{%
%   This is just like how the fact that Turing machines,
%   general recursive functions, and many other models all compute
%   the same sets says that these
%   computable sets are a natural and important collection.
%   This collection 
%   is not just a historical artifact of what happened to be first
%   studied.}

%...................................
\subsection{Definition}
We have seen that
deterministic Finite State machines, nondeterministic Finite State
machines, and regular expressions
all describe the same set of languages.
\citetext{The fact that we can describe this set in many 
  different ways}{\autocite{SE:noperm}.} 
argues that there is something natural and important about it.\footnote{%
  This is just like how the fact that Turing machines,
  general recursive functions, and many other models all compute
  the same sets says that these
  computable sets are a natural and important collection.
  This collection 
  is not just a historical artifact of what happened to be first
  studied.}

%<*def:RegularLangs>
\begin{definition}
  A \definend{regular language}\index{regular language}\index{language!regular} 
  is one that is 
  recognized by some Finite State machine or, equivalently,
  described by a regular expression.
\end{definition}
%</def:RegularLangs>

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:SetRegLangsIsCountable}
Fix an alphabet.
The set of regular languages over it is countably infinite.
% The collection of all languages over that alphabet is uncountable, 
% and consequently 
There
are languages over that alphabet that are not regular.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\zcref{sec:Strings} specifies that 
any alphabet~$\Sigma$ is nonempty. % and finite.
So we can take some $x\in\Sigma$ and then
we have the string $x$, the string $xx$, etc.
Thus the collection of strings $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is infinite.
% Because $\Sigma$ is finite there are finitely many strings of length~$0$,
% finitely many of length~$1$, etc.
% Thus $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is the
% union of these countably many finite sets, and
% therefore countable.

The collection of regular languages over~$\Sigma$ is 
infinite because 
each of these languages is finite and therefore
regular:~$\lang_0=\set{}$, $\lang_1=\set{x}$, $\lang_2=\set{xx}$,
\ldots\sentencespace
The collection of regular languages over $\Sigma$ is 
countable because 
there is one language for each regular expression.
There are finitely many regular expressions of length~$1$, 
finitely many of length~$2$, etc., and 
the union of them all is a countable union of countable sets. 

We finish by showing that the set of all languages over $\Sigma$
is uncountable, from which it follows that
there are languages that are not regular.
The first paragraph shows that the collection of strings $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
is infinite. % countably infinite. 
The collection of sets of strings\Dash that is, the collection of
all languages $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}$\!\Dash is the power set 
of~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and therefore has a greater cardinality, 
which makes it uncountable.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Closure properties}
In proving the first half of Kleene's Theorem, 
\zcref{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine},
we showed that if $\lang_0$ and $\lang_1$
are regular then their union $\lang_0\cup\lang_1$ is 
regular,\footnotetext{%
  If the two languages have different
  alphabets $\Sigma_0$ and~$\Sigma_1$ then
  the two languages as well as their union
  are regular over the alphabet $\Sigma_0\cup\Sigma_1$.} 
as is their concatenation $\lang_0\concat\lang_1$,
and the Kleene star $\kleenestar{\lang_0\!}\!$.
A set is
\definend{closed} under an operation\index{closure under an operation}
if
\citetext{performing that operation on its members always yields another member}{%
  Familiar examples are that adding two integers always gives an integer 
  so the integers are closed under the operation of addition, and 
  that squaring an integer always results in an integer
  so that the integers are closed under squaring.}.\index{closure under an operation}
This restates \zcref{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine} using that term.

%<*lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>
\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under the 
  union of two languages, 
  the concatenation of two languages,
  and the Kleene star of a language.
\end{lemma}%</lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>

We will ask about the closure of regular languages under other operations.
To answer we will use the 
\definend{product construction}.\index{product construction}\index{Finite State machine!product construction}

\begin{example} \label{ex:ProductConstruction}
%<*ex:RegLangsProductConsti>
The machine on the left, $\FSM_0$,  
accepts strings with fewer than two~\str{a}'s.
The one on the right, $\FSM_1$, 
accepts strings with an odd number of~\str{b}'s.
%</ex:RegLangsProductConsti>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm91.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm92.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*ex:RegLangsProductConstii>
The transition tables contain the same information as the pictures.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_0$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  \acceptingstate{} $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $q_1$  &$q_2$  &$q_1$  \\ 
  $q_2$  &$q_2$  &$q_2$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_1$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $s_0$  &$s_0$  &$s_1$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $s_1$  &$s_1$  &$s_0$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
%</ex:RegLangsProductConstii>
%<*ex:RegLangsProductConstiii>
The \definend{product}\index{Finite State machine!product} 
machine~$\FSM$ has states that are the members of the cross product
$Q_0\times Q_1$ 
% \begin{equation*}
%   Q_0\times Q_1
%     =\set{ (q_0,s_0),\, (q_0,s_1),\, (q_1,s_0),\, (q_1,s_1),\, (q_2,s_0),\, (q_2,s_1) }
% \end{equation*}
and transitions that are given by 
$\Delta\bigl(\,(q_i,s_j)\,\bigr)=\bigl(\Delta_0(q_i),\,\Delta_1(s_j)\bigr)$.
Its start state is $(q_0,s_0)$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $(q_0,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_0,s_1)$  \rule{0em}{2ex} \\
  $(q_0,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

As an example, with \str{aba} on the tape, $\FSM$'s states go from 
$(q_0,s_0)$ to $(q_1,s_0)$, to $(q_1,s_1)$, and then to $(q_2,s_1)$.
This is simply because $\FSM_0$ passes from
$q_0$ to~$q_1$, to~$q_1$ again, and to~$q_2$, 
while $\FSM_1$ does
$s_0$ to~$s_0$, to~$s_1$, and to~$s_1$.
In short, the product machine $\FSM$ runs
$\FSM_0$ and $\FSM_1$ in parallel.
%</ex:RegLangsProductConstiii>

%<*ex:RegLangsProductConstiv>
We have not yet fully specified the machine
because we have not said which states are accepting.
On the left below, 
$(q_i,s_j)$ is accepting if both $q_i$ and $s_j$ are accepting.
With this, $\FSM_{\ell}$ accepts a string if and only if both
$\FSM_0$ and~$\FSM_1$ accept it,
and thus recognizes
$\set{\sigma\in\Sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has fewer than two \str{a}'s and an odd number of \str{b}'s}}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\FSM_{\ell}$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $(q_0,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_0,s_1)$  \rule{0em}{2ex} \\
  \acceptingstate{} $(q_0,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  \\
  \acceptingstate{} $(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  \\
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\FSM_r$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  \acceptingstate{} $(q_0,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_0,s_1)$  \rule{0em}{2ex} \\
  $(q_0,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
  \acceptingstate{} $(q_1,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_1,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_1)$  &$(q_2,s_0)$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
On the right, accepting states $(q_i,s_j)$ 
are the ones where $q_i$ is accepting and $s_j$ is not.
This machine accepts strings that are in the language of
$\FSM_0$ but not that of $\FSM_1$,
so $\FSM_r$ recognizes
$\set{\sigma\in\Sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has fewer than two \str{a}'s and an even number of \str{b}'s}}$.
%</ex:RegLangsProductConstiv>
\end{example}

%<*th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{theorem}  \label{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under
  the intersection of two languages, the set difference of two languages, 
  and the set complement of a language. 
\end{theorem}
%</th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

%<*pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{proof}
% This proof is by construction.
Consider
languages $\lang_0$ and~$\lang_1$ over an alphabet~$\Sigma$.
Let them be recognized by the Finite State machines $\FSM_0$ and~$\FSM_1$.
Perform the product construction to get~$\FSM$.

If the accepting states of~$\FSM$
are those pairs where both the first component state is accepting
in $\FSM_0$ and the second component state is accepting in $\FSM_1$
then $\FSM$ recognizes the 
intersection of the languages, $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$. 
If the accepting states of~$\FSM$
are those pairs where the first component state is accepting in $\FSM_0$
but the second is not accepting in $\FSM_1$,
then $\FSM$ recognizes the 
set difference of the languages, $\lang_0-\lang_1$. 
A special case of this is when $\lang_0$ is the set of all strings, 
$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, so that
$\FSM$ recognizes the complement,~$\setcomp{\lang_1}\!$.
\end{proof}
%</pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

These closure properties often simplify 
showing that a language is regular.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:RegLangEvenZerosMoreThanFourOnes}
  To show that
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
               \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s 
                 and more than two \str{1}'s}}
  \end{equation*}
  is regular
  we could produce a machine that recognizes it,
  or exhibit a regular expression.
  Instead, note that~$\lang$ is the intersection
  of
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s}}$
  and
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has more than two \str{1}'s}}$.
  % \begin{equation*}
  %   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s}}
  %   \cap
  %   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has more than two \str{1}'s}}
  % \end{equation*}
  Showing that those two are regular by 
  producing machines or regular expressions is easy.
\end{example}

% -----------------------------------------------------------
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
    \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/155353/50343}
\end{credit}
Someone in class says,
``We know if $\lang_0$ and $\lang_1$ are regular then their 
intersection $\lang_0\intersection\lang_1$ is also regular.
But when $\lang_0$ and $\lang_1$ are not regular 
then $\lang_0\intersection\lang_1=\lang_2$ 
doesn't make $\lang_2$ not regular, why?''   
Explain it to them.
\begin{answer}\verified
Closure of regular languages under intersection does
not imply closure of non-regular languages under intersection.
Here is an example.
\zcref{lem:SetRegLangsIsCountable} says that 
 over $\Sigma_0=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ there is a language
$\lang_0$ that is not regular.
Similarly over $\Sigma_1=\set{\str{c},\str{d}}$ there is a language
$\lang_2$ that is not regular. 
Their intersection is empty because the alphabets do not 
share characters, and the empty language is regular
(we can take it to be a language over $\Sigma_0\union\Sigma_1$). 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is English a regular language?
\begin{answer}\verified
  This is not a well-defined question.

  In principle a person could take $\Sigma$ to be the words in some
  fixed dictionary,
  and we could assert that no English sentence has more than a million words 
  (see \autocite{wiki:LongestSentence}).
  Then we might claim that
  English is a regular language because we can list the sentences that 
  we declare allowed,
  and there are finitely many of them.
  However, that seems unsatisfactory.

  Otherwise we must choose a grammar.
  But 
  natural languages are not sharply defined:~is
  ``My god, it is her.'' valid even though some would disallow it, 
  sying that instead it should be, ``My god, it is she.''?
  (See also \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/116127/50343}.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name a class of languages that are closed under intersection and union
but not under complement. 
\begin{answer}\verified
The finite languages over some fixed alphabet.
  
For another answer, 
we can say that a language~$\lang$ 
is computably enumerable if there is a Turing machine
$\TM$ so that if $\sigma\in\lang$ then when $\TM$ is started with 
the tape consisting of $\sigma$ then it halts, 
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then it does not halt.
It is easy to see that
the set of computably enumerable languages is closed under intersection
and union but not under complementation.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Justify each answer.
\begin{exparts}
\item The empty language is not regular.
\item The intersection of two languages is regular.
\item The language of all bitstrings, 
  $\kleenestar{\B}\!$, is not regular.
\item In every infinite regular language there are two strings
  where no character from the alphabet is in the same place in both.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item False, the empty language is recognized by any Finite State machine
  that has no accepting states.
  It is also described by the regular expression~\re{$\emptyset$}.
  \item False.
    What would be correct is that 
    the intersection of two regular languages is regular.
    For example, where $\lang$ is not regular, the intersection
    $\lang\cap\kleenestar{\B}=\lang$ is not regular.
  \item False. 
  The language~$\kleenestar{\B}$ is the set of all strings recognized by a 
  Finite State machine whose states are all accepting states.
  It is also the language described by the regular 
  expression~\re{(0|1)*}.
  \item False.
    The infinite language
    $\set{\str{ab}, \str{abb}, \ldots}$
    is described by the regular expression \re{ab*}, and every
    pair of strings have \str{a} in the first position.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these is true and one is false.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts*}
\item Any finite language is regular.
\item Any regular language is finite.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first is true and the second is false.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  If the language consists of the strings $\sigma_0$, \ldots $\sigma_{n-1}$
  then a regular expression is
  \re{$\sigma_0$|$\cdots\,$|$\sigma_{n-1}$}.
  If the language is empty then the regular expression is \re{$\emptyset$}.
  \item
  The language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ described by the
  regular expression \re{a*} is
  $\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\str{aa},\ldots}$, and is infinite.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in binary a power of $2$}}$ a regular language?
\begin{answer}\verified
Yes. 
In binary, powers of~$2$ are represented by strings of bits starting 
with~\str{1} and ending in~\str{0}'s.
So the regular expression \re{10*} describes the language.
(If you want to allow leading \str{0}'s then the regular expression is 
\re{0*10*}.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$ is regular.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ starts and ends with $\str{a}$}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{the number of $\str{a}$'s is even}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  One way to show this is to give a regular expression for each.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \re{a(a|b)*a}
  \item \re{b*(ab*a)*b*}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% True or false:~all finite languages are regular, 
% and there are countably many finite languages, 
% and there are countably many regular sets,
% so therefore all regular languages are finite.
% \begin{answer}
% The statement is false.
% There are three premisses, that all finite languages are regular, 
% that there are countably many finite languages, 
% and that there are countably many regular sets.
% All three are true.
% However, the conculsion that all regular languages are finite does not
% follow.
% In addition, it is false:~the language 
% $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$ described by the regular expression
% \re{0*} is regular and infinite.  
% \end{answer}
