% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is the set of strings $\str{9}^n$ that occur
in the decimal expansion of~$\pi$ a regular language?
\begin{answer}\verified
Either that set is
$S_0=\set{\str{9}^n\suchthat n<N}$ for some $N\in\N$, 
or it is $S_1=\set{\str{9}^n\suchthat n\in \N}$.
Both are regular.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
    Suppose that the language $\lang$ over $\B$ is regular.
    Show that the language
    $\hat{\lang}=\set{1\concat\sigma\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$,
    also over~$\B$, is also regular.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Because the language is regular it is described by a regular expression, $R$.
  Then the regular expression \re{1$R$} describes $\hat{\lang}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
If two machines have $n_0$~states and $n_1$~states, how many states 
does their product have?
\begin{answer}\verified
The size of the set $Q_0\times Q_1$ is $n_0\cdot n_1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For these two,
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1041.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1042.pdf}}
\end{center}
give the transition table for the product machine.
Specify the accepting states so that the result will accept
\begin{exparts*}
\item
the intersection of the languages of the two machines, and
\item
the union of the languages.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
These are the tables for the machines.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_0$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  \acceptingstate{} $q_0$  &$q_0$  &$q_1$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $q_1$  &$q_2$  &$q_1$  \\ 
                    $q_2$  &$q_2$  &$q_0$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_1$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
                    $s_0$  &$s_1$  &$s_0$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $s_1$  &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Here is the cross product machine, without specifying accepting states.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
  \\  \cline{2-3}
  $(q_0,s_0)$ &$(q_0,s_1)$   &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_0,s_1)$ &$(q_0,s_0)$   &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_0)$ &$(q_2,s_1)$   &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_1)$ &$(q_2,s_0)$   &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_0)$ &$(q_2,s_1)$   &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_1)$ &$(q_2,s_0)$   &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
To have this machine accept the intersection of the two
languages, take 
$F=\set{(q_0,s_1),(q_1,s_1)}$,
so that $(q_i,s_j)$ is accepting if and only if both
$q_i$ and $s_j$ are accepting.
\item
Take $(q_i,s_j)$ to be accepting if and only if either
$q_i$ or~$s_j$ is accepting, so 
$F=\set{(q_0,s_0),(q_0,s_1),(q_1,s_0),(q_1,s_1)}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find the cross product of this machine,
$\FSM$ from \zcref{ex:ProductConstruction},
with itself.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm92.pdf}}
\end{center}
Set the accepting states so that it accepts the same language as
$\FSM$.
\begin{answer}\verified
This is the transition table for~$\FSM$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_0$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $s_0$  &$s_0$  &$s_1$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $s_1$  &$s_1$  &$s_0$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The product of this machine with itself has four states.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_1$}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
  \\ \cline{2-3}
  $(s_0,s_0)$  &$(s_0,s_0)$  &$(s_1,s_1)$  \\ 
  $(s_0,s_1)$  &$(s_0,s_1)$  &$(s_1,s_0)$  \\ 
  $(s_1,s_0)$  &$(s_1,s_0)$  &$(s_0,s_1)$  \\ 
  \acceptingstate{} $(s_1,s_1)$  &$(s_1,s_1)$  &$(s_0,s_0)$   
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
As shown, the set of accepting states is defined as for the 
intersection, to $F_{\intersection}=\set{(s_1,s_1)}$.
We could have instead used the set 
for union, $F_{\union}=\set{(s_1,s_1),(s_0,s_1),(s_1,s_0)}$ 
(although the two additional states are unreachable).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of our first examples of Finite State machines, \zcref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs},
accepts a string when it contains at least two \str{0}'s as well as an
even number of \str{1}'s.
Make such a machine as a product of two simpler machines.
\begin{answer}\verified
The machine on the left below accepts a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ if
it contains at least two \str{0}'s.
The one on the right accepts a string if it contains an even number of 
\str{1}'s.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_0$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}
  \\  \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$  \\
  $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$  \\
  \acceptingstate{} $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_2$  \\
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta_1$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}
  \\  \cline{2-3}
  \acceptingstate{} $s_0$ &$s_0$ &$s_1$  \\
  $s_1$ &$s_1$ &$s_0$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The product construction gives this.
The set of accepting states is arranged for 
the intersection of the two languages.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|*{2}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}
  \\  \cline{2-3}
  $(q_0,s_0)$ &$(q_1,s_0)$ &$(q_0,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_0,s_1)$ &$(q_1,s_1)$ &$(q_0,s_0)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_0)$ &$(q_2,s_0)$ &$(q_1,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_1,s_1)$ &$(q_2,s_1)$ &$(q_1,s_0)$  \\
  \acceptingstate{}  $(q_2,s_0)$ &$(q_2,s_0)$ &$(q_2,s_1)$  \\
  $(q_2,s_1)$ &$(q_2,s_1)$ &$(q_2,s_0)$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, decide whether it is true or false. 
Briefly justify.
\begin{exparts}
\item Every language is the subset of a regular language.
\item Every language has a subset that is not regular.
\item The union of a regular language and a language that is not regular
 must be not regular.
\item The union of two regular languages is regular, without
  exception.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
True.
Every language is a subset of the language $\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
\item
False.
For any alphabet, 
a language with one element is regular and its only subset is the 
empty language, which is also regular.
\item 
False.
Take $\lang_0$ to be not regular and take $\lang_1$ to be the empty
language, $\lang_1=\set{}$, which is regular.
Their union is $\lang_0$.
\item
True.
This is part of \zcref{lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Choose the right letter to fill in the blank 
(with justification):~the 
concatenation of a regular language with a language that is not regular
\fillinblankLabelled{} regular.
\begin{exparts*}
\item must be
\item might be, or might be not
\item cannot be
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
The answer is (B):~it might be regular, or might be not regular.
Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$
and a language $\lang$ over $\Sigma$ that is not regular.
A concatenation of a regular language with a non-regular one 
that gives a regular outcome is 
$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\concat\lang=\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
A language concatenation that gives an outcome that is not regular is
$\set{s}\concat\lang$ where $s\in\Sigma$
(an easy argument gives that regularity of this language 
implies regularity of $\lang$).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Briefly justify.
\begin{exparts}
\item Regularity is inherited by subsets:~if $\lang_0$ is a regular language 
and $\lang_1\subseteq \lang_0$ then $\lang_1$ is also regular.
\item Non-regularity is inherited by supersets:~if $\lang_1$ is not regular 
and $\lang_1\subseteq \lang_0$ then $\lang_0$ is also not regular.
% \item If $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$ is regular then each of the two is
%   regular.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  Both are false.
  The same example suffices for both.
  For $\lang_0$ use the set of all bitstrings, $\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
  For $\lang_1$ use any non-regular subset of it,
  the existence of which is guaranteed by \zcref{lem:SetRegLangsIsCountable}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Where $\lang$ is a language, define $\lang+$\index{+@$+$!operation on a language}\index{language!+@$+$ operation}
as the language
$\lang\concat\kleenestar{\lang}\!$.
Show that if $\lang$ is regular then so is $\lang+$.
\begin{answer}\verified
By \zcref{lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar}, regular languages
are closed under Kleene star and concatenation.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Use closure properties in showing that if $\lang$ is regular then the
set of even-length strings in~$\lang$ is also regular.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Let $\Sigma$ be the alphabet of~$\lang$.
  The collection of even length strings over~$\Sigma$ is regular since there is
  clearly a Finite State machine that accepts only those strings
  (or, a regular expression is \re{$((s_0s_0$)|($s_0s_1$)|$\ldots$\, ($s_ns_n$))*}
  where $\Sigma=\set{s_0,\ldots\,s_n}$).
  The intersection of~$\lang$ with this collection is an intersection of
  two regular languages, so it is regular. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:RegLangEvenZerosMoreThanFourOnes} shows that closure properties
can make easier some arguments that a language is regular.
It can do the same for arguments that a language is not regular.
The next section's \zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} shows that 
$
  \set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
             \suchthat n\in\N}
$
is not regular.
Show that 
$
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
        \text{$\sigma$ contains the same number of \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}
$
is not regular using closure properties.
\hint~one way is to use closure under intersection.
\begin{answer}\verified
Let the set given as not regular,
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
             \suchthat n\in\N}$,
be $\lang_0$.
Let the language described by the regular expression \re{a*b*} be~$\lang_1$.
And, let the language
$
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
        \text{$\sigma$ contains the same number of \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}
$
be~$\lang_2$.
If $\lang_2$ were regular then because the intersection of regular languages
is a regular language, $\lang_2\cap\lang_1$ would be regular.
But that set is $\lang_0$, which is not regular. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\zcref{lem:SetRegLangsIsCountable} gives a counting argument, a pure existence
proof, that
there are languages that are not regular.
But we can also exhibit such a language.
Prove that $\lang=\set{\str{1}^k\suchthat k\in K}$ is not regular,
where $K$ is the \HP{} set, 
$K=\set{e\in\N\suchthat\TMfcn_e(e)\converges}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
If there was a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ that accepted this language then 
we could solve the \HP{} with a Turing machine.
For, we can obviously write write the finite state machine as a subroutine.
Then, given $e\in\N$, feed $\str{1}^e$ to the subroutine.
If it accepts, then $e\in K$ and otherwise $e\notin K$.    
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user jmite, \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/67317/50343}.
\end{credit}
Show each.
\begin{exparts}
\item The collection of regular languages is not closed under the union
of infinitely many sets.
\item Nor is it closed under the intersection of infinitely many sets.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item For every word~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, the one-word language
$\lang=\set{\sigma}$ is regular.
But any non-regular language is the union of a collection of such languages.
\item
Similarly, 
for every word~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, the language
$\lang=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-\set{\sigma}$ is regular.
Any non-regular language~$\lang$ 
is the intersection of a collection of such languages,
namely the collection over all $\sigma\notin\lang$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\zcref{{lem:SetRegLangsIsCountable}} shows that the collection of 
regular languages over $\B$ is countable.
Show that not every individual language in that collection is countable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The language of all strings $\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$
  is regular and is uncountable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rich}
\end{credit}
An alternative definition of a regular language is as
one generated by a
\definend{regular grammar},\index{regular grammar}\index{grammar!regular}
where rewrite rules have three forms: 
\mbox{X\produces{}\trm{t}Y}, or \mbox{X\produces{}\trm{t}}, 
or \mbox{X\produces\emptystring}.
That is, the rule head has one nonterminal and the rule body 
either has a terminal followed by a nonterminal, or a single nonterminal, 
or is the empty string. 
This is an example, with the language that it generates.
\begin{center}
  \begin{minipage}{1.5in}
  \begin{grammar}
  S \produces{} \trm{a}S \syntaxor \trm{b}S \syntaxor\trm{a}A 

  A \produces{} \trm{a}B

  B \produces{} \emptystring \syntaxor \trm{b}
  \end{grammar}
  \end{minipage} % \rule{1pt}{25pt}
  \hspace{1em}
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma=\tau\concat\str{aa}$ or $\sigma=\tau\concat\str{aab}$}}$
\end{center}
Here we outline an algorithm that inputs a regular grammar and produces a 
Finite State machine that recognizes the same language.
Apply these steps to the above grammar.
\begin{exparts*}
\item For each nonterminal~X make a machine state~$q_{X}$, where 
the start state is the one for the start symbol.
\item 
For each \mbox{X\produces\emptystring} rule make state~$q_{X}$ 
accepting.
\item
For each \mbox{X\produces{}\trm{t}Y} rule put a transition 
from~$q_{X}$ to~$q_{Y}$ labeled~\trm{t}. 
\item
If there are any \mbox{X\produces{}\trm{t}} rules then make
an accepting state~$\hat{q}$, and for each such rule put a transition
from~$q_{X}$ to~$\hat{q}$ labeled~\trm{t}.
% \item  % Needed?
% If there are any state, input pairs not yet specified in the machine
% then make a non-accepting state~$\hat{q}$ and from each such state
% make a transition to~$\hat{q}$ labelled with that input. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  The result is a nondeterministic Finite State machine.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1035.pdf}}
  \item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1036.pdf}}
  \item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1037.pdf}}
  \item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1038.pdf}}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

 
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rich}, \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/1102627}
\end{credit}
    Prove that the collection of regular languages over $\Sigma$
    is closed under each of the operations.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)$ contains those strings that are a 
    prefix of at least one element~$\alpha\in\lang$, that is, 
    $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\sigma\concat\tau=\alpha\in\lang$}}$ 
    \item $\operatorname{suff}(\lang)$ contains the strings that are a suffix
    of some string in $\lang$, that is,
    $\operatorname{suff}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\tau\concat\sigma\in\lang$}}$ 
    \item $\operatorname{allprefs}(\lang)$ contains the strings~$\sigma$ 
    such that every prefix of~$\sigma$ is in $\lang$, so
    $\operatorname{allprefs}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\lang\suchthat \text{every $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ that is a prefix of~$\sigma$ has $\tau\in\lang$}}$ 
    \end{exparts}    
\begin{answer}\verified
  To show closure 
  we must show, for instance, that if $\lang$ is regular then
  $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)$ is also regular.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Fix a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ that recognizes~$\lang$.
  Convert to a new machine $\hat{\FSM}$ with the same states and 
  transitions, but where there are more accepting states.
  Specifically, find all of the states~$q\in\FSM$ such that 
  from $q$ we can reach an accepting state of~$\FSM$.
  (That is, there is a sequence of character transitions taking $\FSM$
  from $q$ to some accepting state of~$\FSM$.)
  In $\hat{\FSM}$, make every such~$q$ an accepting state.

  This $\hat{\FSM}$ recognizes $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)$.
  That's because $\sigma$ is a prefix of some $\alpha\in\lang$
  if and only if it takes the machine~$\FSM$ from its initial state $q_0$
  to some state~$q$ from which the further transitions in~$\tau$ will lead to 
  $\sigma\concat\tau=\alpha$ getting accepted.
  \item Again, fix a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ that recognizes~$\lang$.
  Again we will convert to a new machine $\hat{\FSM}$ 
  by starting with the same states and transitions.

  Let~$S$ be the set of states reachable from~$q_0$ in $\FSM$. 
  Get $\hat{M}$ by introducing a new state $r$, and defining an
  $\varepsilon$~transition from~$r$ to each state in~$S$.
  This new state~$r$ is the start state of~$\hat{M}$. 
  Then $\hat{M}$ recognizes $\operatorname{suff}(\lang)$.
  \item 
  As before, fix a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ that recognizes~$\lang$.
  We will convert to a new machine $\hat{\FSM}$ 
  by starting with the same states and transitions.

  Introduce to the new machine a sink state~$e$ that is not accepting and from
  which any input results in a loop back to~$e$.
  Take every non-accepting state $q$ in~$\FSM$ and replace 
  it with the sink state.
  That is, all transitions into~$q$ now go to~$e$ and we omit $q$ with all of
  its outward transitions.

  This machine accepts a string in~$\lang$ if and only if it accepts all of the 
  prefixes of that string also.
  % We have 
  %   $\operatorname{allprefs}(\lang)=\setcomp{(\setcomp{\lang}\concat\kleenestar{\Sigma})}\!$. 
  %   Regular languages are closed under the operations of language concatenation
  %   and Kleene start, so $\operatorname{allprefs}(\lang)$ is regular.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We can give an alternative proof of \zcref{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff}, that   the collection of regular languages is closed under
set intersection, set difference, and set complement,
that does not rely on ``by construction.''
\begin{exparts}
\item Observe that the identity
$S\cap T=\setcomp{(\setcomp{S}\cup\setcomp{T})}$
gives intersection in terms of union and complement.
Use \zcref{lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar} to argue that
if regular languages are closed under complement then
they are also closed under intersection.
\item Use the identity
$S-T=S\cap\setcomp{T}$
to make a similar observation about set difference.
\item Show that the complement of a regular language is also a
regular language.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item \zcref{lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar}
shows that the collection of regular languages is closed under union.
\item 
This identity shows that closure under complement will also demonstrate
closure under set difference.
\item Fix a regular language~$\lang$ over some alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  It is recognized by some deterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$
  whose input alphabet is~$\Sigma$.

  Define a new machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ with the same states and
  transition function as~$\FSM$ but whose accepting states are the
  complement,
  $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}-F_{\FSM}$.
  We will show that the language of this machine is 
  $\kleenestar{\Sigma}-\lang$.

  Because $\FSM$ is deterministic, each input string~$\tau$ is associated with a
  unique last state, the state that the machine is in after it
  consumes $\tau$'s last character, namely $\hat{\Delta}(\tau)$.
  Observe that $\hat{\Delta}(\tau)\in F_{\hat{\FSM}}$ if and only if
  $\hat{\Delta}(\tau)\notin F_{\FSM}$.
  Thus the language of~$\hat{\FSM}$ is the
  complement of the language of~$\FSM$ and
  since this language is recognized by a Finite State machine, it too
  is regular.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the language recognized by a Finite State machine with~$n$ states is 
infinite if and only if 
\citetext{the machine accepts at least one string of length $k$, where $n\leq k <2n$}{%
  This gives an algorithm that inputs a Finite State machine and determines,
  in a finite time, if it recognizes an infinite language.}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  For one direction suppose 
  that the machine accepts at least one string, $\sigma$, 
  of length $k$ where $n\leq k <2n$.
  In processing~$\sigma$ the machine must go through at least $n$-many
  transitions.
  But the machine has~$n$ states and starting at~$q_0$ and visiting all
  the other states exactly once would require only $n-1$~many transitions.
  Thus, by the Pigeonhole principle, 
  in processing the string~$\sigma$ the machine visits some state~$q$ twice.
  That is, in processing~$\sigma$ the machine makes a loop. 

  Said another way, the accepted string~$\sigma$ decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ 
  where processing the $\alpha$ portion takes the machine from the
  start to the loop (from state $q_0$ to~$q$),
  the $\beta$~portion takes the machine around the nontrivial loop
  (so that $\beta\neq\emptystring$ brings the machine from $q$ to~$q$ again) 
  and the $\gamma$~portion brings the machine to some 
  final state.  
  But then the recognized language is infinite, since
  clearly the machine also accepts 
  $\alpha\concat\beta\concat\beta\concat\gamma$, etc.
  (Note that we can take~$\beta$ to be of length less than~$n$ because 
  otherwise the size of the machine would imply it has a subloop.)

  Conversely, suppose the language of accepted strings is infinite.
  At least one of them is longer than $n$;~fix a minimal length  
  string that is longer.
  As in the prior paragraph,
  because the machine only has~$n$ states, 
  by the Pigeonhole principle the accepted string~$\sigma$ decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ where the $\beta$ portion is a 
  nontrivial loop so processing $\alpha$ brings the machine 
  from $q_0$ to some~$q$, then processing $\beta\neq\emptystring$ 
  brings the machine from $q$ to~$q$ again, 
  and then processing the $\gamma$ portion brings the machine to some 
  final state.  
  Because the string is of minimal length, $\lh{\beta}<n$.
  In total then, $\lh{\sigma}< 2n$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix two alphabets $\Sigma_0,\Sigma_1$.
A function $\map{h}{\Sigma_0}{\kleenestar{\Sigma_1}}$ induces a
\definend{homomorphism} on~$\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}$ via the operation
$h(\sigma\concat\tau)=h(\sigma)\concat h(\tau)$
and $h(\emptystring)=\emptystring$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Take $\Sigma_0=\B$ and $\Sigma_1=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$.
  Fix a homomorphism $\hat{h}(\str{0})=\str{a}$ and $\hat{h}(\str{1})=\str{ba}$.
  Find $\hat{h}(\str{01})$, 
  $\hat{h}(\str{10})$, 
  and $\hat{h}(\str{101})$.
\item Define $h(\lang)=\set{h(\sigma)\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}}$.
  Let $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$;
  describe it with a regular expression.
  Using the homomorphism~$\hat{h}$ from the prior item, describe 
  $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$
  with a regular expression.
\item Prove that the collection of regular languages is closed under
  homomorphism, that if $\lang$ is regular then so is $h(\lang)$.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item For the first, $\hat{h}(\str{01})=\hat{h}(\str{0}\concat\str{1})
    =\hat{h}(\str{0})\concat\hat{h}(1)=\str{aba}$. 
    Similarly $\hat{h}(\str{10})=\str{baa}$ and 
    $\hat{h}(\str{101})=\str{baaba}$
  \item A regular expression to describe 
    $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$
    is \re{(\str{0}|\str{1})*\str{1}}
    A regular expression to describe
    $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$ is \re{(a|ba)*ba}.
  \item Let $\lang$ be described by the regular expression~$R$.
    Apply the function~$h$ to each non-meta symbol in~$R$.
    The result is a regular expression that describes $h(\lang)$. 
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
    % The proofs here work with
    % deterministic Finite State machines.
    Find a nondeterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ so that
    producing another machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by
    taking the complement of the accepting states,
    $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=\setcomp{(F_{\FSM})}\!$,
    will not result in the language of the second machine
    being the complement of the language of the first.
\begin{answer}\verified
  For this machine the language of accepted strings contains~\str{1}.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm57.pdf}
  \end{center}
  If we complement the set of accepting states then the language
  of the resulting machine also contains~\str{1}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
    We will show that the class of regular languages is closed under
    reversal.
    Recall that the reversal of the language is
    defined to be the set of reversals of the strings in the language
    $\reversal{\lang}=\set{\reversal{\sigma}\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item Show that for any two strings the reversal of the concatenation
      is the concatenation, in the opposite order, of the reversals
      $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1)}
         =\reversal{\sigma_1}\concat\reversal{\sigma_0}\!$.
    \hint~do induction on the length of $\sigma_1$.
    \item We will prove the result by showing that for any
    regular expression~$R$, the reversal $\reversal{\lang(R)}$ is 
    described by a regular expression.
    We will construct this expression 
    by defining a reversal operation on regular expressions.
    Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$ and let
    \begin{parenum}
    \item~$\reversal{\emptyset}=\emptyset$,
    \item~$\reversal{\emptystring}=\emptystring$,
    \item~$\reversal{x}=x$ for any~$x\in\Sigma$,
    \item~$\reversal{(R_0\concat R_1)}=\reversal{R_1}\,\concat\reversal{R_0}$,
    \item~$\reversal{\re{($R_0$|$R_1$)}}=\re{$\reversal{R_0}$|$\reversal{R_1}$}$,
    and \item~$\reversal{\re{($R$*)}}=\re{($\reversal{R}$)*}$.
    \end{parenum}
    (Note the connection between (4) and the prior exercise item.)
    Now show that $\reversal{R}$ describes $\reversal{\lang(R)}$. 
    \hint~use
    induction on the length of the regular expression~$R$.
  % \item
  %   Another way to prove this is to reverse the machine.
  %   Take the language to be recognized by some machine~$\FSM$.
  %   Construct a new nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by:
  %   (i)~giving $\hat{\FSM}$ all of $\FSM$'s states, so that
  %   $Q_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}$.
  %   (ii)~adding to $\hat{\FSM}$ a new state~$s$, make $\varepsilon$
  %   transitions from it to all of $\FSM$'s accepting states,
  %   and make $s$ the start state of~$\hat{\FSM}$
  %   (iii)~reverse all of the transitions in the $\FSM$'s graph, 
  %   and
  %   (iv)~turn $\FSM$'s accepting states to non-accepting states, and
  %   turn $\FSM$'s start state to an accepting state.
  %   Show that $\hat{\FSM}$ accepts $\reversal{\lang}$.
  \end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Fix $\sigma_0$.
  We prove this by induction on the length of~$\sigma_1$.
  For the base step consider both $\sigma_1=\emptystring$ and $\sigma_1=a$
  for $a\in\Sigma$.
  Both of the statements 
  $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\emptystring)}=\reversal{(\sigma_0)}=\reversal{(\emptystring)}\concat\reversal{(\sigma_0)}$ 
  and
  $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\str{a})}=\reversal{(\str{a})}\concat\reversal{(\sigma_0)}$
   obviously hold.

  For the inductive step assume that the statement is true 
  for any $\sigma_1$ when 
  $\lh{\sigma_1}=0$, $\lh{\sigma_1}=1$, \ldots{}
  $\lh{\sigma_1}=k$ and consider the  $\lh{\sigma_1}=k+1$ case.
  In this case $\sigma_1=\tau\concat a$ for some character $a\in\Sigma$
  and string $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ of length~$k$.
  Then 
  $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1)}
  =\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\tau\concat a)}\!$.
  Parenthesize as $\reversal{\bigl((\sigma_0\concat\tau)\concat a\bigr)}$ 
  and apply the base case to get
  $\reversal{a}\concat\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\tau)}\!$.
  The inductive hypothesis gives
  $=a\concat\reversal{\tau}\concat\reversal{\sigma_0}\!$,
  and finish by recognizing that as 
  $\reversal{\sigma_1}\concat\reversal{\sigma_0}\!$.
  \item
  We prove this by induction on the length of the regular expression.
  There are no regular expressions of length zero so the
  base case is that $\lh{R}=1$.
  If $R=\emptyset$ then
  by condition~(1), $\reversal{R}$ is the regular expression~$\emptyset$ 
  and
  $\lang(R)=\lang(\reversal{R})$ because both equal the empty set, as required.
  If $R=\emptystring$ then by condition~(2), $\reversal{R}=\emptystring$
  and $\lang(R)=\set{\emptystring}=\lang(\reversal{R})$, again as required.
  And, if $R=x$ for some $x\in\Sigma$ then
  by condition~(3), $\reversal{R}=x$ and 
  $\lang(R)=\set{x}=\lang(\reversal{R})$. 

  Now for the inductive step.
  Assume that the statement is true for regular expressions~$R$ of length $n=1$,
  $n=2$, \ldots{} $n=k$, and consider an expression of length~$n=k+1$.
  We handled the case that it is a concatenation, $R=R_0\concat R_1$, in this
  question's first item.

  If $R=\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}$ then
  $\lang(\reversal{R})
  =\lang(\reversal{(R_0|R_1)})
  =\lang(\reversal{R_0}|\reversal{R_1})$ by condition~(5).
  By the definition of the language associated with an alternation operation
  on regular expressions following \zcref{def:RESyntax}, that is
  $\lang(\reversal{R_0})\cup\lang(\reversal{R_1})$.
  The inductive hypothesis gives that it equals
  $\reversal{\lang(R_0)}\cup\reversal{\lang(R_1)}\!$,
  which equals
  $\set{\reversal{\sigma_0}\suchthat \sigma_0\in\lang(R_0)}
  \cup
  \set{\reversal{\sigma_1}\suchthat \sigma_1\in\lang(R_1)}$
  and again by the material following \zcref{def:RESyntax},
  that equals
  $\reversal{\lang(R)}$.

  The final form is similar.
  If $R=\re{$R_0$*}$ then 
  $\lang(\reversal{R})=\lang(\re{($\reversal{R_0}$)*})$.
  By the definition following \zcref{def:RESyntax} that equals 
  $\set{\sigma_0\concat\cdots\concat \sigma_m\suchthat \sigma_i\in\lang(\reversal{R_0})}$.
  Rewriting gives
  $\set{\reversal{\tau_0}\concat\cdots\concat \reversal{\tau_m}\suchthat \tau_j\in\lang(R_0)}$.
  By this exercise's first item that equals
  $\set{\reversal{(\tau_m\concat\cdots\concat \tau_0)}\suchthat \tau_j\in\lang(R_0)}$,
  which is the set $\reversal{(\kleenestar{\lang(R_0)})}\!$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{regular language|)}\index{language!regular|)}



% ================================================
\section{Non-regular languages}\index{language!non-regular|(}
The prior section shows that there exist languages
that are not regular, via a counting argument.
We now see a technique to show that 
specified languages are not regular.\footnote{%
\protect\zcref{extra:MyhillNerode} contains another way to show that a
language is not regular. 
While more abstract, it applies to 
all non-regular languages whereas the result here does not apply
to some (see \protect\zcref{exer:NonregularButPumpingLemmaWontProve}).}
  This is similar to the second chapter, where we first used a counting argument
  to prove that there are
  unsolvable problems and later showed that
  specific problems including the \protect\HP{} are unsolvable. 

The idea is that although Finite State machines are finite,
they can be fed arbitrarily long inputs.
For instance, the power switch from
\zcref{ex:PowerSwitch} has only two states
but even if we toggle it hundreds of times, it still keeps track of whether
the switch is on or off.
The key observation is that 
to process long inputs with only a small number of states, a machine
must revisit states, that is, it must cycle.

Cycles inside a machine cause a pattern in what that machine accepts.
%<*ex:FSMLoopingi>
The diagram below shows a machine that accepts~$\str{aabbbc}$
(it only shows part of a machine, those states that the machine traverses in
processing this input).
%</ex:FSMLoopingi>
\begin{equation*}  \label{discussion:FSMLooping}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm58.pdf}}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
%<*ex:FSMLoopingii>
Because of the cycle, this machine also accepts
$\str{a}(\str{abb})^2\str{bc}$ since that string 
takes the machine through the cycle twice. 
Likewise, this machine accepts $\str{a}(\str{abb})^3\str{bc}$,
and cycling more times pumps out even more strings that are accepted.
%</ex:FSMLoopingii>

%<*th:PumpingLemma>
\begin{theorem}[Pumping Lemma]\index{Pumping lemma}\index{Finite State machine!Pumping Lemma}  \label{th:PumpingLemma}
Let~$\lang$ be a regular language.
There is a constant~$p\in\N^+\!$, the
\definend{pumping length}\index{pumping length} 
for the language,\footnotemark\
such that every string~$\sigma\in \lang$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$
decomposes into three substrings
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ satisfying:~\begin{parenum}
\item the first two components are short, $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$,
\item $\beta$ is not empty, and 
\item the strings $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
$\alpha\beta^3\gamma$, \ldots{} are also members of the language~$\lang$.
\end{parenum}
\end{theorem}\footnotetext{%
  If a language~$\lang$ is such that the number $p$ works
  then so also does~$p+1$, $p+2$, etc.}
%</th:PumpingLemma>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:PumpingLemmai>
  Suppose that $\lang$ is recognized by the
  deterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
  For~$p$ it suffices to use the number of states in $\FSM$.

  Consider a string $\sigma\in\lang$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$.
  Finite State machines perform one transition per character 
  so the number of characters in an input string equals the number of 
  transitions.
  Thus the number of states, not necessarily distinct ones, 
  that the machine visits 
  is one more than the number of transitions.
  (For instance, 
  with a one-character input a machine visits two 
  states.) 
  So in processing~$\sigma$,
  the machine revisits at least one state. 
  It cycles.
%</pf:PumpingLemmai>

%<*pf:PumpingLemmaii>
  Of the states that are repeated as the machine processes~$\sigma$, 
  fix the one~$q$ that it revisits first.
  Also fix $\sigma$'s shortest two prefixes 
  $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i}$ and
  $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i, \ldots{} s_j}$ 
  that take the machine to~$q$.
  That is, $i$ and $j$ are minimal such that $i\neq j$ and
  the extended transition function gives
  $\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i})
     =\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_j})
     =q$.
  Let $\alpha=\sequence{s_0,\ldots\, s_i}$,
  let $\beta=\sequence{s_{i+1},\ldots\, s_j}$, 
  and let $\gamma=\sequence{s_{j+1},\ldots\, s_k}$.

  These strings satisfy conditions~(1) and~(2).
  In particular, choosing $q$, $i$, and~$j$ to be minimal
  guarantees that that $\lh{\alpha\concat\beta}\leq p$ because the machine
  has $p$-many states and so a state must repeat by at most the 
  $p$-th input character.
  For condition~(3), this string
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\gamma=\sequence{s_0,\ldots\, s_i,s_{j+1},\ldots\, s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  brings the machine from the start state~$q_0$ to~$q$,
  and then to the same ending state as did $\sigma$.
  That is,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\gamma)=\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\beta\gamma)$
  and so the machine accepts $\alpha\gamma$.
  As to the other strings in~(3),
  for instance with
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\beta^2\concat\gamma
      =\sequence{s_0,\ldots\, s_i,s_{i+1},\ldots\, s_{j},s_{i+1},\ldots\, s_{j},s_{j+1},\ldots\, s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  the substring $\alpha$ brings the machine from~$q_0$ to~$q$,
  the first $\beta$ brings it from~$q$ around to~$q$ again,
  the second $\beta$ makes the machine cycle to~$q$ yet again,
  and finally $\gamma$ brings it to the same ending state as did $\sigma$.
%</pf:PumpingLemmaii>
\end{proof}

We typically use the Pumping Lemma to show that a language is
not regular through an argument by contradiction.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}
  The canonical example is to 
  show that this language of matched parentheses is not regular.
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang
    =\set{\str{(}^n\str{)}^n\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
        n\in\N}                                      
    =\set{\varepsilon,\, \str{()},\, \str{(())},\, \str{((()))},\, 
       \str{(}^4\mspace{2mu}\str{)}^4\!,\, \ldots\,}       
  \end{equation*}
  The alphabet is the set of parentheses characters,
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{)},\str{(}}$.

  For contradiction, assume that $\lang$ is regular.
  Then the Pumping Lemma says that $\lang$ has a pumping length.
  Call it~$p$.
  Consider the string $\sigma=\str{(}^p\str{)}^p\!$.

  That string is an element of~$\lang$ and $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$. 
  Therefore it
  decomposes into three substrings $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$
  in a way that satisfies the conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that
  the length of the prefix~$\alpha\concat\beta$ is less than or equal to~$p$.
  Because of this, and because the first $p$-many characters
  of~$\sigma$
  are all open parentheses, we know that both $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ 
  are composed entirely of open parentheses.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is not empty, so it
  consists of at least one~$\str{(}$.

  Condition~(3) is that all of the strings
  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
  \ldots{}
  are elements of~$\lang$.
  To get the contradiction, consider $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$
  (there are other members of the list that 
  we could choose but we only need to choose one).
  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, this string has an extra~$\beta$,
  which adds at least one open parenthesis 
  without also adding any closed parentheses.
  That is, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more \str{(}'s than \str{)}'s. 
  It is therefore not a member of~$\lang$. 
  But the Pumping Lemma says that it must be a member of~$\lang$, 
  and thus the assumption that $\lang$ is regular leads to a contradiction.
\end{example}

% \begin{remark}
% The Pumping Lemma states that any 
% input than the pumping length 
% has a decomposition satisfying the three conditions.
% But we cannot assume that we have a particular
% decomposition;~we must do the arguments by 
% citing the conditions. 

% For example, for the machine in ($*$) above, 
% where $\sigma=\str{a}\str{abbabb}\str{bc}$ 
% (and assuming that it is at least as long as~$p$),
% one decomposition that satisfies the three conditions is 
% $\alpha=\str{a}$, $\beta=\str{abb}$, and $\gamma=\str{abbbc}$.
% But there is at least one other,  
% $\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma=\str{a}\concat\str{abb}^2\concat\str{bc}$,
% also satisfying them.
% \end{remark}

\begin{example}
  Recall that a palindrome
  is a string that reads the
  same backwards as forwards, such as
  \str{bab}, \str{abbaabba},
  or~$\str{a}^5\str{b}\str{a}^5\!$.
  We will show that the language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
  of all palindromes,
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$,
  is not regular.

  For contradiction assume that it is regular. 
  The Pumping Lemma says that therefore $\lang$ has a pumping length.
  Call it~$p$ and
  consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\!$.

  The string~$\sigma$ is an element of~$\lang$ and $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$.
  Thus it decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$
  and so both substrings $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are composed entirely of
  \str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string and so
  $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  Condition~(3) states that all of the strings
  $
    \alpha\gamma,   
    \alpha\beta^2\gamma,
    \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \ldots{}
  $ 
  are members of~$\lang$.
  Consider the first, $\alpha\gamma$
  (there are other choices that would work).

  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, 
  in $\alpha\gamma$ the substring $\beta$ is absent.
  Because $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ consist entirely of~$\str{a}$'s,
  the substring $\gamma$ has the \str{b} character from $\sigma$, 
  and hence also has the $\str{a}^p$ that follows this~\str{b}.
  So compared to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, the string $\alpha\gamma$
  omits at least one~\str{a} before the~\str{b} but none
  of the \str{a}'s after it.
  Therefore $\alpha\gamma$ is not a palindrome, contradicting
  condition~(3) of the Pumping Lemma.
\end{example}

\begin{remark} \label{rem:PLPartsAreNotSigmaParts}
In that example the string $\sigma$ has three parts,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\concat\str{b}\concat\str{a}^p\!$, and it decomposes into
three parts, $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$.
Don't think that the two decompositions line up. 
The Pumping Lemma does not say that $\alpha=\str{a}^p\!$, $\beta=\str{b}$,
and $\gamma=\str{a}^p$\Dash indeed, we've shown  
that
$\beta$ does not contain the~$\str{b}$.
Instead the lemma's first condition only says that the 
first two substrings together, 
$\alpha\beta$, consists exclusively of \str{a}'s.
So perhaps $\alpha\beta=\str{a}^p\!$, or perhaps
$\alpha\beta=\str{a}^k\!$ for some $k<p$
and $\gamma$ starts with some $\str{a}$'s
that are then followed by $\str{b}\str{a}^p\!$.
That is, all we know is that $\alpha\beta$ matches the regular expression
\re{a*} while $\gamma$ matches \re{a*baa$\,\ldots\,$a}, with $p$-many
\str{a}'s at the end.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSMCannotRecognizePlusOne}
  Consider 
  $
    \lang=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat n=m+1}
    =\set{\str{1}, \str{011}, \str{00111}, \ldots}
  $, whose
  members start with a number of \str{0}'s that is one less than
  the number of \str{1}'s at the end.
  We will prove that it is not regular.

  For contradiction assume otherwise, that $\lang$ is regular, and 
  denote its pumping length by~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{0}^{p}\str{1}^{p+1}\in\lang$.
  Because $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$, the Pumping Lemma gives a decomposition
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ satisfying the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) says that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so 
  that the substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ have only \str{0}'s
  (and thus all of $\sigma$'s \str{1}'s are in $\gamma$).
  Condition~(2) says that $\beta$ has at least one character, 
  necessarily~\str{0}.
  Consider Condition~(3)'s list:~$\alpha\gamma$,
    $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
    $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
    \dots\sentencespace
  Compare its first entry, $\alpha\gamma$, to~$\sigma$.
  Because the $\beta$ substring is absent,
  $\alpha\gamma$ has fewer \str{0}'s then does $\sigma$,
  but the same number of~\str{1}'s.
  So the number of \str{0}'s in $\alpha\gamma$ 
  is not one less than the number of \str{1}'s.
  Thus $\alpha\gamma\notin\lang$, which
  contradicts the Pumping Lemma's condition~(3).
\end{example}

We can interpret that example to say that Finite State machines
cannot recognize a predecessor-successor relationship.
We can similarly use the Pumping Lemma to show Finite State
machines cannot recognize other arithmetic relations.

The Pumping Lemma arguments so far have been very similar.
The next example shows that sometimes they vary. 

\begin{example}
The language
$  \lang  =\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat \text{$n$ is a perfect square} } 
         =\set{\emptystring, \str{a}, \str{a}^4\!, \str{a}^9\!,\str{a}^{16}\!,
                 \ldots}$
is not regular.
For, suppose otherwise.  
Denote the pumping length by~$p$ and consider $\smash{\sigma=\str{a}^{(p^2)}}\!$,
so that $\sigma\in\lang$ and $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$.

By the Pumping Lemma, $\sigma$ decomposes as
$\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the
three conditions.
Condition~(1) 
is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, which implies that $\lh{\beta}\leq p$.
Condition~(2) is that $0<\lh{\beta}$.
Now consider the strings  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \dots{}\sentencespace

We will get a contradiction from $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$.
The definition of~$\lang$ is that after $\sigma$ the next longest
string has length $(p+1)^2$. 
The difference between $p^2$ and $(p+1)^2=p^2+2p+1$ is strictly greater than~$p$.
However the gap between the length
$\lh{\sigma}=\lh{\alpha\beta\gamma}$ and the length
$\lh{\alpha\beta^2\gamma}$ is at most~$p$
because $0<\lh{\beta}\leq p$.
Hence the length of $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not a perfect square
and so $\alpha\beta^2\gamma\notin\lang$.
\end{example}

Sometimes we use the Pumping Lemma in conjunction with
the closure properties of regular languages.

\begin{example} \label{ex:AsManyAsAsBs}
  The language
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has the same number of \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$
  is not regular.
  To prove that, observe that
  $\smash{\hat{\lang}}=\set{\str{a}^m\str{b}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat m,n\in\N}$ is
  regular, described by the regular expression~\re{a*b*}. 
  Recall that the intersection of two regular languages is regular.
  But $\lang\cap\hat{\lang}$ is the set
  $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
  and \zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} shows that
  this language isn't regular,
  where we substitute \str{a} and~\str{b} for the parenthesis characters.
\end{example}

We have seen many examples of things that Finite
State machines can do and 
here we have seen things that they cannot.
This is a pleasing balance,
but we are motivated by more than symmetry.

For instance, recognizing
the language of balanced parentheses
as in \zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} is something that 
we often want to do in a compiler.
A Turing machine can solve this problem but we now know that a
Finite State machine cannot.
Therefore, to solve this problem we need 
some kind of scratch memory.
So the results in this section tell us about the resources needed to solve 
problems.


% =============================================
\begin{exercises}
% [A useful trick when you are 
%   stuck on understanding a language description is
%   to list five strings that are in the language and five that are not.
%   Also try describing the language in prose, as though over a telephone.
%   Both help you work through the formalities.]
% Add? https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/156824/50343

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:FSMCannotRecognizePlusOne} shows that
$\lang=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m+1=n}$
is not regular but your friend doesn't get it and asks you,
``What's wrong with the regular expression \re{$\str{0}^{n}\str{1}^{n+1}$}?''
Help them.
\begin{answer}\verified
  There is no such regular expression.
  The syntax of \zcref{def:RESyntax} 
  does not have the capacity for that construct.

  If we fix, say, $n=2$, then we can make a regular expression 
  $\str{0}^2\str{1}^3\!$.
  We can use alternation to connect a finite number of such
  expressions, as in \re{\str{0}|\str{011}|$\str{0}^2\str{1}^3$|$\ldots$\,$\str{0}^{9}\str{1}^{10}\!$}.
  But no regular expression describes all of the strings in the language~$\lang$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:BalancedParenFirstStringNoGood}
\zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} uses $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$
to show that the language of
balanced parentheses is not regular.
Instead get the contradiction by showing that
$\alpha\gamma$ is not a member of the language.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, the string $\alpha\gamma$ has 
  had~$\beta$ omitted.
  By definition $\sigma$ has balanced parentheses.
  Because~$\beta$ consists of at least one open parenthesis and has no
  closed parentheses,
  the omission of $\beta$ means that in $\alpha\gamma$ the number of 
  open parentheses is at least one fewer than the number of 
  closed parentheses.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your friend has been thinking.
They say, ``Hey, the diagram ($*$) before \zcref{th:PumpingLemma} doesn't apply
unless the language is infinite.
Sometimes languages are regular because they only have like three or
four strings.
But the Pumping Lemmas third condition requires that infinitely
many strings be in the language, so the Pumping Lemma is wrong.''
In what way do they need to further refine their thinking?
\begin{answer}\verified
  If a language is finite, $\lang=\set{\sigma_0,\ldots{}\, \sigma_n}$,
  then the conclusion of the Pumping Lemma holds by taking the pumping
  length to be $p>\max(\lh{\sigma_0},\ldots\, \lh{\sigma_n})$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % Derived from https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/30718/67754
  % but not cited because it is mistaken, so credit may not be welcome
Someone in the class emails you, ``If a language has a string with 
length greater than the number of states, which is the pumping length, 
then it cannot be a regular language.''
Correct?
\begin{answer}\verified
It is not correct.
The first problem is that it is not sensible.
There may be many Finite State machines that recognize the language, so
``number of states'' for a language is not well-defined.

Even if we grant that they meant to fix a particular machine, saying
something like, ``where $\FSM$ accepts the language, if that language has a 
string of length greater than the number of states in $\FSM$ then it cannot be a regular language''
then it still isn't sensible because if there is a Finite State machine
then the language for sure is regular.  

And, even if we stretch figuring out what they meant all the way to 
taking that they meant something like this, 
``If in an argument we assume that the language
is recognized by a Finite State machine and we go on to 
show that the language has
a string that is longer than the number of states in that machine,
then we  have the desired contradiction'' then this statement, while now
technically sensible, is wrong.
For example, there is a Finite State machine that accepts strings
described by \re{a*}, and there certainly are strings of that form
whose length is greater than the number of states in the machine.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your study partner has read \zcref{rem:PLPartsAreNotSigmaParts}
but it is still sinking in.
About the matched parentheses example, 
\zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}, they say,
``So $\sigma=\str{(}^p\str{)}^p$, and $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$.
We know that $\alpha\beta$ consists only of \str{(}'s, so it must
be that $\gamma$ consists of \str{)}'s.''
Give them a prompt.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The Pumping Lemma only allows us to conclude that $\alpha\beta$ consists
  of \str{(}'s, not that this substring got all the \str{(}'s.
  The substring~$\gamma$ indeed got all of the close parentheses, the \str{)}'s,
  but that we know, it may have gotten some of the open parentheses as well.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each problem, fill in the blanks to show that the problem is not regular.
\begin{quotation}\it
\noindent
Assume otherwise, that the language is regular.
Then there is a number~$p$ that is its pumping length.
Consider the string $\sigma=\fillinblankLabelled{(1)}$.
It is in the language and $\sizeof{\sigma}\geq p$ so the Pumping Lemma
says that it decomposes $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$
subject to three conditions.
The first condition is that $\sizeof{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, and so
\fillinblankLabelled{(2)}.
The second condition is that $\beta\neq\emptystring$, and so
\fillinblankLabelled{(3)}.
Finally, the third condition is that all of the strings
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$, \ldots{} 
are also members of the language.
But that is a contradiction because compared with $\sigma$, the
string \fillinblankLabelled{(4)}
has 
\fillinblankLabelled{(5)},
and is therefore not in the langugage.
\end{quotation}
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{c}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$ 
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^{2n}\suchthat n\in\N}$ 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
  (1)~$\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{c}^p$,
  (2)~the string $\alpha\concat\beta$ consists entirely of \str{a}'s,
  (3)~$\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a},
  (4)~$\alpha\gamma$,
  (5)~at least one fewer \str{a} but the same number of \str{c}'s.
  (For (4) and~(5) other choices are possible.)
\item
  (1)~$\str{a}^p\str{b}^{2p}$,
  (2)~the string $\alpha\concat\beta$ consists entirely of \str{a}'s,
  (3)~$\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a},
  (4)~$\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
  (5)~at least one extra \str{a} but the same number of \str{b}'s.
  (For (4) and~(5) other choices are possible.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}  \label{}
% In class someone asks, ``Isn't it true that languages don't have a 
% unique pumping length?
% That if a length of $p=5$ will do then $p=6$ will also do?''
% Before the prof answers, what do you think?
% \begin{answer}\verified
%   Yes, that is right.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
    For each, give five strings that are elements of the language 
    and five that are not, and then show that the language is not regular
    by using the Pumping Lemma.
    \begin{exparts*}
    \item $\lang_0=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n+2=m}$
    \item $\lang_1=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\str{c}^n\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
    \item $\lang_2=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n<m}$
    \end{exparts*}    
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Strings are in this language if they consist of a string of \str{a}'s
  followed by a string of \str{b}'s, and the number of \str{b}'s is two 
  more than the number of~\str{a}'s. 
  Five elements of $\lang_0$ are \str{bb}, \str{abbb}, \str{aabbbb}, 
  $\str{a}^3\str{b}^5$, and~$\str{a}^4\str{b}^6\!$.
  Five strings that are not elements are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{aabbb}, and~\str{baabbb}. 

  Assume for contradiction that $\lang_0$ is regular.
  By the Pumping Lemma it has a pumping length,~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+2}$.
  This string is a member of the language that is of length greater
  than or equal to~$p$ so
  the Pumping Lemma gives a decomposition 
  $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so 
  because the first $p$-many characters of $\sigma$ are all \str{a}'s,
  the two substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ contain only~\str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is nonempty, so it consists of at least 
  one~\str{a}.

  Consider the list 
  $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Its second element $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more~\str{a}'s, 
  but no more~\str{b}'s, than does $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$.
  That means $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not an element of the 
  language~$\lang_0$, a contradiction to the Pumping Lemma's condition~(3).
  \item 
  In prose, this language consists of 
  \str{a}'s followed by~\str{b}'s and then followed by~\str{c}'s,
  with only the restriction that the number of 
  \str{a}'s equals the number of~\str{c}'s.
  Thus five elements of $\lang_1$ are:~\emptystring, 
  \str{ac}, \str{b}, \str{aabcc}, and~\str{bb}. 
  Five that are not are:~\str{aac}, \str{acc}, \str{ab}, \str{bbc}, and~\str{ca}.

  Assume for contradiction that $\lang_1$ is regular.
  Then it has a pumping length~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{c}^p\!$.
  Because $\sigma\in\lang_1$ and $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$, the Pumping Lemma
  says that it decomposes, $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$, 
  in a way that satisfies the three conditions.
  By condition~(1) the first two substrings together are short, 
  $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$.
  Thus these two consist entirely of~$\str{a}$'s.
  By condition~(2) the second substring~$\beta$ is nonempty,
  so it consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  Finally, condition~(3) gives that the strings 
  $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all members of
  the language~$\lang_1$.
  But the string~$\alpha\gamma$, when compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  has at least one fewer~\str{a} but the same number of~\str{c}'s.
  That means that the number of \str{a}'s in $\sigma$ differs from the
  number of~\str{c}'s,
  so it is not a member of the language, which is a contradiction.
  \item 
  In $\lang_2$ the strings have \str{a}'s followed by \str{b}'s,
  with the number of \str{a}'s less than the number of \str{b}'s. 
  So five strings that are members of $\lang_2$ are 
  \str{abb}, \str{abbb}, \str{abbbb}, $\str{a}\str{b}^5$, 
  and~$\str{a}^4\str{b}^5\!$.
  Five strings that are not elements are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{aab}, and~\str{baabbb}. 

  For contradiction 
  suppose that $\lang_2$ is regular and let its pumping length be~$p$.
  The string $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+1}$ is an element of the language
  whose length is greater than or equal to the pumping length.
  So the Pumping Lemma gives a decomposition, 
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1), that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, says that these two substrings 
  contain only~\str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2), that $\beta$ is nonempty, says that it consists of at least 
  one~\str{a}.

  Consider the strings 
  $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  the string $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more~\str{a}'s, but no more~\str{b}'s.
  Thus the number of \str{a}'s is at least~$p+1$ while the number of \str{b}'s
  is fixed at~$p+1$.
  That means that $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not a member of the 
  language~$\lang_2$, which contradicts the Pumping Lemma's condition~(3).
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language % over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ 
produce five members.
Then decide whether that language is regular.
Prove your assertion either by producing a
regular expression or using the Pumping Lemma.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n=3}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n+3=m}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat m-n>12}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Five strings that are members are \str{aaa}, \str{aaab}, \str{aaabb},
  \str{aaabbb}, and~\str{aaabbbb}.
  This languages is regular, and a regular expression that describes it
  is~\re{aaab*}.
  \item Five strings in this language are 
  \str{bbb}, \str{abbbb}, \str{aabbbbb}, $\str{a}^3\str{b}^6$, 
  and~$\str{a}^4\str{b}^7\!$.
  This language is not regular.

  To get a contradiction assume that the language is regular.
  The Pumping Lemma says that the language has a pumping length~$p$.
  The string $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+3}$ is a member of the language
  and has length greater than or equal to the pumping length.
  So it decomposes, $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, in a way that is 
  subject to the three conditions.
  The first condition is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$ and so 
  the two substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ contain only \str{a}'s.
  The second condition is that $\lh{\beta}>0$ and so the substring
  $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.

  Finally, consider the list $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, 
  \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Condition~(3) says that any element of that list is a member of the language.
  However, the list's first element has fewer \str{a}'s than $\sigma$ 
  but the same number of~\str{b}'s, and therefore the number of \str{a}'s is not
  three less than the number of~\str{b}'s.
  This is a contradiction.
  \item Five strings in this language are \emptystring, \str{aab}, \str{b},
  \str{aaaabbbb}, and~\str{abbbb}.
  This language is regular.
  A regular expression that describes it is \re{a*b*}.
  \item Five strings in this language are 
  $\str{b}^{13}$, $\str{a}\str{b}^{14}$, $\str{b}^{14}$,
   $\str{a}^2\str{b}^{15}$, and~$\str{a}\str{b}^{15}$.
  This language is not regular.

  Assume that it is regular, for contradiction.
  Then the language has a pumping length~$p$.
  Let $\sigma=\str{a}^{p}\str{b}^{p+13}\!$.
  By the Pumping Lemma it decomposes into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$
  satisfying the three conditions.
  A consequence of the first condition is that 
  the first two substrings contain only
  of \str{a}'s.
  A consequence of the second condition is that $\beta$ consists of at least
  one~\str{a}.

  Consider $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace.
  The string $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has at least one more~\str{a}
  than does~$\sigma$.
  Thus the number of \str{b}s' minus the number of \str{a}'s 
  is not more than $12$, which means that it is not a member of the language.
  That contradicts the Pumping Lemma's third condition.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Each language is non-regular and $\sigma$ is a good choice 
as part of a proof using the Pumping Lemma, where $p$ is the pumping length.
For each, give the most specific
regular expression describing $\alpha\beta$ and $\gamma$.
Take $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^{2n}\suchthat n\in\N}$,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{2p}$  
\item $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^{n+5}\suchthat n\in\N}$,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+5}$  
\item $\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^{j}\str{a}^{i+j}\suchthat \text{$i\in\N$ and $j\in\N^+$}}$,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^{p+1}$  
\item $\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\concat\tau\suchthat \text{$k\in\N$ and $\lh{\tau}=k$}}$,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p}$  
\item $\lang=\set{\tau\suchthat \text{$\tau$ is a palindrome and $\lh{\tau}$ is even}}$,
$\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{bb}\str{a}^{p}$  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The two substrings of $\sigma$ are: $\alpha\beta$ matches \re{a*}
while $\gamma$ matches \re{a*bb$\,\ldots\,$b} where there are $2p$-many 
\str{b}'s.    
\item The substring $\alpha\beta$ matches \re{a*}
while $\gamma$ matches \re{a*bb$\,\ldots\,$b} with $p+5$-many 
\str{b}'s.    
\item The substring $\alpha\beta$ matches \re{a*}
while $\gamma$ matches \re{a*ba$\,\ldots\,$a} with $p+1$-many 
\str{a}'s.    
\item The substring $\alpha\beta$ matches \re{a*}
and $\gamma$ matches \re{a*b$\,\ldots\,$b} with $p$-many 
\str{b}'s.    
\item The substring $\alpha\beta$ matches \re{a*}
while $\gamma$ matches \re{a*bba$\,\ldots\,$a} with $p$-many 
\str{a}'s.    
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
With a friend you try to apply the Pumping Lemma to
$\set{\tau\concat\tau\suchthat \tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item List five elements of the language.
\item You pick $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\str{b}$; go through the
  argument.
\item Your friend tries $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{a}^p$ and can't get their
 argument to go.
 Suggestions?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
  \item Five members of this language are 
  \emptystring, \str{abab}, \str{babbab}, \str{aaaa}, and~\str{aaabaaab}.
  \item
  Suppose otherwise, that it is regular.
  By the Pumping Lemma the language has a pumping length~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\concat\str{a}^p\str{b}$.
  It is a member of the language whose length is greater than or equal to~$p$ and
  so it has a decomposition $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ that satisfies the
  three conditions.
  The first condition, $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, gives that the two 
  substrings contain only \str{a}'s.
  The second condition, $\lh{\beta}>0$, gives that the second substring
  is nonempty and so
  consists of at least one~\str{a}.

  Now consider the list from the third condition, $\alpha\gamma$, 
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Compare $\alpha\gamma$ to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$.
  Because the $\beta$ is omitted in $\alpha\gamma$, before its first~\str{b} it
  has fewer \str{a}'s than $\sigma$ has before its first~\str{b}.
  But before the second~\str{b} the two strings have the same number of
  \str{a}'s.
  So $\alpha\gamma$ is not a member of the language, 
  which contradicts the third condition.
  \item The argument in the prior part has a marker~\str{b} that ends up in
  the substring~$\gamma$.
  The attempted argument in this part has no marker, so we cannot compare
  the string $\sigma$ to the strings in the list 
  $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Your friend needs to pick a string with a marker of some kind.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Use the Pumping Lemma to prove that
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^{m-1}\str{c}\str{b}^{m}\suchthat m\in\N^+}$
is not regular.
It may help to first produce five strings from the language.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Five strings from the language are \str{cb}, \str{acbb}, \str{aacbbb},
  \str{aaacbbbb}, and \str{aaaacbbbbb}.

  To show that this language is not regular, 
  assume for contradiction that it is.
  Then the language has a pumping length~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{c}\str{b}^{p+1}$.
  Because this string is a member of the language and has length at least~$p$,
  the Pumping Lemma says that it decomposes into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  subject to the three conditions.

  The first condition, $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, implies that the two 
  substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ contain only \str{a}'s.
  The second condition, $\lh{\beta}>0$, implies that $\beta$ consists of
  at least one~\str{a}.

  Now, in the list $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} consider the
  first one, $\alpha\gamma$.
  By the prior paragraph, compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ this string
  has at least one fewer~\str{a} but the same number of~\str{b}'s.
  So the number of \str{a}'s is not one less than the number of \str{b}'s,
  and this string is not in the language, which contradicts the third
  condition. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the language over $\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
of strings having more \str{a}'s than \str{b}'s is not
regular.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Let $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
                 \suchthat\text{the number of \str{a}'s is greater than the number of \str{b}'s}}$.
  Assume for contradiction that it is regular.
  Then the Pumping Lemma applies and this language has a pumping length~$p$.
  The string $\sigma=\str{a}^{p+1}\str{b}^{p}$ is an element of the language
  whose length is greater than or equal to~$p$.
  So there is a decomposition $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ that satisfies the
  three conditions.
  Condition~(1) gives that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$ and so these two
  substrings contain only \str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) gives that $\beta$ is nonempty and thus it consists of 
  at least one~\str{a}.

  The contradiction comes on considering condition~(3)'s list
  $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace. 
  The first entry in that list, $\alpha\gamma$, when compared with 
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ will have at least one fewer~\str{a}
  and the same number of~\str{b}'s.
  It is therefore not a member of the language~$\lang$,
  contradicting condition~(3).
  This contradiction shows that~$\lang$ is not regular.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular, one is not.
Which is which?
(Prove your assertions.)
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat n=m^2}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat 3<m,n}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first is not regular while the second is regular.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item For contradiction, suppose that this language is regular.
  Then it has a pumping length~$p$.
  Fix the string $\sigma=\str{a}^{p^2}\str{b}^p\!$.
  It is a member of the language whose length is greater than or equal to~$p$
  so it decomposes into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ in a way that satisfies the
  three conditions.
  Condition~(1) implies that both $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ contain only~\str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) implies that $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.

  Now consider condition~(3)'s list, $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  Compared to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, the entry $\alpha\gamma$ has 
  at least one fewer~\str{a} but the same number of~\str{b}'s.
  Therefore the number of \str{a}'s is not the square of the number
  of \str{b}'s, and so $\alpha\gamma$ is not in the language.
  That contradicts condition~(3).  
  \item A regular expression that describes this language is 
  \re{aaaa*bbbb*}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user David Richerby, \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/97885/67754}
\end{credit}
Is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B} \suchthat 
  \text{$\sigma=\alpha\beta\reversal{\alpha}$ for $\alpha,\beta\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$ regular?
Either way, prove it.
\begin{answer}\verified 
  The language is regular.
  This is a trick question:~take $\alpha=\emptystring$ to get that the set equals
  $\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the language
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{1}}}
            \suchthat \text{$\lh{\sigma}=n!$ for some $n\in\N$}}$
is not regular.
\hint~the differences $(n+1)!-n!$ grow without bound.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Assume that it is regular and let the pumping length be~$p$
  (for the parenthesized comment below it is convenient to take $p>1$).
  Take $\sigma=\str{1}^{p!}\!$.
  This string is a member of~$\lang$ and has length at least~$p$,
  so the Pumping Lemma says that it decomposes as $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  subject to the three conditions.
  The second condition implies that $\lh{\beta}>0$.
  Consider the list $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
  \ldots{}\sentencespace
  After the first, the difference between the lengths of successive list elements
  is always~$\lh{\beta}$.
  But, as the hint notes, the difference between successive factorials
  is not fixed, instead it grows without bound.
  (Said another way, the difference in length between
  $\sigma=\str{1}^{p!}$ and $\hat{\sigma}=\str{1}^{(p+1)!}$ is 
  $(p+1)!-p!=\bigl((p+1)-1\bigr)\cdot p!=p\cdot p!$.
  But the difference in length between $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$
  and $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is $\lh{\beta}$, which is smaller than
  $p\cdot p!$ since $\beta$ is a substring of a length~$p!$ string,
  and we took $p>1$.)
  So there is a list element that is not in the language, which 
  contradicts the third condition.
  Thus the language is not regular.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}  
  
\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular, one is not:
$\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat m,n\in\N}$ and
$\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
Which is which?
(Prove the assertions.)
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first is regular, and is described by the regular expression \re{0*10*}.
  The second is not regular.
  To prove that, assume for contradiction that it is regular.
  Then it has a pumping length~$p$.
  Let $\sigma=\str{0}^p\str{1}\str{0}^p$. \
  Then $\sigma$ is a member of the language
  and has length at least~$p$ so the Pumping Lemma says it decomposes into
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ in a way that satisfies the three conditions.
  The first condition implies that the $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ substrings
  contain only~\str{0}'s.
  The second condition says that $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{0}.

  Consider the strings on the third conditions's list $\alpha\gamma$, 
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{}\sentencespace
  By the prior paragraph the first, $\alpha\gamma$, has fewer \str{0}'s
  before the \str{1} than does $\sigma$, but the same number of \str{0}'s
  after the~\str{1}.  
  Hence it is not a member of the language, contradicting the Pumping Lemma's
  third condition.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a Finite State machine that recognizes this language
of all sums totaling less than four,
$\lang_4=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$ and $k<4$}}$.
Use the Pumping Lemma to show that no Finite State machine recognizes 
the language of all sums,
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$}}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The language $\lang_4=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$ and $k<4$}}$ is finite and any finite language is regular.

  As to the language of all sums, 
  $\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$}}$, 
  suppose that it is regular and fix a pumping length~$p$.
  Fix $\sigma=a^pb^0c^p\!$.
  Because $|\sigma|\geq p$, it has a decomposition 
  $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ subject to the three conditions
  of the Pumping Lemma.
  By the first condition the length $|\alpha\beta|$ is less than or equal to~$p$,
  and so $\alpha\concat\beta$ has only \str{a}'s.
  By the second condition the string~$\beta$ is nonempty and so it 
  consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  The third condition says that $\alpha\gamma$ is also a member of the language,
  but that is a contradiction since this string has fewer \str{a}'s than 
  \str{c}'s, and it has no \str{b}'s because $\sigma$ had no \str{b}'s to
  start with, and thus $\alpha\gamma$ is not a member of~$\lang$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rich}  % , p 182.
\end{credit}
Decide if each is a regular language of bitstrings:
\begin{exparts*}
\item the number of \str{0}'s plus the number of \str{1}'s equals five,
\item the number of \str{0}'s minus the number of \str{1}'s equals five.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item It is regular because it is finite, 
  $\set{\str{00000}, \str{00001}, \str{00010}, \ldots{} \str{11111}}$.
  \item The language 
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{the number of \str{0}'s minus the number of \str{1}'s is five}}$
  is not regular.
  For, suppose otherwise.
  Let the language have pumping length~$p$ 
  and let $\sigma=\str{0}^{p+5}\str{1}^p\in\lang$.
  It has length at least~$p$ so 
  decompose it into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1), that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, implies that these two substrings
  have only \str{0}'s. 
  Condition~(2) says that $\beta$ is not empty so it consists of at least
  one~\str{0}.

  Condition~(3) says that every string on the list $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} is a member of~$\lang$, 
  but that is not true here.
  By the prior paragraph the string 
  $\alpha\gamma$ has at least one fewer~\str{0} than the string 
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, but it has the same number of~\str{1}'s. 
  It is therefore not an element of~$\lang$.
  By this contradiction we have shown that $\lang$ is not regular.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that
$\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\neq n}$
is not regular.
\textit{Hint:} use the closure properties of regular languages
and consider
the set $\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^m\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\in\N}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Call this language
  $\lang=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\neq n}$.
  Where 
  $\hat{\lang}=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^m\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\in\N}$,
  we have $\hat{\lang}=\kleenestar{\B}-\lang$.
  By \zcref{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff}, regular languages are closed
  under set difference. 
  Clearly $\kleenestar{\B}$ is regular\Dash it is described by the
  regular expression \re{(\str{0}|\str{1})*}\Dash so if $\lang$ were
  regular then it would imply that $\hat{\lang}$ is regular also.
  But $\hat{\lang}$ is not regular, since it is the language
  of balanced parentheses, \zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang},
  with \str{0}'s substituted for open parentheses and \str{1}'s 
  for closed parentheses. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\newcommand{\underbracketthick}{0.10ex}
\newcommand{\underbrackethgt}{1pt}
\zcref{ex:AsManyAsAsBs} shows that
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has as many \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$
is not regular.
In contrast, show that 
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has as many \str{ab}'s as \str{ba}'s}}$
is regular.
\hint~think of \str{ab} and \str{ba} as marking a transition from
a block of one character to a block of another. 
For instance, this string has three such transitions 
$\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{a}\str{a}\str{a}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{b}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{a}\str{a}\str{a}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{b}\str{b}\str{b}}$
(and is not in a member of~$\lang$).
\begin{answer}\verified
\newcommand{\underbracketthick}{0.10ex}
\newcommand{\underbrackethgt}{1pt}
Following the hint, for a string to be a member of the language $\lang$,
it must have the same number of transitions in as transitions out.
For instance, the string $\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{a}\str{a}\str{a}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{b}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{a}\str{a}\str{a}}\underbracket[\underbracketthick][\underbrackethgt]{\str{b}\str{b}\str{b}}$
is not a member of~$\lang$ because 
there are two transitions into a sequence of \str{b}'s 
from a sequence of \str{a}'s, but only one transition out of a sequence of 
\str{b}'s and back to a sequence of \str{a}'s.

In short, $\lang$ is the set of strings whose last character equals its
first character.
Thus the regular expression is \re{(a(a|b)*a)|(b(a|b)*b)}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Rebut someone who says to you, 
``Sure, for the machine before \zcref{th:PumpingLemma}
on page~\pageref{discussion:FSMLooping},
a single loop will cause $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$.
But if the machine had a double
loop like below then you'd need a longer decomposition.''
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa51.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The proof of the Pumping Lemma uses the Pigeonhole Principle to show that 
  there is at least one loop, and 
  it then reasons with that loop.
  It is unaffected by there being a later loop.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
    
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:NoFSMAcceptsPrimes}
Show that 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
\text{$\sigma=\str{1}^n$ where $n$ is prime}}$
is not a regular language.
\hint~the third condition's sequence has a 
constant difference in string lengths. 
\begin{answer}\verified
  Assume for contradiction that this language is regular 
  and fix a pumping length~$p$.
  Consider condition~(3)'s list.
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\gamma, \alpha\beta^2\gamma, \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \ldots{}\,  \alpha\beta^n\gamma, \ldots{} 
    \qquad n\geq 2
  \end{equation*}
  All of the gaps between strings on this list (except for the first gap) 
  have the same size,
  $\lh{\alpha\beta^{n+1}\gamma}-\lh{\alpha\beta^n\gamma}=\lh{\beta}$.
