We show here just five lines out of its~$81$ but that's enough to
make the point about its complexity.
\begin{lstlisting}
(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:(?:(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]
)+|\Z|(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t]))*"(?:(?:
\r\n)?[ \t])*)(?:\.(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\031]+(?:(?:(
?:\r\n)?[ \t])+|\Z|(?=[\["()<>@,;:\\".\[\]]))|"(?:[^\"\r\\]|\\.|(?:(?:\r\n)?[ 
\t]))*"(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*))*@(?:(?:\r\n)?[ \t])*(?:[^()<>@,;:\\".\[\] \000-\0
\end{lstlisting} % }{%

Even if we do have a string that fits
the standard and we send it off into the Internet,
we don't know that there is an email server listening at that address that
can handle every legal string.
In practice, people often use a first approximation regex such as
\re{\textbackslash S+@\textbackslash S+}, 
for instance as a sanity check on a web form that expects users to input
an email address.

At this point regexes may be starting to seem
a little less like a fast and neat problem-solver
and a little more like a potential development and maintenance problem.
The full story is that sometimes a regex is just what you need
for a quick job,
and sometimes they are good for more complex tasks also.
But some of the time the cost of complexity
outweighs the gain in expressiveness.
This power/complexity tradeoff is often referred to online by citing this
quote from
% \urldef\zawinski\url{https://groups.google.com/forum/?hl=en#!msg/alt.religion.emacs/DR057Srw5-c/Co-2L2BKn7UJ}
\citetext{J~Zawinski}{%
  The post is from \texttt{alt.religion.emacs} on 1997-Aug-12.
  For some reason it keeps disappearing from the online archive.
  The full discussion reveals that the quote is more dogmatic than
  the complete assertion.
  One response to the quote is,
  ``Some people, when confronted with a problem, think
  `I know, I'll quote Jamie Zawinski.'
  Now they have two problems.'' 
  (Martin Liebach, 2009-Mar-04, 
   \url{https://m.lieba.ch/2009/03/04/regex-humor/}.}.

\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  {\begin{center}\begin{minipage}{0.8\textwidth}
    \begin{raggedright}\small
    \noindent The notion that [regexes] are the solution to all
    problems is \ldots{} braindead.
    \ldots\sentencespace
    Some people, when confronted with a problem, think "I know,
    I'll use regular expressions."  
    \citetext{Now they have two problems.}{%
      A classic example is trying to use regular expressions to parse
      an HTML document.
      Sometimes scraping a fixed document to get some needed data by using 
      regexes is just what you need, quick and not too hard. 
      But to parse significant parts of an HTML document, or to 
      try to anticipate possible changes, just leads to horrors.
      See \autocite{bobnice}.}
    \end{raggedright}
  \end{minipage}\end{center}}
}{%
  \smallskip
  \noindent\hspace*{0.5em}\begin{minipage}{0.48\textwidth}
  \begin{raggedright}\small
  \noindent The notion that [regexes] are the solution to all
  problems is \ldots{} braindead.
  \ldots\sentencespace
  Some people, when confronted with a problem, think "I know,
  I'll use regular expressions."  
  \citetext{Now they have two problems.}{%
    A classic example is trying to use regular expressions to parse
    an HTML document.
    Sometimes scraping a fixed document to get some needed data by using 
    regexes is just what you need, quick and not too hard. 
    But to parse significant parts of an HTML document, or to 
    try to anticipate possible changes, just leads to horrors.
    See \autocite{bobnice}.}
  \end{raggedright}
  \end{minipage}
  \hspace*{0.5em}%
  \begin{minipage}{0.48\textwidth}
  % https://xkcd.com/1171/
    \begin{tabular}[t]{c}
      \includegraphics[scale=0.25]{automata/pix/xkcdperlproblems.png}  \\
      \href{https://xkcd.com/1171/}{Courtesy \texttt{xkcd.com}}
    \end{tabular}
  \end{minipage}
}

% As with the postal codes, one pattern that you see 
% in practice if for programmers to make the engineering choice to use
% regular expressions to give something of a rough cut, something
% good-enough.
% But they have plenty of other uses also, such as validating
% new usernames, or input to a database.

% regex for Roman numerals: https://phvu.wordpress.com/2009/05/19/an-cfg-for-roman-numerals/
% '^M{0,4}(CM|CD|D?C{0,3})(XC|XL|L?X{0,3})(IX|IV|V?I{0,3})$'

% Possible exercise:
%  https://pretty-rfc.herokuapp.com/RFC3986#reserved
\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Which of the strings matches the regex
\re{ab+c}?
\begin{exparts*}
\item \str{abc}
\item \str{ac}
\item \str{abbb}
\item \str{bbc}
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Which of the strings matches the regex
\re{[a-z]+[\textbackslash .\textbackslash? !]}?
\begin{exparts*}
\item \str{battle!}
\item \str{Hot}
\item \str{green}
\item \str{swamping.\@}
\item \str{jump up.\@}
\item \str{undulate?}
\item \str{is.?}
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regex for each.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Match a string that has \str{ab} followed by zero or more~\str{c}'s,
\item \str{ab} followed by one or more~\str{c}'s,
\item \str{ab} followed by zero or one~\str{c},
\item \str{ab} followed by two~\str{c}'s,
\item \str{ab} followed by between two and five~\str{c}'s,
\item \str{ab} followed by two or more~\str{c}'s,
\item \str{a} followed by either \str{b} or~\str{c}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{.*abc*.*}
\item \re{.*abc+.*}
\item \re{.*abc{2}.*}
\item \re{.*abc{2,5}.*}
\item \re{.*abc{2,}.*}
\item \re{.*a(b|c).*}
   or \re{.*a[bc].*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regex to
accept a string for each description.
\begin{exparts}
\item Containing the substring \str{abe}.
\item Containing only upper and lower case ASCII letters and digits.
\item Containing a string of between one and three digits.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{.*abe.*}
Note that in practice you often can just write \re{abe}; see your
language's documentation.
\item \re{[a-zA-Z0-9]*}
\item \re{[\textasciicircum]}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regex to
accept a string for each description.
Take the English vowels to be \str{a}, \str{e}, \str{i}, \str{o}, and~\str{u}.
\begin{exparts}
\item Starting with a vowel and containing the substring \str{bc}.
\item Starting with a vowel and containing the substring \str{abc}.
\item Containing the five vowels in ascending order.
\item Containing the five vowels.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{(a|e|i|o|u).*bc.*}
\item \re{(a|e|i|o|u)(bc|.*bc).*}
\item \re{.*a.*e.*i.*o.*u.*}
\item \re{(.*a.*e.*i.*o.*u.*)|(.*a.*e.*i.*u.*o.*)|\ldots |(.*u.*o.*i.*e.*a.*)}
 There are $5!=120$ clauses.
 You might use a program to write this regex.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regex matching strings that contain an open 
square bracket and an open curly brace.
\begin{answer}
\re{.*([.*\{|\{.*[).*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Every lot of land in New York City is denoted by a string of digits called BBL,
for Borough (one digit), Block (five digits), and Lot (four digits).
Give a regex.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textbackslash d\{10\}}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:NAPhoneNumber} gives a regex for North American phone numbers.
\begin{exparts*}
\item They are sometimes written with parentheses around the area code.
  Extend the regex to cover this case.
\item Sometimes phone numbers do not include the area code.
  Extend to cover this also.  
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{(\textbackslash d\{3\}|\textbackslash (\textbackslash d\{3\}\textbackslash )) \textbackslash d\{3\}-\textbackslash d\{4\}}
\item We must factor out the space as well as the area code:
  \re{(\textbackslash d\{3\} |\textbackslash (\textbackslash d\{3\}\textbackslash ) )?\textbackslash d\{3\}-\textbackslash d\{4\}}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Most operating systems come with file that has a list of words, 
for spell-checking, etc.
For instance, on Linux it may be at \str{/usr/share/dict/words}.
%  but
% in any event you can find it by running
% \str{locate words | grep dict}.
Use that file to find how many words fit the criteria.
\begin{exparts*}
\item contains the letter \str{a}
\item starts with~\str{A}
\item contains \str{a} or~\str{A}
\item contains \str{X}
\item contains \str{x} or~\str{X}
\item contains the string \str{st}
\item contains the string \str{ing}
\item contains an~\str{a}, and later a~\str{b}
\item contains none of the usual vowels \str{a}, \str{e}, \str{i}, \str{o}
  or~\str{u}
\item contains all the usual vowels
\item contains all the usual vowels, in ascending order
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a regex to accept time in a 24~hour format.
It should match times of the form `hh:mm:ss.sss' or
`hh:mm:ss' or `hh:mm' or `hh'.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textbackslash d\{2\}:\textbackslash d\{2\}:\textbackslash d\{2\}(.\textbackslash d +)|\textbackslash d\{2\}(:\textbackslash d\{2\})?}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Give an extended regular expression
% for ISO 8601 hours, minutes, seconds (with optional time zone).
% You can find an exact description on the web.
% \begin{answer}
% \re{\textasciicircum(2[0-3]|[01][0-9]):?([0-5][0-9]):?([0-5][0-9])(Z|[+-](?:2[0-3]|[01][0-9])(?::?(?:[0-5][0-9]))?)\$}
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a regex describing a floating point number.
\begin{answer}
One is \re{[-\textbackslash +]?(\textbackslash d)+\textbackslash .?|\textbackslash d)*\textbackslash .\textbackslash d+)}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a suitable regex.
\begin{exparts*}
\item All Visa card numbers start with a \str{4}.
New cards have 16 digits. Old cards have 13.
\item MasterCard numbers either start with \str{51} through \str{55},
or with the numbers \str{2221} through \str{2720}.
All have 16 digits.
\item American Express card numbers start with \str{34}
  or~\str{37} and have 15 digits. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{4(\textbackslash d{15}|\textbackslash d{12})}
\item \re{(5[1-5]\textbackslash d{2}|222[1-9]|22[3-9]\textbackslash d|2[3-6]\textbackslash d{2}|27[01]\textbackslash d|2720)\textbackslash d{12}}
\item \re{3[47][0-9]{13}}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Postal codes in the United Kingdom have six possible formats.
They are: (i)~\str{A11 1AA},
(ii)~\str{A1 1AA},
(iii)~\str{A1A 1AA},
(iv)~\str{AA11 1AA},
(v)~\str{AA1 1AA},
and (vi)~\str{AA1A 1AA}, where
\str{A} stands for a capital ASCII letter and \str{1} stands for a digit.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give a regex.
\item Shorten it.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
We can do the six as alternatives.
\begin{center}
  \re{[A-Z]\textbackslash d{2} \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]\textbackslash d \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]\textbackslash d[A-Z] \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d{2} \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d[A-Z] \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}}
\end{center}
\item We can factor out the ending of \re{\d[A-Z]{2}}.
\begin{center}
  \re{[A-Z]\textbackslash d{2}|[A-Z]\textbackslash d|[A-Z]\textbackslash d[A-Z]|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d{2}|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d|[A-Z]{2}\textbackslash d[A-Z]) \textbackslash d[A-Z]{2}}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You are stuck on a crossword puzzle.
The first letter (of eight) is an \str{g},
the third is an \str{n}
and the seventh is an \str{i}.
You have access to a file that contains all English words, each on its own
line.
Give a suitable regex.
\begin{answer}\verified
A suitable regex is \re{textasciicircum g.n.\{3\}i.\textdollar} and a match is
`gynaecia', meaning the aggregate of carpels or pistils in a flower.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% Why not \re{\textbackslash s(\emptystring|am|pm)}, which
% factors out the whitespace?
% \begin{answer}
% Factoring out the whitespace would mean that times without \str{am} or \str{pm}
% must have a trailing whitespace.
% \end{answer}

\begin{exercise}
Imagine that you decide to avoid regexes but still want to do 
the sanity check for email addresses discussed above, of accepting the
string if and only it it consists of a nonempty string of characters,
followed by \str{@}, followed by a nonempty string of characters.
Implement that as a routine in your favorite language.
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} % https://stackoverflow.com/a/1723225
% Give a regex that matches no string.
% \begin{answer}
% This tries to match a character after the end of the string: \re{\$.}

% newline character and so this can match. 
% But that lies outside our scope.)
% \end{answer}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The Roman numerals from grade school use the letters I, V, X, L, C, D,
and~M to represent 1, 5, 10, 50, 100, 500, and 1000.
They are written in descending order of magnitude, from M to~I, and are
written greedily so that we don't write six I's but rather VI.
Thus, the date written on the book held by the Statue of Liberty is 
MDCCLXXVI, for 1776.  
Further, we replace IIII with IV, and replace VIIII with IX.
Give a regular expression for valid Roman numerals less than 5000.
\begin{answer}
\re{\textasciicircum M{0,4}(CM|CD|D?C{0,3})(XC|XL|L?X{0,3})(IX|IV|V?I{0,3})\$}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:LangSquares}
\zcref{ex:LangSquares} says that 
$  \lang=
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}
         \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\tau\concat\tau$ for some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$}}$,
the language of square strings
over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$,
% \begin{equation*}
%   \lang=
%   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}
%          \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\tau\concat\tau$ for some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$}}
% \end{equation*}
is not regular.
Verify that.
\begin{answer}
Assume it is a regular language and let its pumping length be~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\str{b}$
It is an element of~$\lang$ whose length is greater than~$p$ so
By the Pumping Lemma it decomposes as $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$
subject to the three conditions.
By the first condition the length of $\alpha\concat\beta$ is less than~$p$,
and so these two substrings consist solely of~\str{a}'s.
The second condition is that $\beta$ is not the empty string.
The third condition says that the strings
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all members of~$\lang$.
Note that the first is not a square because in omitting~$\beta$ it omits at
least one~\str{a} from the prefix but not from the suffix.
That's a contradiction.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$\lang=\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat n>0}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Show that it is not regular.
\item Find a regex.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item We take the alphabet to be $\Sigma=\B$.
Assume that it is regular and let its pumping length be~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{0}^p1\str{0}^p$, which is a member of the language.
By the Pumping Lemma it therefore decomposes into $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$
subject to the three conditions.
By the first and second conditions $\alpha\beta$ consists solely of
\str{0}'s, and is therefore part of the prefix before the~\str{1},
and $\beta$ is nonempty.
By the third condition the string $\alpha\gamma$ is also a member of~$\lang$.
But this is a contradiction because the omission of $\beta$ means
that $\alpha\gamma$ has fewer \str{0}'s before the~\str{1} than after.
\item \re{(a*)b\textbackslash 1}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In \citetext{\definend{regex golf}}{See \url{https://alf.nu/RegexGolf},
and \url{https://nbviewer.jupyter.org/url/norvig.com/ipython/xkcd1313.ipynb}.}
you are given two lists and must produce a
regex that matches all the words in the first list but none of the
words in the second.
The `golf' aspect is that the person who finds the shortest regex, 
the one with the fewest characters, wins.
Try these: accept the words in the first list and not the words in the second.
\begin{exparts}
\item Accept: Arthur, Ester, le~Seur, Silverter

Do not accept: Bruble, Jones, Pappas, Trent, Zikle

\item Accept: alight, bright, kite, mite, tickle

Do not accept: buffing, curt, penny, tart

\item Accept: afoot, catfoot, dogfoot, fanfoot, foody, foolery, foolish,
fooster, footage, foothot, footle, footpad, footway, hotfoot, jawfoot,
mafoo, nonfood, padfoot, prefool, sfoot, unfool

Do not accept: Atlas, Aymoro, Iberic, Mahran, Ormazd, Silipan, altared,
chandoo, crenel, crooked, fardo, folksy, forest, hebamic, idgah, manlike,
marly, palazzi, sixfold, tarrock, unfold
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{.*r}
\item \re{.*i.*t.*}
\item \re{.*foo.*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\index{regular expression!in practice|)}



% \extra{XML} %  SEE https://www.w3.org/TR/xml/#sec-notation
% \begin{center} % https://xkcd.com/1144/
%   \includegraphics[scale=0.50]{automata/xkcdtags.png}
% \end{center}







\extra{The Myhill-Nerode theorem}\index{Myhill-Nerode theorem|(}\label{extra:MyhillNerode}
We introduced regular languages by defining them in terms of Finite State
machines.
Here we will give a characterization that instead goes directly to 
the properties of the language.

Recall that in this chapter's first section, \zcref{rem:HowToDesignFSM} said
that the key to designing Finite State machines is to think of each
state as about the future, as about the characters to come in the input string. 

\begin{definition}
  Fix a language $\lang$ over an alphabet $\Sigma$, along with 
  two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
  Then $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is a 
  \definend{distinguishing extension} when of
  $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$, one
  is an element of~$\lang$ and the other is not.
  If such an extension exists then the strings are 
  \definend{$\lang$-distinguishable},\index{L-distinguishable@$\lang$-distinguishable}
  otherwise they are
  \definend{$\lang$-indistinguishable}\index{L-indistinguishable@$\lang$-indistinguishable}
  or \definend{$\lang$-related},\index{L-related@$\lang$-related}
  denoted \definend{$\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:SimSubLangIsEquivalence}
  For any language~$\lang$,
  the binary relation $\sim_{\lang}$
  is an equivalence and hence partitions the universe of all strings
  into equivalence classes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\zcref{exer:VerifyEquivRels}, item~\ref{exer:SimSubLangIsEquivalence}.  
\end{proof}

\noindent
We denote the classes with \definend{$\eclass_{\lang,j}$}.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:MyhillNerodeExampleLangFiveClasses}
Let $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \lh{\sigma}=3}$.
Consider whether $\sigma_0=\str{aa}$ and $\sigma_1=\str{a}$
are distinguishable.\footnote{%
  Here $\protect\sigma_1=\protect\str{a}$ is not a character, 
  it is a length one string containing
  the character \protect\str{a}.
  We won't worry too much about the distinction.}
The string $\tau=\str{bb}$ is a distinguishing extension
because $\sigma_0\concat\tau=\str{aabb}\notin\lang$ while
$\sigma_1\concat\tau=\str{abb}\in\lang$.
In this way we can distinguish any length one string from any
length two string.

The equivalence classes are the collections of indistinguishable strings.
So the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes are:~the  
length zero strings, $\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring}$,
and the length one strings,
$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$,
and those of length two,
$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\set{\str{aa},\str{ab},\str{ba},\str{bb}}$,
and length three,
$\eclass_{\lang,3}=\set{\str{aaa},\str{aab},\ldots\,\str{bbb}}$,
along with the longer ones,
$\eclass_{\lang,4}=\set{\sigma\suchthat\lh{\sigma}\geq 4}$.
In the picture below the box is the universe of all strings 
$\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
It is partitioned into the equivalence classes, with every string 
a member of one and only one class. 
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa59.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:LangOfMNMachineComparePartitions}
  Consider
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has even length}}$.
  If a string $\sigma$ has even length then
  $\sigma\concat\tau\in\lang$ for an extension $\tau$ 
  if and only if $\tau$ has even length. 
  So there is no extension that distinguishes between two even-length strings.
  Similar reasoning holds for
  two odd-length strings.
  Thus the $\sim_{\lang}$ relation breaks 
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
  into two parts,
  $\eclass_{\lang,0} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\lh{\sigma}$ is even}}$
  and
  $\eclass_{\lang,1} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\lh{\sigma}$ is odd}}$.
  This shows the partition of the universe
  $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\!$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa61.pdf}
  \end{center}
\end{example}

So far the languages have finitely many equivalence classes.
Some languages have infinitely many.

\begin{example}
Consider
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n\str{b}^n$ for some $n\in\N$}}$
from \zcref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}.
To show that the number of classes is infinite 
we don't need to produce them all.
It is enough to
show that there exist infinitely many unequal classes,
or for that matter, infinitely many mutually distinguishable strings.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{\lang,i_0}=\set{\emptystring}
  \quad
  \eclass_{\lang,i_1}=\set{\str{a}}
  \quad
  \eclass_{\lang,i_2}=\set{\str{aa}}
  \quad
  \eclass_{\lang,i_3}=\set{\str{aaa}}
  \quad
  \ldots
\end{equation*}
To verify that these singleton sets are equivalence classes, 
let $\sigma=\str{a}^n$ for $n\in\N$.
Because $\sigma\concat\str{b}^n\in\lang$,
the only candidates
for strings~$\alpha$ that are $\lang$-indistinguishable from $\sigma$
but unequal to it are ones where $\alpha\concat\str{b}^n\in\lang$.
So they have the form $\alpha=\str{a}^{n+j}\str{b}^{j}$ with $j>0$.
However, an extension 
distinguishing $\sigma=\str{a}^n$ from $\alpha=\str{a}^{n+j}\str{b}^j$
is $\tau=\str{a}\str{b}^{n+1}$ because
$\sigma\concat\tau=\str{a}^{n+1}\str{b}^{n+1}\in\lang$ but
$\alpha\concat\tau=\str{a}^{n+j}\str{b}^j\str{a}\str{b}^{n+1}\notin\lang$.
\end{example}

We next make a connection between the machines that recognize 
a language~$\lang$ and the relationship of
of $\lang$-indistinguishability.

\begin{example} \label{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions}
This machine recognizes 
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has even length}}$,
the language of \zcref{ex:LangOfMNMachineComparePartitions}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa19.pdf}
\end{center}
In contrast to that example's partition of all strings
that arose from the language, we will now get a partition
of the strings by starting with the machine.
Consider all string inputs,
not just the accepted ones,
and see where they bring the machine.
\begin{center}  \small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{0.85\tabcolsep}
  \begin{tabular}{r|*{13}{c}}
    \tabulartext{Input string $\sigma$}
    &$\emptystring$ &\str{a} &\str{b}
      &\str{aa} &\str{ab} &\str{ba} &\str{bb}
      &\str{aaa} &\str{aab} &\str{aba} &\str{abb} &$\ldots$        \\
    \cline{2-13}
    \tabulartext{Ending state $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$}
    &$q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$
      &$q_3$ &$q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_4$
      &$q_1$ &$q_1$ &$q_1$ &$q_1$ &$\ldots$                \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
The set of input strings breaks into five parts,
those that bring the machine to~$q_0$,
those that bring it to~$q_1$, etc.
% This is another kind of partition, which
% will prove to be related to but different than the partition above.
Denote the classes of this partition with 
$\eclass_{\FSM,i}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_i}$.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{l}
    $\eclass_{\FSM,0}=\set{\varepsilon}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,1}=\set{\str{a}, \str{aaa},\,\ldots}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,2}=\set{\str{b}, \str{baa},\,\ldots}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,3}=\set{\str{aa}, \str{ab},\,\ldots}$
    \\
    $\eclass_{\FSM,4}=\set{\str{ba}, \str{bb},\,\ldots}$
  \end{tabular}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa62.pdf}}
\end{center}
Below we have overlain the language-related partition,
with two parts, on
the machine-related one with five.
\begin{equation*}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa20.pdf}}
    \tag{$*$}\label{ex:MNMachineComparePartitionsGraphic}
\end{equation*}
The $\FSM$-related parts are subsets of the $\lang$-related parts.
That is, the $\FSM$-related partition is finer than the
$\lang$-related partition.\footnote{%
  `Finer' in the sense that sand is finer than gravel.}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
  Let $\FSM$ be a Finite State machine with alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  Two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ are
  \definend{$\FSM$-related}\index{M-related@$\FSM$-related} 
  if $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$, 
  that is, if starting the machine with input~$\sigma_0$
  ends with it in the same state as does starting the machine
  with input~$\sigma_1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:SimSubFSMIsEquivalence}
  The relation between two strings of being
  $\FSM$-related is an equivalence and so partitions $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
  into equivalence classes.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
See \zcref{exer:VerifyEquivRels}'s item~\ref{exer:SimSubFSMIsEquivalence}.  
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:MRelatedImpliesLRelated}
  Let $\FSM$ be a deterministic Finite State machine that recognizes~$\lang$.
  If two strings are $\FSM$-related then they are $\lang$-related.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume that $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ are  $\FSM$-related, so that  
starting $\FSM$ with input $\sigma_0$
causes it to end in the same state as does starting it with input~$\sigma_1$.
The machine is deterministic so this implies that for any suffix~$\tau$, 
starting $\FSM$ with the input $\sigma_0\concat\tau$
causes it to end in the same state as does starting it with
the input $\sigma_1\concat\tau$.
In particular, for all~$\tau$ the string $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ takes $\FSM$
to an accepting state if and only if $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ does.
Hence the two strings are $\lang$-related.  
\end{proof}

\zcref{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions}'s $\eclass_{\FSM,i}$ classes
reflect the machine $\FSM$ in the following sense.
In $\FSM$, if the machine is in~$q_1$ and sees an~\str{a} then it transitions
to~$q_3$
% Consider $q_1$'s class
% $\eclass_{\FSM,1}=\set{\str{a}, \str{aaa},\,\ldots}$
% and $q_3$'s class
% $\eclass_{\FSM,3}=\set{\str{aa}, \str{ab},\,\ldots}$.
% Just as when the machine is in~$q_1$ and reads an~\str{a} 
% then it transitions to~$q_3$,
So also if we choose any string in $q_1$'s class $\sigma\in\eclass_{\FSM,1}$,
and append an~\str{a} to it,
then $\sigma\concat\str{a}$ is an element of~$\eclass_{\FSM,3}$.
For example, choosing $\sigma=\str{a}\in\eclass_{\FSM,1}$ 
and appending \str{a} to it gives $\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\FSM,3}$.

This way of thinking of the $\FSM$~classes has them acting as a machine,
in that there are transitions among them, just as with a machine.
We  can extend to also think of 
the $\lang$~classes as constituting their own machine.
The prior paragraph suggests 
how to define the transitions in this new machine.

\begin{definition}  \label{def:LMachine}
Let $\lang$ be a language over $\Sigma$ and let the collection of
$\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes $\eclass_{\lang,i}$ be $E$.
The \definend{$\lang$-machine}
has states that are the classes. 
Its start state is the class containing~\emptystring.
A state is accepting if it contains any string from~$\lang$.
The transition operation
$\map{\Delta}{E\times\Sigma}{E}$
inputs $\eclass_{\lang,i}$ and $x\in\Sigma$.
To find the output, choose any $\sigma$ in the class and  
then $\sigma\concat x$ is an element of some $\eclass_{\lang,j}$.
Set $\Delta(\eclass_{\lang,i},x)=\eclass_{\lang,j}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example} \label{ex:LanguageEndingInb}
Let $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}} \suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{b}}}$.
These are the $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes
(verification is \zcref{exer:LanguageEndingInb}).
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{\lang,0}=\lang
  \qquad
  \eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{a}}}
\end{equation*}
For any string $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$, if we concatenate it with
the one-character string~\str{a}
then the result is a member of the class $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
Similarly, if $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$ then
$\sigma\concat\str{b}$ is in  $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
Here is the full table and the arrow diagram.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\ 
    \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate\,\eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\eclass_{\lang,0}  \\
    \eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\eclass_{\lang,0}  \\
  \end{array}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa76.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

Another example is that the machine for \zcref{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions}'s 
two-class language is the two-state machine in  
(\ref{fig:MMachineCollapsesToLMachine}) below.

To find the output of $\Delta(\eclass_{\lang,i},x)$ the definition 
starts with any input~$\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$ at all. 
We must establish that   
choosing different string representatives from the input class
does not result in different output classes.

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:MNPriorThm}
  Fix a language $\lang$.
  \begin{parenum}
  \item The transition operation is well-defined:~if two strings 
    $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ are $\lang$-related,
    $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$, then adjoining a common
    character~$x\in\Sigma$ gives strings that are also
    $\lang$-related,
    $(\sigma_0\concat x)\sim_{\lang}(\sigma_1\concat x)$.
    \label{lem:MNPriorThmTransitionWellDefined}
  \item If one member \label{lem:MNPriorThmLangCount}  
    of a class % $\sigma_0\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$
    is an element of~$\lang$ then every other member
    of that class % $\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$ 
    is also an element of~$\lang$.
  \item There is one and only
    one class containing the empty string.
  \end{parenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  For the first item, if $\sigma_0\concat x$ were not 
  $\lang$-related to $\sigma_1\concat x$ then the single-character string~$x$
  would be a distinguishing extension.
  But $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$ so they have no distinguishing extension.

  The second item is:~if $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$
  and $\sigma_0\in\lang$
  but $\sigma_1\notin\lang$ then they are distinguished by the empty string,
  which contradicts that they are $\lang$-related.

  The third item is trivial since for any string, empty or not,
  there is one and only one
  equivalence class containing that string. 
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:IfLMachineHasFinitelyManyStates}
If the $\lang$-machine has finitely many states then it 
is a Finite State machine.
Further, that machine recognizes $\lang$.  
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first sentence is trivial.
For the second, fix $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
By \zcref{def:LMachine}'s description of transitions,
starting the $\lang$-machine with $\sigma=s_0s_1\ldots\,s_{n-1}$ on the
tape causes it to transition first to the equivalence class containing the
one-character string $s_0$, then to the class containing the string $s_0s_1$,
etc., ending in the class containing~$\sigma$.
The lemma's item~\ref{lem:MNPriorThmLangCount} says that this is
an accepting class of the $\lang$-machine if and only if $\sigma\in\lang$.  
\end{proof}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:LMachineWithThreeClasses}
Let 
$\lang=\set{\str{a}\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}=\set{\str{a},\str{ab},\str{abb},\,\ldots}$.  
We will find the equivalence classes and then
determine the $\lang$-machine's transitions.

There are three classes.
First, $\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring}$ because for any nonempty 
$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\!$, a distinguishing extension 
between  $\emptystring$ and $\sigma$ is the single-character string~\str{a}.

The second class is $\eclass_{\lang,1}=\lang$.
To see that any two elements of this set are 
$\lang$-related, take 
$\str{a}\str{b}^{i},\str{a}\str{b}^{j}\in\lang$ (with $i,j\in\N$).
Suppose that $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
If $\tau$ has the form $\str{b}^k$ for $k\in\N$ then both of
$\str{a}\str{b}^i\concat\tau$ and $\str{a}\str{b}^j\concat\tau$ 
are members of~$\lang$.
If $\tau$ has at least one~\str{a} then both are not members 
because they have at least two \str{a}'s.
It remains to show that if $\sigma_0\in\lang$ and $\sigma_1\notin\lang$ then
they are not $\lang$-related.
That's because
they have a distinguishing extension of~\emptystring.

The final class contains all remaining strings, 
$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-(\eclass_{\lang,0}\union\eclass_{\lang,1})$.
We will be done if we show that any two elements of this set are $\lang$-related
(there is no need to argue that elements of $\eclass_{\lang,2}$
are not equivalent to  
strings outside of the set because we have already shown that in the 
prior paragraphs).
A string~$\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$ must have at least one character
and must conform to at least one of two cases:~either its 
first item is not~\str{a},
or the rest of it contains at least one~\str{a}.
In both cases for any extension $\tau$ the string $\sigma\concat\tau$
is not an element of~$\lang$, and therefore
there are no distinguishing extensions.

In summary, the universe of all strings is partitioned by
$\sim_{\lang}$ into these classes.
\begin{equation*}
  % \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa64.pdf}}
    \eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring}  \quad
    \eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\str{a},\str{ab},\str{abb},\ldots}  \quad
    \eclass_{\lang,2}=\set{\str{b},\str{aa},\str{ba},\str{bb},\str{aaa},\str{aba},\ldots}  
\end{equation*}
To compute the transitions, for each class choose one representative element,
append in turn each of \str{a} and \str{b},
and find the resulting classes.
For instance, 
as representative for $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ we can choose the one-character string 
$\str{a}$.
Then we calculate the table's second line with
$\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$ and $\str{ab}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\ 
    \cline{2-3}
    \eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\eclass_{\lang,2}  \\
    \acceptingstate\eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\eclass_{\lang,2}  &\eclass_{\lang,1}  \\
    \eclass_{\lang,2}
       &\eclass_{\lang,2}  &\eclass_{\lang,2}  
  \end{array}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa65.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[Myhill-Nerode]
A language~$\lang$ is regular if and only if the relation
$\sim_{\lang}$ gives rise to only finitely many equivalence classes,
so that the $\lang$-machine is a Finite State machine.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose that $\lang$ is a regular language,
recognized by some Finite State machine $\FSM$.
The number of elements in the partition
associated with the machine-related equivalence $\sim_{\FSM}$ is finite,
as there is one part for each of $\FSM$'s reachable states.
Consequently the number of element in the partition induced by
the language-related equivalence $\sim_{\lang}$ is finite
because 
by \zcref{lem:MRelatedImpliesLRelated}, the number of classes in
$\sim_{\lang}$'s partition 
is at most the number of classes in $\sim_{\FSM}$'s partition. 

For the other direction
let the number of equivalence classes in the partition
induced by $\sim_{\lang}$ be finite.
By \zcref{cor:IfLMachineHasFinitelyManyStates} 
the $\lang$-machine is a Finite State machine that
recognizes~$\lang$.
Since there is a Finite Sate machine recognizing it, $\lang$ is regular.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The  $\lang$-machine is minimal}
Consider again \zcref{ex:MNMachineComparePartitions}'s
diagram~(\ref{ex:MNMachineComparePartitionsGraphic}).
The $\lang$~classes are collections of $\FSM$~classes  
so we can think that the $\lang$ classes come from
collapsing together the $\FSM$ classes.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa62.pdf}}
  \qquad\text{$\Longrightarrow$}\qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa77.pdf}}  
\end{equation*}
And, collapsing~$\FSM$ in that way gives
the $\lang$-machine\footnote{%
  The collapse is a function~$h$ that inputs $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes
  and outputs $\sim_{\lang}$~classes.
  The picture shows it mapping
  $h(\eclass_{\FSM,0})=h(\eclass_{\FSM,3})=h(\eclass_{\FSM,4})
  =\eclass_{\lang,0}$
  and also
    $h(\eclass_{\FSM,1})=h(\eclass_{\FSM,2})
  =\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
  Machines include transitions in addition to states so
  collapsing the machine requires that $h$ respects the
  next-state function, meaning that
  $h(\Delta_{\FSM}(\eclass_{\FSM,i},x))$ equals
  $\Delta_{\lang}(h(\eclass_{\FSM,i}),x)$.
}
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa19.pdf}}
  \qquad\text{$\Longrightarrow$}\qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa63.pdf}}
  \tag{$**$} \label{fig:MMachineCollapsesToLMachine}
\end{equation*}
that recognizes the same language, namely~$\lang$,
but does so with a minimal number of states.

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:LMachineIsMinimal}
If the language $\lang$ is regular then from among all the deterministic 
Finite State machines that recognize~$\lang$,
the $\lang$-machine has a minimal number of states.  
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\lang$ be recognized by the deterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
Consider two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
If those two take $\FSM$ to the same state, 
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$,
then appending any common extension does the same,
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0\concat\tau)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1\concat\tau)$.

The contrapositive statement is that
if the two strings have a distinguishing extension, 
$\sigma_0\not\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$, then starting $\FSM$ in those two does not
end in the same state,
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)\neq\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$.
Suppose that there are $k$-many $\sim_{\lang}$ classes $\eclass_{\lang,i}$. 
Then there must be $k$-many strings $\sigma_0,\ldots\,\sigma_{k-1}$
that end in different states,
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_i)\neq\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_j)$.
Hence any machine recognizing~$\lang$ must have at least that many 
different states.
But there exists a machine recognizing $\lang$ and having $k$ states,
namely the $\lang$-machine.
So $k$ is the minimal number of states that will do.
\end{proof}

To analyze a language with the Myhill-Nerode theorem, the challenge lies 
in finding the $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes.
One helpful point is that
\zcref{lem:MNPriorThm}'s item~\ref{lem:MNPriorThmLangCount}
says that 
if a class contains even one string from~$\lang$ 
then it consists entirely of such strings
(it may be that more than one class contains strings from~$\lang$).
Another point is that if the given language is recognized by some  
Finite State machine then with our experience often
we see how to build such an~$\FSM$.
If our $\FSM$ 
is minimal then by \zcref{lem:LMachineIsMinimal} 
the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes equal the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
Even if it is not minimal 
it can still give us insight into 
the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes, since
\zcref{lem:MRelatedImpliesLRelated} says that they
come from collapsing together a number of $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.

\begin{example} \label{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}
In this chapter's first section, \zcref{ex:ModuloFourFSM} discusses 
the language of bitstrings
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat\text{the number of \str{1}'s is a multiple of four}}$.

The $\sim_{\lang}$ relation partitions the set of
bitstrings into four equivalence classes.
The first consists of strings where
the number of \str{1}'s is evenly divisible by four,
$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\lang$.
Next is the class $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ consisting of bitstring
where the number of \str{1}'s leaves the remainder of~$1$,
and the other two are for remainders of $2$ and~$3$.
\zcref{exer:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour} asks for the proof of that but
here is the intuition.
Choose a class and two strings from it, for instance
$\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
To both, concatenate some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
Because $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ are in $\eclass_{\lang,1}$,
their number of \str{1}'s leave a remainder of~$1$ 
on division by~$4$.
Thus $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are members
of $\lang$ if and only if the number of \str{1}'s in $\tau$
leaves a remainder of~$3$,
and so they are indistinguishable.

To find the transitions in the $\lang$-machine, 
choose a representative element from each class,
append in turn each character of the alphabet,
and find the resulting class.
Thus, from $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ we can choose~$\emptystring$, and appending the
one-character string \str{0} gives $\str{0}\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$
while appending \str{1} gives $\str{1}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
For the next line we can choose the representative
$\str{1}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$, and
appending \str{0} gives $\str{10}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$ while
\str{1} gives $\str{11}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
The rest of the table works the same way.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\ 
     \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate\eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\eclass_{\lang,0}  &\eclass_{\lang,1}  \\
    \eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\eclass_{\lang,2}  \\
    \eclass_{\lang,2}
       &\eclass_{\lang,2}  &\eclass_{\lang,3} \\ 
    \eclass_{\lang,3}
       &\eclass_{\lang,3}  &\eclass_{\lang,0}  
  \end{array}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa66.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
\citetext{This machine is essentially the same as \zcref{ex:ModuloFourFSM}'s}{%
  The two machines are said to be `isomorphic'.}.
\end{example}

\medskip
\begin{wrapfigure}{l}[3em]{0.33\textwidth}  % "l" for left side so they look into page
  % \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.16\textwidth]{automata/pix/myhill.png}% 
  % \vspace*{1ex}
  \hspace*{1em}
  \includegraphics[width=0.14\textwidth]{automata/pix/nerode.png}% 
  \caption{\protect\citetext{\label{fig:myhill}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Myhill}{John Myhill Sr\ 1923--1987} and \label{fig:nerode}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anil_Nerode}{Anil Nerode b~1932}}{Photo credits Paul Halmos, Jason Koski/Cornell University}}\index{Myhill, J!picture}\index{Nerode, A!picture}
\end{wrapfigure} % Photo credits Paul Halmos, Jason Koski/Cornell University
We close with a reflection.
At the chapter's start we constructed machines by hand, 
using what ideas we could muster.
In contrast, the Myhill-Nerode result
starts with the desired language~$\lang$, 
considers the relation $\sim_{\lang}$, and
if there are finitely many associated classes 
then gives a Finite State machine
that recognizes~$\lang$.

That is,
by seeing the patterns inside~$\lang$ given by $\sim_{\lang}$, 
we don't have to rely on cleverness to make a machine recognizing that language, 
the mathematics gives it to us.
And for free, this machine is minimal.
This is a deep kind of wizardry\Dash these problems are in some sense
solved automatically.
