\begin{exercises}
% https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/89689/78536

% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/136195/50343

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Use the machine from \zcref{ex:LMachineWithThreeClasses}.
\begin{exparts*}
\item What class contains the string \str{bba}? 
\item \str{abba}?
\item \str{babab}?  
\item \str{abbbb}?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
Every string in the language 
$\lang=\set{\str{a}\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}=\set{\str{a},\str{ab},\str{abb},\,\ldots}$
is a member of $\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
Of the other strings,
the only member of $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ is \emptystring{}
and all other strings are in $\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\str{bba}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$
\item $\str{abb}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$
\item $\str{babab}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$
\item $\str{abbbb}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Use the $\lang$-machine from \zcref{ex:LMachineWithThreeClasses}.
For each string, identify the string's class, and 
then append the character~\str{a} and identify the ending class for the 
machine's transition.
\begin{exparts*}
\item \str{bba}
\item \emptystring
\item \str{abbb}
\item \str{a} 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The starting string's class is $\str{bba}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
  After appending to get
  $\str{bba}\concat\str{a}=\str{bbaa}$ we have $\str{bbaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
  This fits with the lower-left entry in the $\Delta$ table given in 
  \zcref{ex:LMachineWithThreeClasses} (that is, with the bottom entry in
  the column headed `\str{a}').
\item The input class is $\emptystring\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
  Appending gives 
  $\emptystring\concat\str{a}=\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
  This fits the upper-left entry in the $\Delta$ table.
\item The starting class is $\str{abbb}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
  Appending results in $\str{abbba}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$,
  which matches the middle-left entry in the transition table.
\item The starting class is $\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
  Appending results in $\str{aaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$,
  which fits the table's bottom-left entry.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}\label{exer:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}
\zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour} considers the language of
bitstrings where the number of \str{1}'s is a multiple of~$4$.
It asserts that there are four $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes, where
$\eclass_{\lang,i}$ is the set of bitstrings where the number of
\str{1}'s leaves remainder $i$ on division by $4$.
Prove that.
\begin{answer}\verified
We will follow the intuition given in the example.
Fix some $i\in\set{0,1,2,3}$.
We first show that if $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$
then they are indistinguishable.
Consider a bitstring $\tau$ and let $k\in\N$ be the number of
\str{1}'s in $\tau$.
Then the number of \str{1}'s in $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ is the remainder on
division by four of $i+k$, and that is the same as the number of \str{1}'s in
$\sigma_1\concat\tau$.
Thus $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$ if and only if
$\sigma_1\concat\tau\in\lang$.

We finish by showing that if $\sigma_0\in\eclass_{\lang,i}$
and $\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,j}$ with $j\neq i$ then they are distinguishable. 
If $\tau=\str{1}^{4-i}$
then because $i\neq j$ we have $\sigma_0\in\lang$ but $\sigma_1\notin\lang$,
so they are distinguishable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
This illustrates the point about `well-defined' in 
\zcref{lem:MNPriorThm}'s part~\ref{lem:MNPriorThmTransitionWellDefined}.
Consider the $\lang$-machine of \zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}.  
\begin{exparts}
\item
Three representative strings from $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ are
$\sigma_0=\str{00}$,
$\sigma_1=\str{11011}$,
and $\sigma_2=\str{00}\str{11111111}=\str{00}\str{1}^8\!$.
Append \str{0} to each and name the class of the resulting string.
Verify that all three lead to a single class and that
in the machine the state $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ transitions
on input \str{0} to that class.
\item
Using the same three strings,
to each append \str{1} and name the class of the resulting string.  
Verify that the three lead to the same class, and that
in the machine the state $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ transitions
on input \str{1} to it.
\item 
Repeat that for three strings from $\eclass_{\lang,1}$, with both
\str{0} and~\str{1}.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Appending to the three strings gives  
$\sigma_0\concat\str{0}=\str{000}$,
$\sigma_1\concat\str{0}=\str{110110}$,
and $\sigma_2\concat\str{0}=\str{00}\str{1}^8\str{0}$.
All three are members of~$\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
Looking at the machine's transition table, 
that is indeed the result of starting with the class
$\eclass_{\lang,0}$ and transitioning on input~\str{0}.
\item Appending gives  
$\sigma_0\concat\str{1}=\str{001}$,
$\sigma_1\concat\str{1}=\str{110111}$,
and $\sigma_2\concat\str{1}=\str{00}\str{1}^8\str{1}$.
All three are members of~$\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
The machine's transition table shows this as 
the result of starting with the class
$\eclass_{\lang,0}$ and transitioning on input~\str{1}.
\item Of course, many triples of strings are possible but
for illustration we can give 
$\sigma_0=\str{100}$,
and $\sigma_1=\str{010}$,
and $\sigma_2=\str{111101}$.

Concatenating \str{0} gives
$\sigma_0\concat\str{0}=\str{1000}$,
and $\sigma_1\concat\str{0}=\str{0100}$,
and $\sigma_2\concat\str{0}=\str{1111010}$.
All three are members of~$\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
The machine's transition table shows that $\eclass_{\lang,2}$ 
is indeed the result of starting with the class
$\eclass_{\lang,1}$ and transitioning on input~\str{0}.

As to concatenating \str{1}, we get
$\sigma_0\concat\str{1}=\str{1001}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$,
$\sigma_1\concat\str{1}=\str{0101}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$,
and $\sigma_2\concat\str{1}=\str{1111011}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}$. 
The machine's transition table agrees, showing that 
the result of starting with the class
$\eclass_{\lang,1}$ and transitioning on input~\str{1}
is $\eclass_{\lang,2}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:LangOfMNMachineComparePartitions} gives a language  
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sizeof{\sigma}$ is even}}$
with two classes.
Produce the transition table and arrow diagram for the associated 
$\lang$-machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
That example gives the classes as
$\eclass_{\lang,0} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\lh{\sigma}$ is even}}$
and
$\eclass_{\lang,1} =\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\lh{\sigma}$ is odd}}$.
We can pick any string from a class to represent that class.
As a representative of $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ we can use 
the length zero string \emptystring. 
As a representative of $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ we can use the length one 
string~\str{a}. 
The only accepting state is $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ because it contains all
of the strings from~$\lang$.
It is also the start state because it contains the empty string.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\ 
    \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate\,\emptystring\in\eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\str{b}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  \\
    \str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,0}  &\str{ab}\in\eclass_{\lang,0}  \\
  \end{array}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa67.pdf}}
\end{equation*}  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \recommended
\begin{exercise}
\zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeExampleLangFiveClasses} gives a language  
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \lh{\sigma}=3}$
with five classes.
Produce the $\lang$-machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
The classes are
$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring}$,
and
$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \lh{\sigma}=1}=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$,
and
$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \lh{\sigma}=2}=\set{\str{aa},\str{ab},\str{ba},\str{bb}}$,
and
$\eclass_{\lang,3}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \lh{\sigma}=3}$,
along with
$\eclass_{\lang,4}=\set{\sigma\suchthat\lh{\sigma}\geq 4}$.
To determine the transitions, from each class we can
pick any string member.
As a representative of $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ we must use \emptystring. 
To represent $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ we can use the length one 
string~\str{a}. 
To represent $\eclass_{\lang,2}$ we can pick~\str{aa},
to represent $\eclass_{\lang,3}$ we can use~\str{aaa},
and to represent $\eclass_{\lang,4}$ we can take~\str{aaaa}. 
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\ 
    \cline{2-3}
    \emptystring\in\eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\str{b}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  \\
    \str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}  &\str{ab}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}  \\
    \str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,2}
       &\str{aaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,3}  &\str{aab}\in\eclass_{\lang,3}  \\
    \acceptingstate\,\str{aaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,3}
       &\str{aaaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,4}  &\str{aaab}\in\eclass_{\lang,4}  \\
    \str{aaaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,4}
       &\str{aaaaa}\in\eclass_{\lang,4}  &\str{aaaab}\in\eclass_{\lang,4}  \\
  \end{array}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa68.pdf}}
\end{equation*}  
Because it contains all
of the strings from~$\lang$, the sole accepting state is $\eclass_{\lang,3}$.
The start state is the class containing \emptystring, 
namely~$\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:LanguageEndingInb}
\zcref{ex:LanguageEndingInb} says that
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{b}}}$
has the $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes
$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\lang$
and
$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{a}}}$.
Verify that.
\begin{answer}\verified
We first verify that all of the strings in   
$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{b}}}$
are $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalent.
So fix $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
Take $\tau\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
and consider $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$.
There are two cases.
If $\tau\neq\emptystring$ then 
whether or not $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and
$\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are both elements of~$\lang$ is determined only
by the final character of $\tau$, so it doesn't matter what $\sigma_0$ and
$\sigma_1$ are, they play no role.
If $\tau=\emptystring$ then $\sigma_0\concat\tau=\sigma_0$ and
$\sigma_1\concat\tau=\sigma_1$ are both elements of~$\lang$.
In either case $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$ if and only if
$\sigma_1\concat\tau\in\lang$.

The same argument works for 
$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{a}}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Let $\lang$ be the set of strings from $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
ending in~\str{a}.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that $\lang$ is an equivalence class, $\eclass_{\lang,1}$, for 
the relation~$\sim_{\lang}$.
\item Show that the complement $\setcomp{\lang}$ is also
an equivalence class, $\eclass_{\lang,0}$,
and therefore there are exactly two classes.
\item What is the initial state of the $\lang$-machine?
\item What are the accepting states?
\item Give the transition table and the diagram.
\item Which of the strings~\emptystring,
\str{a}, \str{b}, \str{abba}, and \str{bba} are accepted by this machine?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
Observe that where $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\!$, 
if $\tau$ is nonempty then $\sigma\concat\tau$ is in the language
if and only if $\tau$ ends in~\str{a}.
\begin{exparts}
\item 
The observation implies that no pair $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\lang$ has a
distinguishing extension, since $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$
are elements of~$\lang$ just if either $\tau$ is empty or $\tau$ ends in~\str{a}.
In addition, no string $\sigma\in\lang$ is equivalent to a string 
$\alpha\notin\lang$ 
since $\sigma$ ends in~\str{a} and $\alpha$ does not, and therefore 
a distinguishing extension is \emptystring.     
\item 
The observation also implies, by the same reasoning, that 
the pair $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\setcomp{\lang}$ does not have a 
nonempty distinguishing extension. 
And, $\tau=\emptystring$ is also not a distinguishing extension. 
(We do not need to establish that no string in $\setcomp{\lang}$ is equivalent
to a string in~$\lang$ since we did that in the prior item.)
\item The initial state is the class containing the empty string.
In this machine,
the empty string is an element of $\eclass_{\lang,0}$.
\item A class is accepting if it contains a string from~$\lang$.
In this machine,
that is only the class $\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
\item As class representatives we can choose $\emptystring\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$
and the length~one string $\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|rr}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\Delta}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\ 
    \cline{2-3}
    \emptystring\in\eclass_{\lang,0}
       &\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\str{b}\in\eclass_{\lang,0}  \\
    \acceptingstate\,\str{a}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}
       &\str{aa}\in\eclass_{\lang,1}  &\str{ab}\in\eclass_{\lang,0}  \\
  \end{array}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa69.pdf}}
\end{equation*} 
The accepted strings are those that end in \str{a}, namely
\str{a}, \str{abba}, and~\str{bba}. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For the language
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^2\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
with alphabet $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ this is a minimal
Finite State machine.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa72.pdf}}
\end{center}
List the $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes.
\begin{answer}\verified
We follow the discussion before \zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}, 
that where the
machine is minimal the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes are the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
As to finding the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes, these four implications are clear.
\begin{align*}
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \sigma=\emptystring           \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_1
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \sigma=\str{a}           \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_2
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{aab*}}    \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_3
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ is otherwise (specifically, $\sigma$ matches \re{b|ab|(aab*a(a|b)*)})}
\end{align*}
Consequently, these are the four $\sim_{\lang}$ classes.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{c|l} 
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Associated with}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Class}}  \\ \hline
    $q_0$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\emptystring}$  \\
    $q_1$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\str{a}}$  \\
    $q_2$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\lang$  \\
    $q_3$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,3}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma\notin(\eclass_{\lang,0}\union\eclass_{\lang,1}\union\eclass_{\lang,2})$}}$  
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
The class $\eclass_{\lang,2}$ contains strings from $\lang$ 
so it is a final state.
The class $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ contains the empty string so it is the initial 
state.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The language $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$ is regular.
Find the $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes.
The alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
We follow the discussion before \zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}, 
that where the
machine is minimal the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes are the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
Here is the natural Finite State machine to recognize this language.  
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa71.pdf}}
\end{display}
It is clearly minimal, so we find the 
the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
These implications are straightforward.
\begin{align*}
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{a*}}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_1
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{a*b}}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_2
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ is otherwise (specifically, it matches \re{a*b(a|b)(a|b)*})}  
\end{align*}
These are the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{c|l} 
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Associated with}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Class}}  \\ \hline
    $q_0$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$  \\
    $q_1$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n\str{b}$ for $n\in\N$}}$  \\
    $q_2$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n\str{b}\concat\beta$ for $n\in\N$ and $\beta\neq\emptystring$}}$  
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
The class $\eclass_{\lang,1}$ contains strings from $\lang$ 
so it is a final state.
The class $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ contains the empty string so it is the initial 
state.

\textit{Comment:}~while the Finite State machine given certainly appears 
minimal, strictly speaking we have not shown it to be minimal.
We could show that using Extra~\ref{sec:Minimization}.
% We can verify that these are the equivalence classes
% without relying on minimality as follows.

% Consider first the collection~$\lang$, which we can call $\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
% Suppose that $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\lang$.
% Then for any string~$\tau$ we have that $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and 
% $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are elements of~$\lang$ if and only if $\tau$ is the
% empty string.
% So they are equivalent.
% To show that any such string is not equivalent to some $\sigma_2\notin\lang$,
% note that a distinguishing extension is the empty string. 

% Next consider the collection of strings of the form $\sigma=\str{a}^n\!$, which
% we will call $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ since it contains the empty string.
% For any extension~$\tau$, the string $\sigma\concat\tau$ is in the 
% language if and only if $\tau$ has the form $\str{a}^i\str{b}$ for some 
% $i\in\N$.
% So any two elements of $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ are equivalent.

% To show that any $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$ is not equivalent to a string 
% $\alpha\notin(\eclass_{\lang,0}\union\eclass_{\lang,1})$,
% note that $\alpha$ has the form $\str{a}^n\str{b}\concat\beta$ for some nonempty
% string~$\beta$ (in the above Finite State machine it is associated with $q_2$).
% In particular, $\alpha$ contains at least one~\str{b}.
% Hence for every extension~$\tau$ we have $\alpha\concat\tau\notin\lang$.
% It follows that any $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$ is distinguished from 
% such an $\alpha$
% with the one-character string $\tau=\str{b}$.

% Finally, consider 
% $\eclass_{\lang,2}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-(\eclass_{\lang,0}\union\eclass_{\lang,1})$.
% As covered in the prior paragraph,
% every string~$\sigma$ in this collection 
% has the form $\str{a}^n\str{b}\concat\beta$ for some 
% string~$\beta$, and therefore there is no extension~$\tau$ with
% $\sigma\concat\tau\in\lang$.
% Thus there is no extension that distinguishes betwee two members of this
% set, and so they are all equivalent.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find the $\lang$~equivalence classes for the language strings 
that end in \str{01}.
The alphabet is $\B$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Approach the problem by using \zcref{lem:LMachineIsMinimal},
building a minimal Finite State machine for the 
language and using the $\FSM$ classes as the $\lang$~classes.
\item Instead directly show that the $\lang$~class sets are 
equivalence classes.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item    
  This is a regular language and the natural Finite State machine
to recognize it is below.
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa70.pdf}}
\end{display}
We take this machine to be minimal.
(We could verify that using Extra~\ref{sec:Minimization}.)
We follow the discussion before \zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}, 
that where the
machine is minimal, the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes are the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.

These implications are straightforward.
\begin{align*}
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_2
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma\in\lang$ (that is, it matches the regular expression \re{(0|1)*01})}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_1
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{0} (it matches \re{(0|1)*0})}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ is otherwise}  
\end{align*}
These are the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{c|l} 
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Associated with}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Class}}  \\ \hline
    $q_2$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\lang$   \\   
    $q_1$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{0}}}$  \\
    $q_0$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}-(\eclass_{\lang,2}\union\eclass_{\lang,1})}$  
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
The class $\eclass_{\lang,2}$ is the only one containing strings from $\lang$ 
so it is the only final state.
The class $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ contains the empty string so it is the initial 
state.
\item
We are looking at three sets as candidates for the equivalence classes.
We are thinking that one of them, here named~$\eclass_{\lang,2}$, is $\lang$.
Another, here named~$\eclass_{\lang,1}$, holds those strings that end in~\str{0},
and the final set must contain all of the other strings,
$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-(\eclass_{\lang,1}\union\eclass_{\lang,2})$.

First we argue that $\eclass_{\lang,2}=\lang$ is a $\sim_{\lang}$
equivalence class.
Assume that $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\lang$, aiming to show that 
$\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$. 
Both strings end in \str{01}.
Consider an extension string~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}\!$.
If $\tau=\emptystring$ then $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$
and $\sigma_i\concat\tau\in\lang$.
If $\tau\neq\emptystring$ then $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and
and $\sigma_i\concat\tau$ are both elements of $\lang$ if and only if
$\tau$ ends in \str{01}. 
In both of those two cases the pair have no distinguishing extension.

We finish showing that this set is an equivalence class by showing that 
no string $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,2}=\lang$ is $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalent
to a string $\alpha\in\setcomp{\lang}\!$.
This holds because \emptystring{} distinguishes between
$\sigma$ and~$\alpha$.

Next we argue that the set of strings ending in~\str{0} is an
equivalence class.
Consider two such strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$ 
along with an extension~$\tau$.
If $\tau=\emptystring$ then both $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$
are not elements of~$\lang$.
If $\tau$ has length one then both $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ 
and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are elements of~$\lang$ if and only if 
$\tau=\str{1}$.
If $\tau$ has length greater than one then both $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ 
and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are elements of~$\lang$ if and only if 
$\tau$ ends in \str{01}.
In all of those three cases $\tau$ does not distinguish between
$\sigma_0$ and~$\sigma_1$.

To finish showing that this is an equivalence class, we argue that no
$\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,1}$ is $\sim_{\lang}$ indistinguishable from 
some $\alpha\notin\eclass_{\lang,1}$.
The argument is:~no string ending in~\str{0} is equivalent to a string
not ending in~\str{0} because a distinguishing extension is $\str{1}$.

Finally, consider the remaining strings, those that do not end with~\str{01}
and do not end with~\str{0}.
For any such $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$, the extension $\sigma\concat\tau$
is in the language if and only if $\tau$ ends with \str{01}.
So there is no extension that distinguishes between a pair of such strings, 
and they are mutually equivalent. 

There is no need to show that a $\sigma\in\eclass_{\lang,0}$ cannot be 
equivalent to an $\alpha\notin\eclass_{\lang,0}$ because we have already
done so for the earlier two classes.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Describe the $\lang$~equivalence classes
for the set of strings where every \str{0} is immediately followed by two
\str{1}'s.
The alphabet is $\B$.
\begin{answer}\verified
We follow the discussion before \zcref{ex:MyhillNerodeNumOnesMultOfFour}, 
that where the
machine is minimal, the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes are the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
Here is the natural Finite State machine to recognize the language.  
\begin{display}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa73.pdf}}
\end{display}
It is clearly minimal, which means that 
the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes equal the $\sim_{\FSM}$ classes.
These implications are straightforward.
\begin{align*}
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{(1|011)*}}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_1
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{(1|011)*0}}      \\
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_2
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{(1|011)*01}}     \\ 
  &\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=q_3
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \text{$\sigma$ is otherwise; it does not match \re{(1|011)*(\emptystring|0|01)}}  
\end{align*}
These are the $\sim_{\lang}$ classes.
\begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{c|l} 
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Associated with}} 
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Class}}  \\ \hline
    $q_0$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,0}=\lang$  \\
    $q_1$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,1}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{(1|011)*0}}}$  \\
    $q_2$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,2}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ matches \re{(1|011)*01}}}$      \\ 
    $q_3$ 
                      &$\eclass_{\lang,3}=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma} \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ does not match \re{(1|011)*(\emptystring|0|01)}}}$  
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
The class $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ is the only accepting class because it is the only
one containing strings from $\lang$.
The class $\eclass_{\lang,0}$ also contains the empty string so it is the initial 
class.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In all of the $\lang$-machines in the examples, the language is a
single equivalence class.
Produce a language~$\lang$ that is divided by the $\sim_{\lang}$ 
relation into more than one equivalence class.
\begin{answer}\verified
There are of course many possible answers.
A reasonable approach is to
think of a regular language where to to make the Finite State machine there
are two or more natural accepting states.    

Consider $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$ containing
all strings that end in~\str{a}, and also all strings of length two.
Then the strings \str{a} and \str{bb} are both members of~$\lang$
but they have a distinguishing extension $\tau=\str{b}$,
so the set $\lang$ splits into at least two equivalence classes.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The language of palindromes  
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\str{a},\str{b}}\suchthat \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
is not regular.
Find infinitely many $\lang$~equivalence classes.
\begin{answer}\verified
We will show there are infinitely many $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes
by showing that there are infinitely many non-equivalent strings.

We claim that no two unequal strings of the form $\str{a}^n$
are equivalent.
As an example consider $\sigma_2=\str{aa}$ and $\sigma_3=\str{aaa}$.
Take $\tau=\str{baa}$.
Because $\sigma_2\concat\tau\in\lang$ and $\sigma_3\concat\tau\notin\lang$,
the two are not equivalent.
Clearly this works for any two such strings.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The language $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\str{a},\str{b}}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has the same number of \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$
gives rise to infinitely many $\sim_{\lang}$ equivalence classes.
Prove that.
\begin{answer}
The classes are:~the set of strings with the same number of \str{a}'s
as \str{b}'s.
those with one more \str{a},
those with one more \str{b},
those with two more \str{a}'s, etc.
We don't have to prove that, we need only prove that there are infinitely
many distinguishable strings.

So let $n(\sigma)$ be the number of \str{a}'s minus the number of \str{b}'s.
That could be positive, negative, or zero.
Assume that $n(\sigma_0)\neq n(\sigma_0)$ for
$\sigma_0,\sigma_1\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$.
Then a distinguishing string is any 
$\tau\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$ where $n(\tau)=-1\cdot n(\sigma_0)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the language of strings $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$
where the number of \str{0}'s in $\sigma$ is the same as the number of \str{1}'s
is not regular
by using the Myhill-Nerode Theorem.
\begin{answer}\verified
We will show that there are infinitely many non-equivalent strings.
\begin{display}
  \emptystring
  \quad \str{0}
  \quad \str{00}
  \quad \str{000}
  \quad \ldots
\end{display}
If $i\neq j$ then $\str{0}^i\not\sim_{\lang}\str{0}^j$ since 
$\tau=\str{1}^i$ is a distinguishing extension.
That's true because $\str{0}^i\concat\str{1}^i$ is a member of~$\lang$
but $\str{0}^j\concat\str{1}^i$ is not.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the language of strings of the form
$\str{0}^{2^j}\!$, that is, 
$\lang=\set{\str{0},\str{0}^4,\str{0}^8,\ldots}$, 
is not regular.
\begin{answer}\verified
We will show that every string in the language is in its own $\sim_{\lang}$
equivalence class, and so there are infinitely many such classes.

Consider $\sigma_0=\str{0}^{2^n}$ and $\sigma_1=\str{0}^{2^m}$ where $n\neq m$.
One of them must be smaller;~suppose that $n<m$.
Then the extension string $\tau=\str{0}^{2^n}$ gives
$\sigma_0\concat\tau=\str{0}^{2^n}\concat\str{0}^{2^n}=\str{0}^{2\cdot 2^n}=\str{0}^{2^{n+1}}$ is an 
element of the language.
But because $m$ is greater than~$n$ the string 
$\sigma_1\concat\tau$ is not 
an element of the language (the number of \str{0}'s in the string
is $2^m+2^n$, which is strictly less than $2^{2(m+1)}$ because $n$ is strictly
less than~$m$).
So they have a distinguishing extension.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:VerifyEquivRels}
Recall that a binary relation~$\sim$ is an equivalence if it
has three properties:
\begin{parenum}
\item reflexivity, that $x\sim x$ for all~$x$,
\item symmetry, that if $x\sim y$ then $y\sim x$, and
\item transitivity, that if $x\sim y$ and $y\sim z$ then also $x\sim z$.   
\end{parenum}
\begin{exparts*}
\item \label{exer:SimSubLangIsEquivalence}
Verify \zcref{lem:SimSubLangIsEquivalence}.  
\item \label{exer:SimSubFSMIsEquivalence}
Verify \zcref{lem:SimSubFSMIsEquivalence}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
For both arguments write the language as $\lang$ and the alphabet
as~$\Sigma$.
\begin{exparts}
\item By definition, $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$ if there is no distinguishing
extension~$\tau$.
That holds if and only if either both of $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and 
$\sigma_1\concat\tau$ are members $\lang$ or both are not.

The relation $\sim_{\lang}$ is reflexive
because for any string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
either both of $\sigma\concat\tau$ and  $\sigma\concat\tau$ are in the 
language or both are not.

To verify that the relation is symmetric assume that $\sigma_0\sim\sigma_1$.
Then for any $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$,
either both of $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ 
are in the language or both are not.
By symmetry of the word `and', 
either both of $\sigma_1\concat\tau$ and $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and  
are in the language or both are not.
That is equivalent to the statement that $\sigma_1\sim\sigma_0$.

Finally, for transitivity assume that $\sigma_0\sim\sigma_1$ and 
$\sigma_1\sim\sigma_2$.
Let $\tau$ be a string.
Either $\sigma_1\concat\tau\in\lang$ or not.
If $\sigma_1\concat\tau\in\lang$ 
then $\sigma_0\sim\sigma_1$ implies that $\sigma_0\concat\tau\in\lang$,
and $\sigma_1\sim\sigma_2$ implies that $\sigma_2\concat\tau\in\lang$,
and therefore both of $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and  
$\sigma_2\concat\tau$ are members of the language.
Similarly if $\sigma_1\concat\tau\notin\lang$  
then both of  both of $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and  
$\sigma_2\concat\tau$ are not members of the language.
Consequently, $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_2$.
\item 
By definition, $\sigma_0\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_1$ if 
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$.
The relation $\sim_{\FSM}$ is reflexive
because
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$
for any string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.

To verify that the relation is symmetric, assume that 
$\sigma_0\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_1$.
Then $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$
and so by symmetry of equality, 
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)$.
Thus $\sigma_1\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_0$.

For transitivity assume that $\sigma_0\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_1$ and 
$\sigma_1\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_2$.
The first gives $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)$
while the second gives
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_1)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_2)$.
Therefore
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_0)=\extended{\Delta}(\sigma_2)$
and $\sigma_0\sim_{\FSM}\sigma_2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Generalize the first item in \zcref{lem:MNPriorThm} to:~~if two strings 
% $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$ are $\lang$-related,
% $\sigma_0\sim_{\lang}\sigma_1$, then adjoining any common
% extension~$\beta$ gives strings that are also $\lang$-related,
% $(\sigma_0\concat \beta)\sim_{\lang}(\sigma_1\concat \beta)$.
% \begin{answer}\verified
% Assume that
% $\sigma_0$ and $\sigma_1$ are $\lang$-related.
% Suppose otherwise, that there is a string $\beta$ such that
% $(\sigma_0\concat \beta)\not\sim_{\lang}(\sigma_1\concat \beta)$.
% Then there is an extension string $\tau$ such that 
% one of 
% $(\sigma_0\concat \beta)\concat \tau$ and
% $(\sigma_1\concat \beta)\concat \tau$ is in the language.
% But then $\beta\concat\tau$ is a distinguishing extension
% between $\sigma_0$ and~$\sigma_1$.
% So no such $\beta$ is possible.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the equivalence classes for the language~$\lang$ are the same as for
the language that is its complement, $\setcomp{\lang}\!$.  
\begin{answer}\verified
Two strings $\sigma_0,\sigma_1\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ 
are $\lang$-indistinguishable if
and only if for every extension~$\tau$, 
either both of $\sigma_0\concat\tau$ and  $\sigma_0\concat\tau$
are elements of $\lang$
or both of them are elements of $\setcomp{\lang}\!$.
That's equivalent to being  $\setcomp{\lang}$-indistinguishable.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{Myhill-Nerode theorem|)}










% ...................................................
\extra{Machine minimization}\index{minimization of a Finite State machine|(}\index{Finite State machine!minimization|(}\label{sec:Minimization}
Imagine that a person is tasked with ensuring that input for a password form 
contains both
upper and lower case ASCII characters, and they produce the machine on the left.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa74.pdf}}
  \quad    
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa75.pdf}}    
\end{center}
The machine on the right is better in that it has one fewer state. 
We will give an algorithm, \definend{Moore's algorithm},\index{Moore's algorithm}\index{minimization of a Finite State machine!Moore's algorithm}
% (or the table-filling algorithm),\index{table-filling algorithm}
that inputs a deterministic Finite State machine and outputs
a deterministic machine that is \definend{minimal}, 
that from among all of the machines recognizing the same
language has the fewest states.\footnote{%
  Moore's algorithm is easy to understand and is suitable for
  small calculations but there is another,
  \protect\citetext{Hopcroft's algorithm}{See \protect\autocite{Knuutila}}, 
  that is more efficient.}
  (The prior section, \protect\zcref{extra:MyhillNerode}, gives an algorithm
  that starts with a language and returns the minimal machine recognizing
  that language.
  Here we start with a machine and needn't work through the language.)

% This algorithm works by collapsing together redundant states
% so we begin with an example of those.
% This machine recognizes the language of bitstrings that contain
% at least one~\str{0} and at least one~\str{1}.
% % $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
% %   \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{0} and at least one \str{1}}}$.
% \begin{equation*} 
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}}
%   \tag{$*$} \label{fig:MachinesWithRedundantStates}
% \end{equation*}
In this chapter's first section, \zcref{rem:HowToDesignFSM} recommended
designing Finite State machines by thinking about each
state's future, anticipating inputs to come. 
Above, the future for $q_3$
is the same as that for~$q_4$\Dash
those states are redundant.
% Likewise, $q_5$'s future matches~$q_3$'s.

% This table shows the future of each state by listing what happens if the machine 
% is started in the given state and the
% given string is on the tape.
% Entries are `+' 
% if the machine then ends in an accepting state,
% and otherwise are blank. 
% The states $q_2$ and~$q_4$ have the same rows, at least for the strings
% listed, as do the states $q_3$ and~$q_5$. 
% \begin{equation*}\small
%   \begin{tabular}{r|*{8}{c}c}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } 
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\varepsilon$} 
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{00}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{01}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{10}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{11}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{000}}
%       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ldots}  
%     \\ \cline{2-10}
%     $q_0$ &  &  &  &  &+ &+ &  &  &\ldots \\
%     $q_1$ &  &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &\ldots \\
%     $q_2$ &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &+ &\ldots \\
%     $q_3$ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%     $q_4$ &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &+ &\ldots \\
%     $q_5$ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   \end{tabular}
%   \tag{$**$} \label{ex:MinimizationInitial}
%   % \begin{tabular}{r|*{10}{c}c}
%   %   \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } 
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\varepsilon$} 
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{00}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{01}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{10}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{11}}
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{000}}  
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{001}}  
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{010}}  
%   %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ldots}  
%   %   \\ \cline{2-12}
%   %   $q_0$ &  &  &  &  &+ &+ &  &  &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   %   $q_1$ &  &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   %   $q_2$ &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   %   $q_3$ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   %   $q_4$ &  &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &  &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   %   $q_5$ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &+ &\ldots \\
%   % \end{tabular}
% \end{equation*}
% In contrast, $q_0$ does not have the same row as any other state,
% nor does $q_1$.

\begin{definition}\label{def:distinguishable}
Fix a Finite State machine over an alphabet $\Sigma$. 
For two states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$, a $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ 
is a \definend{distinguishing string}
if starting the machine in $q$ with $\sigma$ on the tape
and starting it in $\hat{q}$ with $\sigma$ on the tape results
in two different outcomes:~for one the machine ends in an
accepting state while for the other it rejects. 
Two states for which there is a distinguishing string are 
\definend{distinguishable}.\index{distinguishable states}\index{states!distinguishable}
Otherwise they are
\definend{indistinguishable},\index{indistinguishable states}\index{states!indistinguishable} 
denoted \definend{$q\sim\hat{q}$}.\footnotemark
\end{definition}\footnotetext{%
  The prior Extra section has distinguishable and indistinguishable strings.
  These are  distinguishable and indistinguishable states.}

% \begin{example} \label{ex:MachinesWithMinNoStates}
% This is a minimal version of the machine in 
% (\ref{fig:MachinesWithRedundantStates}).
% \begin{equation*} 
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa16.pdf}}
%   % \tag{$**$}
% \end{equation*}
% Starting in state~$r_0$
% and processing the one character string~$\sigma=\str{1}$
% ends in a rejecting state, while
% starting in $r_1$ and processing the same string ends in an accepting state.
% So the two are distinguished by~$\sigma$.
% The states $r_0$ and~$r_2$,
% are distinguished by the string $\sigma=\str{0}$,
% and $r_0$ is distinguished from~$r_3$ by the empty string.
% Similarly, the pairs $r_1,r_2$ and $r_1,r_3$, and $r_2,r_3$ are all
% distinguishable.
% This minimal machine has no indistinguishable states.
% \end{example}

So our formal interpretation of `redundant' is `indistinguishable'.
% As illustrated in the example below,
The algorithm that we will use to find the minimal machine collapses
indistinguishable states together into classes.
These classes are the states of that machine.

To compute whether states are indistinguishable,
we first check whether they 
are distinguished by strings of length~$0$, 
then by strings of length~$1$, etc.

\begin{definition}
Two states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$
are \definend{$n$-distinguishable}\index{n distinguishable states@$n$-distinguishable states}\index{states!n distinguishable@$n$-distinguishable}
if there is a distinguishing string of length at most~$n$,
otherwise they are \definend{$n$-indistinguishable}, 
denoted \definend{$q\sim_{n}\hat{q}$}.\index{n indistinguishable states@$n$-indistinguishable states}\index{states!n indistinguishable@$n$-indistinguishable}
\end{definition}

Observe that two states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$
are $0$-indistinguishable if and only if both are accepting states or
both are rejecting states.

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}
  The relations $\sim_0$, $\sim_1$, \ldots{}
  are equivalence relations and so
  partition the states into
  equivalences classes,
  the \definend{$0$-distinguishable states},\index{n-distinguishable states@$n$-distinguishable states}
  the \definend{$1$-distinguishable states},
  etc.
  The relation of being indistinguishable, $\sim$, is also
  an equivalence, and also partitions the set of states into
  equivalence classes, the 
  \definend{distinguishable states},\index{distinguishable states@distinguishable states}\index{states!distinguishable}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \zcref{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}.  
\end{proof}

% \begin{example}  \label{ex:UnrefinedMinimization}
% For the machine~(\ref{fig:MachinesWithRedundantStates}),
% here are some $n$-equivalence classes.
% The computation uses the table~(\ref{ex:MinimizationInitial})
% \begin{equation*}
%   \begin{array}{r|l}
%   \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Step $n$}}
%   &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$\sim_{n}$ classes}}
%   \\ \hline
%   0
%   &\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_3,q_5}
%   \\
%   1
%   &\eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
%   \\
%   2
%   &\eclass_{2,0}=\set{q_0}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{2,1}=\set{q_1}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{2,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
%   \quad
%   \eclass_{2,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
%   \end{array}
% \end{equation*}
% The $0$-distinguishable classes divide the
% rejecting states from the accepting ones.

% The $1$-distinguishable classes subdivide those,
% based on the length one strings.
% Specifically, starting with $\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}$,
% we can next distinguish $q_0$ from~$q_1$ because they
% differ on the one-character string~\str{1}.
% Similarly, $q_0$ differs from $q_2$ and~$q_4$ on~\str{0}.
% And, $q_1$ differs from $q_2$ and~$q_4$ on the string~\str{0}.
% So where there was one  $\sim_0$ class~$\eclass_{0,0}$ 
% there are now three $\sim_1$ classes,
% $\eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}$,
% $\eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}$, 
% and $\eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}$. 

% Next, using the length two strings to find the
% the $2$-distinguishability classes does not result in
% any further subdivisions.
% So the $\sim$ equivalence classes are
% $\eclass_0=\set{q_0}$,
% $\eclass_{1}=\set{q_1}$,
% $\eclass_{2}=\set{q_2,q_4}$,
% and $\eclass_{3}=\set{q_3,q_5}$.

% Now we make the minimal machine from these classes.
% Consider the transition table below.
% The accepting classes are the ones containing accepting states.
% As to its first line,  we have $q_0\in\eclass_0$ and
% if the machine~($*$) is in that state and is given the character~\str{0}
% then it goes to $q_1\in\eclass_1$,
% while feeding it~\str{1} in the same state 
% takes it to $q_2\in\eclass_2$.
% (The circle diagram is the one from 
% \zcref{ex:MachinesWithMinNoStates}.
% It reflects the table but with the state names changed from
% $\eclass_i$ to $r_i$.)
% \begin{center}
%   \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
%                 &\str{0}     &\str{1}   \\
%     \cline{2-3}
%     $\eclass_0$ &$\eclass_1$ &$\eclass_2$   \\
%     $\eclass_1$ &$\eclass_1$ &$\eclass_3$    \\
%     $\eclass_2$ &$\eclass_3$ &$\eclass_2$  \\
%     \acceptingstate
%     $\eclass_3$ &$\eclass_3$ &$\eclass_3$    \\
%   \end{transitionfunction}
%   \hspace*{3em}
%   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa16.pdf}}
% \end{center}
% An important thing happens in the final two lines,
% where the classes have multiple elements.
% For instance, $\eclass_2$ has $q_2$ and~$q_4$.
% Going through the calculation shows that
% both $\Delta(q_2,\str{a})$ and $\Delta(q_4,\str{a})$ give~$\eclass_3$.
% Similarly, $\Delta(q_2,\str{b})=\Delta(q_4,\str{b})=\eclass_2$.
% \zcref{le:MooresAlgCorrect} verifies that in
% Moore's algorithm the $\Delta$ is well-defined,
% returning the same output class no matter which
% state of the machine~($*$) we use to
% represent the input class. 
% \end{example}

Moore's algorithm has two phases.
The first finds the $\sim_0$ classes, 
then the $\sim_1$ classes, etc.,
in a sequence of steps.
This phase ends with the $\sim$~classes.
The second phase uses those as the states of the desired machine.
% Its transition function is:~compute
% $\Delta(\eclass_i,x)$
% by picking any element~$q\in\eclass_i$ as a
% representative,
% finding the state that the machine~$\FSM$ transitions to
% when it is in $q$ and reads the character~$x$,
% and then as output giving the class containing that state. 

From the definition the natural way to perform the first phase is:~to
get the $\sim_0$ classes it could check all length~$0$ string inputs,
to get the $\sim_1$ classes it could check all length~$1$ strings,
then check all length~$2$ strings, etc.
% This algorithm does indeed return a minimal machine recognizing the
% same language.
% However, as performed in the above example it has one aspect
% that needs fine tuning.
% To get the $\sim_0$ classes it checks all length~$0$ string inputs,
% to get the $\sim_1$ classes it checks all length~$1$ strings,
% then it checks all length~$2$ strings, etc.

However,
an algorithm that at step~$n$ checks all length~$n$
strings takes exponentially long, because there are $2^n$ many such strings.
To address that, consider states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ that are not
$n$-distinguishable but are $n+1$-distinguishable.
Write a distinguishing string 
$\sigma=\sequence{s_0,s_1,\,\ldots s_{n-1},s_n}$, 
as $\sigma=\alpha\concat s_n$.
Because $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ are not $n$-distinguishable,
where the length~$n$ string $\alpha$ brings the machine from
$q$ to some state~$r$
and also brings the machine from $\hat{q}$ to some~$\hat{r}$,
then $r$ and~$\hat{r}$ are $n$-equivalent, so $r,\hat{r}\in\eclass_{n,i}$.
Therefore, $n+1$-distinguishing between $q$ and $\hat{q}$
must happen with the final character,~$s_n$.
That character must take the machine from state~$r$ to a state in one 
class~$\eclass_{n+1,j}$, 
and also take the machine from state~$\hat{r}$ to 
a state in a different class~$\eclass_{n+1,\hat{j}}$ with $j\neq\hat{j}$.
In short, when looking for a split in passing from the 
$n$-distinguishability classes
to the $n+1$-distinguishability classes, 
we need only look at single character inputs.
The number of characters does not grow from step to step.

The rest of this section contains two full minimization examples.
% using that observation to
% refine \zcref{ex:UnrefinedMinimization}.

\begin{example}
\begin{credit}
    Diane J.\ Cook, \url{https://www.eecs.wsu.edu/~cook/tcs/l10.html}  
\end{credit}
We will minimize this machine, which  
accepts strings described by \re{(ab|ba)*}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min09.pdf}
\end{center}
The algorithm's first phase is a sequence of steps.
Step~0 makes two classes,
one with the accepting states and one with the others.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_2}
  \quad
  \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_1, q_3, q_4, q_5}
\end{equation*}
For bookkeeping we will use a triangular table. 
Each pair of states $q_i,q_j$ has a table cell.
We start by checkmarking the $q_i,q_j$ cell if
those states are $0$-distinguishabile,
that is, if $q_i$ and~$q_j$ are in different $\sim_0$ classes.
\begin{center} 
  \begin{minimizetable}{6}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{1-1}
    \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{2-2}
        &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    &    \\
    \cline{3-3}
    \ck &       &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    &    \\
    \cline{4-4}
    \ck &      &\ck  &   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   &    \\
    \cline{5-5}
    \ck &      &\ck    &   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\ 
    \cline{1-5}      
  \end{minimizetable}
\end{center}
For later steps
note that we can read that $\sim_0$ equivalence classes
from the cells that are blank.
For the first class, there is a blank in the table cell $q_0,q_2$.
The second class is described by the cluster of blanks in the 
cells  $q_1,q_3$ and $q_1,q_4$ and~$q_1,q_5$,
and $q_3,q_4$, and $q_3,q_5$, and $q_4,q_5$.

Step~1 determines whether the two $\sim_0$ classes split.
We start with a calculation:~for
each pair $q_i,q_j$ that are together in a $\sim_0$ class and
for each input character, find the classes where that character
sends those two states.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$   
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$      \\ 
      \cline{2-3}       
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,0}$       \\ 
      $q_1,q_4$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$       \\ 
      $q_1,q_5$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$       \\ 
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$       \\ 
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$       \\ 
      $q_4,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$       \\ 
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  We have shown that two states are
  $0$-indistinguishable but $1$-distinguishable if they are
  sent to unequal classes by a single-character input.
  So we look for entries having two different classes.
  The first such case is in the $q_1,q_3$ line, 
  where \str{a} takes $q_1$ to a state in~$\eclass_{0,0}$ and
  takes $q_3$ to a state in~$\eclass_{0,1}$.
  Consequently the $\sim_0$ class containing $q_1$ and~$q_3$ 
  will split into multiple $\sim_{1}$ classes.
  (The same line also shows that split in the \str{b} column
  but that is repeat information.)
  The other lines showing a split are
  $q_1,q_4$, and $q_1,q_5$, and $q_3,q_4$, and $q_4,q_5$. 

  To record those splits 
  add a checkmark to the triangular table's cell for~$q_1,q_3$,
  as well as to the cells for
  $q_1,q_4$, and $q_1,q_5$, and $q_3,q_4$, and $q_4,q_5$.
  Here the changed cells are highlighted.\footnote{%
    A person doing the algorithm can just keep a single table and
    update it at each step.
    But for this presentation
    at each step we will show the entire revised table.}
\begin{center} 
  \begin{minimizetable}{6}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{1-1}
    \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{2-2}
        &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    &    \\
    \cline{3-3}
    \ck &\textcolor{highlightcolor}{\ck}  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}  &    \\
    \cline{4-4}
    \ck &\textcolor{highlightcolor}{\ck} &\ck  &\textcolor{highlightcolor}{\ck}   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   &    \\
    \cline{5-5}
    \ck &\textcolor{highlightcolor}{\ck}  &\ck    &   &\textcolor{highlightcolor}{\ck}    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\ 
    \cline{1-5}      
  \end{minimizetable}
\end{center}
  Finish step~$1$ by using that table to get the  $\sim_{1}$~classes.
  There is a blank cell in $q_0,q_2$.
  There is also a blank cell in $q_3,q_5$. 
  There are no cells involving $q_1$ or $q_4$
  but since every state must be in some class, they each go
  into a singleton class.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0,q_2} \quad
    \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}   \quad
    \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_3,q_5}  \quad
    \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_4}
  \end{equation*}
  The $\sim_0$ class $\eclass_{0,1}$ has split
  into three $\sim_1$~classes. 

  Step~$2$ iterates.
  Start with the calculation involving pairs of states from the
  $\sim_1$ classes.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$   
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,1}$      \\ 
      \cline{2-3}       
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,3},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,3}$    
                &$q_0\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$       \\ 
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  This table does not show any splitting.
  So the first phase of the algorithm terminates.
  These are the $\sim$ classes.
  \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_0=\set{q_0,q_2} \quad
        \eclass_1=\set{q_1}   \quad
        \eclass_{2}=\set{q_3,q_5} \quad
        \eclass_{3}=\set{q_4}      
  \end{equation*}

  The second phase of Moore's algorithm produces the minimal machine.
  Its states are those $\sim$~classes. 
  For its transitions, these tables illustrate.
  The one on the right takes
  $r_0$ as a name for $\eclass_{0}$ 
  and $r_1$ for $\eclass_{1}$, etc.
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
      \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}      \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$ &$q_3\in \eclass_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1}$  \\
      $q_2$ &$q_5\in \eclass_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1$ &$q_2\in \eclass_0$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{3}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3$ &$q_4\in \eclass_3$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{0}$  \\
      $q_5$ &$q_4\in \eclass_3$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_4$ &$q_4\in \eclass_3$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace*{3em}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_0$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_1$}  \\
  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_1$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_0$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$}  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_0$}  \\
  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$}  \\ 
\end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  The important thing happens in the classes $\eclass_{0}$ and
  $\eclass_{2}$, which have multiple elements.
  For instance, in $\eclass_0$ both of the elements $q_0$ and~$q_2$
  show the same transition pattern:~with input \str{a} they both
  transition to a state in $\eclass_2$
  and with input \str{b} they both transition to $\eclass_1$.
  We get the same output class no matter which
  state  we use to represent the input class.
  We say that the transition function is well defined.\footnote{%
  More on well-defined in in \protect\zcref{sec:Functions}.}
  % \zcref{le:MooresAlgCorrect} verifies that in
  % Moore's algorithm the returned $\Delta$ is well-defined.

  In short, in the output machine compute
  $\Delta(r_i,x)$ for $x\in\Sigma$
  by picking any representative~$q\in\eclass_i$,
  finding the state $\hat{q}$ to which the machine~$\FSM$ transitions
  when it is in $q$ and reads the character~$x$,
  and then as output giving~$r_j$ where $\hat{q}\in\eclass_j$.

  Here is the algorithm's output, the minimal machine.
  The start state is $r_0$ because $q_0\in\eclass_0$. 
  Similarly the final states are the ones containing final states of the
  original machine, $r_1$ and $r_3$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min10.pdf}
  \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FirstMinimization}
  We will find a machine that recognizes the same language as this one but
  that is minimal.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}
  \end{center}

  Strictly speaking, even before the first phase we should drop from the
  machine any states that are unreachable from $q_0$.
  By eye we can see that there are no such states here.

  Step~$0$ distinguishes the states that are accepting from those that are not.
  That gives two $\sim_0$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_3,q_4}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1,q_2,q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
  Here is the initial triangular table. 
  % Each pair of states $q_i,q_j$ has a table cell.
  % We start by checkmarking the $q_i,q_j$ cell if
  % those states are $0$-distinguishabile,
  % that is, if $q_i$ and~$q_j$ are in different $\sim_0$ classes.
  % (In step~$1$ we will refining the table to show $1$-distinguishability, etc.)
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  % For later steps
  % note that we can also read that $\sim_0$ equivalence class
  % information from the blank cells.
  % For instance, there are
  % blanks in table entries $q_0,q_3$ and $q_0,q_4$ and~$q_3,q_4$ 
  % and this cluster forms the first $\sim_0$ equivalence class.
  % Similarly there is a cluster of blanks in $q_1,q_2$ 
  % and $q_1,q_5$ and~$q_2,q_5$. 

  Step~$1$ starts with this calculation.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$   
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$      \\ 
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$      
                &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$       \\ 
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$       
                &$q_5\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$       \\ 
      \cline{2-3}       
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$    
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$       \\ 
      $q_1,q_5$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$     
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$       \\  
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$     
                &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$   
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  The first case where we find states that
  are $0$-indistinguishable but $1$-distinguishable is in the $q_1,q_5$ line, 
  where \str{a} takes $q_1$ to a state in~$\eclass_{0,0}$ and
  takes $q_5$ to a state in~$\eclass_{0,1}$.
  The line for $q_2,q_5$ similarly shows that those two states 
  are $1$-distinguishable.
  Add checkmarks to the triangular table's cells for~$q_1,q_5$
  and for $q_2,q_5$.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  
  Finish step~$1$ by using that table to get the  $\sim_{1}$~classes.
  There is a cluster of blank cells in $q_0,q_3$ and $q_0,q_4$ and $q_3,q_4$.
  There is also a blank cell in $q_1,q_2$. 
  There is no cluster involving $q_5$ but since every state must be in some
  class, that state goes into a singleton class.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0,q_3,q_4} \quad
    \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
    \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}
  The $\sim_0$ class $\eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_5}$ has split
  into two $\sim_1$~classes. 

  Step~$2$ iterates.
  % Again start with a calculation involving pairs of states from the
  % classes.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$   
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$  
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$   
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}                              
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,0}$  
                &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,0}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  There is a split on the $q_0,q_3$ line and another on the
  $q_0,q_4$ line.
  Add those to the triangular table. 
  \begin{center}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  The blank cells give these $\sim_2$ classes.
  \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_{2,0}=\set{q_0} \quad
        \eclass_{2,1}=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
        \eclass_{2,2}=\set{q_3,q_4} \quad
        \eclass_{2,3}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}

  Step~$3$ will be the final iteration.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}    \\
      \cline{2-3}                              
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_4\in\eclass_{2,2}$  
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_3\in\eclass_{2,2}$    \\  
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_5\in\eclass_{2,3}$   
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_5\in\eclass_{2,3}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  There is no more splitting
  so the first phase of the algorithm terminates.
  These are the $\sim$ classes.
  \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_0=\set{q_0} \quad
        \eclass_1=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
        \eclass_{2}=\set{q_3,q_4} \quad
        \eclass_{3}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}

  The second phase produces the minimal machine.
  Its states are those $\sim$~classes. 
  For its transitions, these tables illustrate.
  The one on the right takes
  $r_0$ as a name for $\eclass_{0}$ 
  and $r_1$ for $\eclass_{1}$, etc.
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
      \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}      \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$ &$q_1\in \eclass_1$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{1}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1$ &$q_3\in \eclass_2$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{2}$  \\
      $q_2$ &$q_4\in \eclass_2$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{2}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3$ &$q_5\in \eclass_3$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{3}$  \\
      $q_4$ &$q_5\in \eclass_3$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{3}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_5$ &$q_5\in \eclass_3$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace*{3em}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_0$} &$r_1$ &$r_1$  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_1$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$}  \\ 
  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_3$}  \\
  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$}  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Again we find that transition function on the right is well defined.
% the states in the multi-element classes 
%   The important thing happens in the classes $\eclass_{1}$ and
%   $\eclass_{2}$, which have multiple elements.
%   All of their elements show the same transition pattern.
%   For instance, $\eclass_1$ has $q_1$ and~$q_2$
%   and on both lines on both inputs they transition to the same class,
%   namely with input \str{a} they both transition to $\eclass_2$
%   and with input \str{b} they both transition to $\eclass_2$.
% Going through the calculation shows that
% both $\Delta(q_2,\str{a})$ and $\Delta(q_4,\str{a})$ give~$\eclass_3$.
% Similarly, $\Delta(q_2,\str{b})=\Delta(q_4,\str{b})=\eclass_2$.
\zcref{le:MooresAlgCorrect} below verifies that in
Moore's algorithm the $\Delta$ is well-defined,
returning the same output class no matter which
state we use to
represent the input class.


  % In short, compute
  % $\Delta(r_i,x)$ for $x\in\Sigma$
  % by picking any representative~$q\in\eclass_i$,
  % finding the state $\hat{q}$ to which the machine~$\FSM$ transitions
  % when it is in $q$ and reads the character~$x$,
  % and then as output giving~$r_j$ where $\hat{q}\in\eclass_j$.

  Here is the algorithm's output, the minimal machine.
  The start state is $r_0$ because $q_0\in\eclass_0$. 
  Similarly the final states are the ones containing final states of the
  original machine, $r_1$ and $r_3$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min04.pdf}
  \end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
To explain the terminology that this algorithm `collapses' together 
the redundant states, 
consider this picture of the prior example, showing a kind of projection.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{rc}
    \tabulartext{Input $\FSM$:}
      &\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}} \\[6.0ex]
    \tabulartext{Output $\mathcal{N}$:}
      &\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min04.pdf}}
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}

% \begin{example}  \label{ex:SecondMinimization}
%   We will minimize the machine below.
%   This illustrates one additional point of the algorithm since this
%   machine has an unreachable state,~$q_5$.
%   Start by omitting it.
%   \begin{center}
%     \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min00.pdf}
%   \end{center}
%   That leaves these $\sim_0$ classes and the initial triangular table.
%   \begin{center} 
%     $\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2} 
%       \quad
%       \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_3,q_4}$
%     \qquad  
%     \begin{minimizetable}{5}
%       \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
%       \cline{1-1}
%           &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
%       \cline{2-2}
%           &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
%       \cline{3-3}

%   \begin{center}
%     \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min05.pdf}
%   \end{center}
% \end{example}


\begin{lemma} \label{le:MooresAlgCorrect}
Moore's algorithm 
outputs a machine that recognizes the same language as the input machine
and that is minimal.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\zcref{exer:ProofMooresAlgorithm}
verifies that that Moore's algorithm always halts, 
that it produces a Finite State machine
with a well-defined transition function, 
that this output machine recognizes the same language as the input machine,
and that the output machine is minimal.
\end{proof}

\medskip
We close by describing a common scenario in which minimization
plays an important role.
We have seen that when we have a problem to solve
with a Finite State machine, 
often a nondeterministic machine is easier and more natural.
An example is an algorithm that inputs a regular expression
and outputs a machine recognizing that expression.
But our algorithm for converting a nondeterministic machine 
to a deterministic machine has the difficulty
that where the nondeterministic machine has $n$~states, 
the deterministic machine has $2^n$~many.
This section's result alleviates that exponential blow-up.
We now have a three-step process to solve these 
problems:~start with a nondeterministic
answer, convert to an equivalent deterministic machine,
and then minimize to get a reasonably-sized final answer.
This gives good results in practice.

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
From the triangular table find the $\sim_i$ classes.
\begin{center}\small
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
There is a cluster of blank cells in the entries $q_0,q_1$ and
$q_0,q_2$ and $q_1,q_2$.
There is also a blank in $q_3,q_5$.
There is no blank associated with $q_4$ so it is alone in its class.
The equivalence classes are $\eclass_{i,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$,
and $\eclass_{i,1}=\set{q_3,q_5}$,
and $\eclass_{i,2}=\set{q_4}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Suppose that

and
$\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_3, q_4}$, and
we compute this table.
\begin{center} \small
  \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,0}$                    
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,1}$     \\  
    $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$                    
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$     \\  
    $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$                    
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
    $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$                    
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
    $q_1,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
    $q_2,q_5$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$

    $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$

  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Which states are $1$-distinguishable that

\item Give the resulting $\sim_1$~classes.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The classes are different for $q_0,q_1$, and

\item
The matching $\sim_1$~classes are
$\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_5}$, 
and $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}$,

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
This machine accepts strings
with an odd number of~\str{1}'s.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min07.pdf}
\end{center}
Minimize it with Moore's algorithm.
\begin{answer}\verified
  First, all the states are reachable by inspection.

  Step~$0$ is to
  put into separate classes the accepting states and the non-accepting states.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1}        
   \end{equation*}
  We also make the initial triangular table by
  checkmarking the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{3}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}    \\
      \cline{1-2}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{center}
   Thus the checkmarks denote a pair of states that are
   $0$-distinguishable and the 
   blanks denote pairs of states that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   
   Step~$1$ sees if any of the $\sim_0$ classes split.
   Here is the computation.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  Neither of the two classes split.
  The first phase of the algorithm stops with two $\sim$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}=\eclass_0=r_0
     \qquad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}=\eclass_1=r_1        
   \end{equation*}
  Incorporating the transition information from the original machine gives 
  this minimized one.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min08.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For many machines we can find the unreachable states by eye,
but there is an algorithm.
It inputs a machine~$\FSM$ and initializes the set of reachable states
to $R_0=\set{q_0}$.
For $n>0$, step~$n$ of the algorithm is:~for each
$q\in R_n$ find all states~$\hat{q}$ reachable from~$q$ in one transition
and add those to make~$R_{n+1}$.
That is, 
$R_{n+1}=R_n\cup\set{\hat{q}=\Delta_{\FSM}(q,x)\suchthat\text{$q\in R_n$ and $x\in\Sigma$}}$.
The algorithm stops when $R_k=R_{k+1}$ and the set of reachable states 
is $R=R_k$.
The unreachable states are the others, $Q-R$.
For each machine, perform this algorithm.\\
\begin{exparts*}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min11.pdf}}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min13.pdf}}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item These are the sets.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Step $n$}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R_n$}
    \\ \hline
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  \\
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  Thus the set of reachable states is $R=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$.
  The set of unreachable states is $Q-R=\set{q_3}$.
  Here is the machine with only the reachable states.
  \begin{display}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min12.pdf}}
  \end{display}
  \item These are the sets.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Step $n$}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R_n$}
    \\ \hline
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$  \\  
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$  \\  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The set of reachable states is $R=R_2=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$.
  The set of unreachable states is $Q-R=\set{q_2,q_5}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
