    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,1}$   
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$      \\ 
    $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,0}$      
                &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,1}$       \\ 
    \end{tabular}
\end{display}
This triangular table is completely filled and each state
is in its own equivalence class.
\begin{equation*}
   \begin{minimizetable}{6}
     \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
     \cline{1-1}
     \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
     \cline{2-2}
     \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
     \cline{3-3}
     \ck &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
     \cline{4-4}
     \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
     \cline{5-5}
     \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
     \cline{1-5}
   \end{minimizetable}
   \qquad
   \eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0}        
   \quad
   \eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1}
   \quad
   \eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_2}
   \quad
   \eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_3}
   \quad
   \eclass_{2,4} =\set{q_4}
   \quad
   \eclass_{2,5} =\set{q_5}
\end{equation*}
Since there are no indistinguishable states to collapse together,
the original machine is minimal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  \begin{credit}
    From K.\ M.\ George, \url{http://www.cs.okstate.edu/~kmg/class/5313/fall13/notes/four.pdf}
  \end{credit}
Perform Moore's minimization algorithm on this machine.
\begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa79.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  For reference, here is the machine.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa79.pdf}
  \end{display}
  By inspection every state is reachable.

  Step~$0$ separates the states into two $\sim_0$ classes,
  one where the states are not accepting and one where they are.
  Also shown is the associated triangular table, marking the
  $0$-disinguishable states,
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4,q_5}
    \quad
    \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_1,q_3}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &     &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &\ck &     &\ck &  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{equation*} 

   Step~$1$ sees if any of the two $\sim_0$ classes split.
   Here is the calculation.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_4,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  That gives this triangular table and the
  $\sim_1$ equivalence classes, showing that
  $\eclass_{0,0}$ splits into two.
  \begin{equation*} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &     &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4}
    \quad
    \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_5}
    \quad
    \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_1,q_3}
   \end{equation*} 

  Next, step~$2$.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,2}$    \\
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,2}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,2}$    \\  
     \cline{2-3}
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,1}$
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,0}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  This triangular table summarizes, showing that $\eclass_{1,2}$ splits.
  \begin{equation*} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    \eclass_{2,0}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4}
    \quad
    \eclass_{2,1}=\set{q_5}
    \quad
    \eclass_{2,2}=\set{q_1}
    \quad
    \eclass_{2,3}=\set{q_3}
   \end{equation*} 
   
  Step~$3$'s calculation shows no more splitting.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{2,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_3\in\eclass_{2,3}$    \\
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{2,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_3\in\eclass_{2,3}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_4$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_1\in\eclass_{2,2}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_3\in\eclass_{2,3}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  These are the $\sim$ equivalence classes.
  \begin{equation*} 
    \eclass_{0}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4}
    \quad
    \eclass_{1}=\set{q_5}
    \quad
    \eclass_{2}=\set{q_1}
    \quad
    \eclass_{3}=\set{q_3}
  \end{equation*}
  
  This is the minimal machine recognizing the same language as
  the original machine.
   \begin{display}
     \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa80.pdf}
   \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \url{https://www.eecs.wsu.edu/~cook/tcs/l10.html}
\end{credit}
This machine accepts strings described by \re{(ab|ba)*}.
Minimize it, using the algorithm of this section.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min09.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  By inspection the states $q_6$ and~$q_7$ are unreachable
  so drop them.

  With that, step~$0$ checkmarks the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
     $\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}$        
     \quad
     $\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1, q_3, q_4, q_5}$
     \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &    &\ck  &  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &\ck  &  &  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}

   Step~$1$ sees if any of these classes split.
   First comes the calculation.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,1}$   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_4$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_4,q_5$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The pairs that are $1$-distinguishable but not $0$-distinguishable are
  $q_1,q_3$, and $q_1,q_4$, and $q_1, q_5$, and $q_3,q_4$, and $q_4,q_5$.
  Add the checkmarks to the triangular table.
  Then the clusters of blank cells tell us the $\sim_1$ classes.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad\qquad
    \begin{tabular}{l}
     $\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}$  \\
     $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}$       \\        
     $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_5}$   \\        
     $\eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_4}$            
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  Step~$2$ determines which states are $2$-distinguishable.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,3},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  % \begin{display}
  %   \begin{minimizetable}{6}
  %     \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
  %     \cline{1-1}
  %     \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &  &     \\
  %     \cline{2-2}
  %         &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &  &     \\
  %     \cline{3-3}
  %     \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &      \\
  %     \cline{4-4}
  %     \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$} &       \\
  %     \cline{5-5}
  %     \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}        \\
  %     \cline{1-5}
  %   \end{minimizetable}
  % \end{display}
  There is no splitting. 

  So the minimal machine has four states.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     r_2=\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
     \quad
     r_3=\eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}
  The transitions from the input machine give 
  this minimized one.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min10.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
If a machine's start state is accepting, must the minimized machine's
start state be accepting?
If so then prove it, and if not then
give an example machine where it is false.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer is ``yes.''
  The initial state of the minimal machine, $r_0$, is the one that 
  contains~$q_0$.
  In the minimized machine a state is accepting if and only if it contains any 
  accepting states in the original machine.
  So if $q_0$ is accepting then $r_0$ is also accepting.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
Minimize.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min01.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  Step~$0$ checkmarks the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_3}$  \\        
      $\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_2, q_4}$
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &      \\
      \cline{3-3}

      \ck &\ck &     &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}       \\

   \end{display}

  Next, step~$1$ sees if any of the $\sim_0$ classes split.
  The calculation is below.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$

                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The state pairs that are $1$-distinguishable 
  but not $0$-distinguishable are $q_0,q_1$, and $q_0,q_3$, and $q_2,q_4$.
  The triangular table reflects these changes,
  and the blank cells show that there are four $\sim_1$ classes.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}

    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}

      $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_3}$  \\        
      $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2}$   \\        
      $\eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_4}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  Step~$2$ decides of any of the $\sim_1$~classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}

  That calculation shows that $q_1$ and~$q_3$ are $2$-distinguishable.
  That's reflected in the triangular table.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &      \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &       \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}       \\
      \cline{1-4}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0}$    \\
      $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{1,4} =\set{q_4}$              
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  
   Obviously at the next step no classes will split because singleton classes
   cannot split.
   In short, every $\sim$ class is a singleton and 
   the starting machine is itself minimal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Minimize.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min17.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  By eye, all of the states are reachable.
  Step~$0$ splits the collection of all states into the class of
  accepting state and the class of non-accepting states.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_3, q_4}
   \end{equation*}
  We also give the initial triangular table by checkmarking the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and~$q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &    &     &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}

  Step~$1$ computes whether any state pairs are 
  $1$-distinguishable that are not $0$-distinguishable.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\ 
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  That computation distinguishes 
  $q_0,q_1$, and $q_0,q_2$, and $q_1,q_5$, and $q_2,q_5$.
  Here is the updated triangular table.
  Its blank cells give these as the $\sim_1$ classes.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_5}$    \\
      $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The class $\eclass_{0,0}$ has split into two.

  Iterate.
  Step~$2$ computes whether any of those $\sim_1$ equivalence classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,0}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,2}$    \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,2},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,2}$
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_5\in\eclass_{1,0}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The only state pair that is $2$-distinguishable but was not 
  $1$-distinguishable is $q_0,q_5$.
  The triangular table reflects this additional checkmark.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0}$        \\
      $\eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}$    \\        
      $\eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_5}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  Again.
  Step~$3$'s calculation is here.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{2,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{2,1}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_4\in\eclass_{2,2}$    \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{2,2},\,q_4\in\eclass_{2,2}$
                 &$q_5\in\eclass_{2,3},\,q_5\in\eclass_{2,3}$    \\  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  There is no splitting.
  The first phase of Moore's algorithm terminates.
  These are the $\sim$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_{3,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_{3,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}        
     \quad
     r_2=\eclass_{3,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}        
     \quad
     r_3=\eclass_{3,3} =\set{q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
   
  As to the second phase, here is the minimized machine.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min18.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
This machine has no accepting states.
Minimize it.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min15.pdf}
\end{center}
What happens to a machine where all states are accepting?
\begin{answer}\verified
  We will go through the algorithm but before we do, observe that
  the answer must be that a machine with no accepting states
  therefore accepts no string.
  A minimal such machine has one state, the initial state,
  and all arrows are loops.
  So we know what the answer must be before we start and we are
  just verifying that the algorithm does the right thing.

  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  Step~$0$ divides the collection of states into the accepting states
  and the others.
  Here we also include the initial triangular table.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_3}$        
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{minimizetable}{4}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &      \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &       \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &        \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}      \\
      \cline{1-3}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   The point is that there is one $\sim_{0}$-equivalence class, not the
   two that we have seen in other minimizations.

  Step~$1$ sees if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_3$  &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_0\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  No state pairs are distinguished.
  Since there is only one class, $\eclass_{0,0}$, 
  no entry in that computation table can show two classes. 
  So there is only one $\sim_1$ class.
  The first phase of Moore's algorithm stops.

  For the second phase there is only one class so 
  this is the resulting minimized machine, as predicted.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min16.pdf}
  \end{display}

  If we instead start with a machine where all states are accepting
  then a similar thing happens.
  It recognizes the language $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and its
  minimization has one state, which is both initial and accepting.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Minimize this machine.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min02.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  Step~$0$ splits the states into two classes, those that are accepting
  and those that are not.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_2, q_3, q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1, q_4}
   \end{equation*}
  Here is the initial triangular table,
  with entry $q_i,q_j$ checkmarked  
  when one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &    &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &\ck &     &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}     \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}

  Step~$1$ computes whether any of those classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_5\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\  
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_5\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\  
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_1,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  In that calculation we find different classes in entries on the lines
  for $q_0,q_2$, and $q_0,q_3$, and $q_0,q_5$,
  and $q_2,q_3$, and $q_3,q_5$, and $q_1,q_4$.
  This is all pairs except for $q_2,q_5$.
  Update the triangular table and split the classes.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &     &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}     \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0}$
      $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}$        
      $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2,q_5}$        
      $\eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3}$        
      $\eclass_{1,4} =\set{q_4}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  So, $q_2$ and~$q_5$ are not $1$-distinguishable but all the other pairs are
  $1$-distinguishable.

  Finally, step~$2$ computes whether the one remaining multi-state
  class splits.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{1,4}$
                 &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,4},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,1}$  
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  There is a split, and so 
  %   \begin{minimizetable}{6}
  %     \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
  %     \cline{1-1}
  %     \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &  &  &   &   \\
  %     \cline{2-2}
  %     \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_{2}$} &  &   &   \\
  %     \cline{3-3}
  %     \ck &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$} &  &     \\
  %     \cline{4-4}
  %     \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}  &       \\
  %     \cline{5-5}
  %     \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_5$}     \\
  %     \cline{1-5}
  %   \end{minimizetable}
  % \end{display}
  all of the states are $2$-distinguishable.
  Every $\sim_2$ class is a singleton.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,4} =\set{q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,5} =\set{q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
   The original machine is minimal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  What happens if we perform the minimization procedure on a machine that
  has an unreachable state, such as 
  \zcref{ex:SecondMinimization},  % cref fails  
  without first omitting the unreachable state?
\begin{answer}\verified
  You will get a machine that recognizes the same language.
  The reachable states of this machine will form a minimal set.
  But there will still be unreachable states (although perhaps
  fewer of them.)  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:MachineTakesNStepsToMinimize}
  Minimize.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min06.pdf}
  \end{center}
  Note that the algorithm takes a time that is roughly
  are equal to the number of states in the machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  Step~$0$ splits the collection of states into two classes,
  the accepting states and the non-accepting states.
  The initial triangular table checkmarks the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting but the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$   \\
      $\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_4}$
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad\quad
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
   \end{equation*}

  Step~$1$ computes whether any of those classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
      $q_2,q_3$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  We must put checkmarks in the triangular table cells
  $q_0,q_3$, and $q_1,q_3$, and $q_2,q_3$.
  In short, $q_3$ is $1$-distinguishable from all of the other states.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2}$   \\
      $\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_3}$             \\        
      $\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_4}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  The next time through is step~$2$.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$ 
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{1,0}$    \\
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,1}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$    \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_2\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_3\in\eclass_{1,1}$
                 &$q_1\in\eclass_{1,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{1,0}$    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \end{display}
  As with what happened in the prior step,
  the calculation gives that we need checkmarks in cells
  $q_0,q_2$ and $q_1,q_2$,
  so a single state is distinguished, namely,~$q_2$ splits
  from $q_0$ and~$q_1$.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0, q_1}$   \\
      $\eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_2}$        \\
      $\eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_3}$        \\        
      $\eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_4}$        
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  There is only one class that could split, so step~$3$ will be the
  last one, whether or not there is any splitting.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{2,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{2,1}$
                 &$q_0\in\eclass_{2,0},\,q_1\in\eclass_{2,0}$    \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  They do split;~$q_1$ is $3$-distinguishable from $q_0$. 
  These are the $\sim_3$ classes.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \begin{tabular}{@{}l}
      $\eclass_{3,0} =\set{q_0}$   \\
      $\eclass_{3,1} =\set{q_1}$   \\
      $\eclass_{3,2} =\set{q_2}$   \\
      $\eclass_{3,3} =\set{q_3}$   \\        
      $\eclass_{3,4} =\set{q_4}$              
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
   The first phase of Moore's algorithm stops.
   The minimized machine has five states; it is the machine 
   that we started with.

   Observe that had we started with a similar machine having a one more
   state then the algorithm would take one more step.
   The number of steps is two less than the number of states, that
   is, the time taken grows linearly with the number of states.  
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}
  Verify \zcref{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item Verify that each $\sim_{n}$ is an equivalence relation between
    states.
  \item Verify that $\sim$ is an equivalence.
  \end{exparts*}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Recall that to show a binary relation is an equivalence we must show
    that it is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.
    \begin{exparts}
      \item
      Fix some~$n\in\N$.
      For reflexivity,
      obviously any state is $n$-indistinguishable from itself, for any~$n$.
      Symmetry is also clear.
      So suppose $q_0\sim_{n} q_1$ and  $q_1\sim_{n} q_2$ (for some machine).
      Let $\sigma$ be a string of length less than or equal to~$n$.
      If $\sigma$ takes the machine from $q_0$ to an accepting state then
      because of $q_0\sim_{n} q_1$ it also takes the machine from
      $q_1$ to an accepting state.
      Because of $q_1\sim_{n} q_2$ it also takes the machine from
      $q_2$ to an accepting state.
      The same holds for non-accepting states.
      \item It is an equivalence because it is $\sim_k$ for some~$k$.
    \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:SketchOfProofMinimizationWorks}
This illustrates the verification of \zcref{le:MooresAlgCorrect}.
We use the two machines given at the section's start
(we write `\str{a}' for `\mbox{\texttt{\str{a}\,..\,\str{z}}}'
and `\str{A}' for `\mbox{\texttt{\str{A}\,..\,\str{Z}}}').
Call the machine on the left~$\FSM$ and the one on the right~$\mathcal{N}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item  
Take the union of the two sets of states.
Form the $\sim_0$ equivalence classes by dividing that
union into the accepting states and the non-accepting states.
\item Perform the first step of Moore's algorithm on that union.
\item Perform the next step (it is the last one).
\item
  Argue that this calculation shows that $\mathcal{N}$
  is the minimization of~$\mathcal{M}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item These are the classes.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_3,q_4,r_3} 
    \quad
   \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,r_0,r_1,r_2}
\end{equation*}
Here is the initial triangular table.
\begin{center} 
  \begin{minimizetable}{9}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{1-1}
        &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
    \cline{2-2}
        &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
    \cline{3-3}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
    \cline{4-4}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
    \cline{5-5}
        &    &    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_0$}   \\
    \cline{6-6}
        &    &    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_1$}   \\
    \cline{7-7}
        &    &    &\ck &\ck &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_2$}   \\
    \cline{8-8}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_3$}   \\
    \cline{1-8}      
  \end{minimizetable}
  \quad
\end{center}
\item
Step~1 looks at pairs from the two $\sim_0$ classes, 
calculating whether character transitions
split any $\sim_0$ class.
\begin{display}\small
  \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{A}}    \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_3,r_3$  &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_4,r_3$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_0,r_0$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_0,r_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_0,r_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_4\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_1,r_0$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_1,r_1$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_1,r_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_2,r_0$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_2,r_1$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_2,r_2$  &$q_4\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $r_0,r_1$  &$r_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$  
               &$r_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $r_0,r_2$  &$r_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$r_2\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $r_1,r_2$  &$r_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,r_3\in\eclass_{0,0}$  
               &$r_3\in\eclass_{0,0},\,r_2\in\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
There are many splits, for example $q_0$ and~$q_1$ are $1$-distinguishable.
\begin{display} 
  \begin{minimizetable}{9}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &    &    &    \\
    \cline{1-1}
    \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    &    &    \\
    \cline{2-2}
    \ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   &    &    \\
    \cline{3-3}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_3$}   &    \\
    \cline{4-4}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_4$}   \\
    \cline{5-5}
        &\ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_0$}   \\
    \cline{6-6}
    \ck &    &\ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_1$}   \\
    \cline{7-7}
    \ck &\ck &    &\ck &\ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_2$}   \\
    \cline{8-8}
    \ck &\ck &\ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$r_3$}   \\
    \cline{1-8}      
  \end{minimizetable}
\end{display}
There are six empty cells, at~$q_0,r_0$, 
at $q_1,r_1$, at $q_2,r_2$, at $q_3,q_4$, at $q_3,r_3$, and at $q_4,r_3$. 
We conclude that $\eclass_{0,0}$ does not split but that
$\eclass_{0,1}$ splits into three.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_3,q_4,r_3}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_0,r_0}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_1,r_1}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_2,r_2}  
\end{equation*}

\item
Step~2 shows no more splittings.
\item
Moore's algorithm has grouped $q_0$ with~$r_0$,
and $q_1$ with~$r_1$,
and $q_2$ with~$r_2$.
It has also grouped $q_3$ and~$q_4$ together with~$r_3$.
Since there is one and only one~$r_i$ in each class, that machine
recognizes the same language as the $q_j$~machine
and is minimal.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ProofMooresAlgorithm}
  We will verify \zcref{le:MooresAlgCorrect},
  that Moore's algorithm halts on any input machine~$\FSM$ and
  outputs an~$\mathcal{N}$ that recognizes the same language,
  and that is minimal.
\begin{exparts}
\item Prove that the algorithm always halts. 
\item Prove that the transition function of $\mathcal{N}$ is well-defined.
\item Verify that the two machines recognize the same language.
\item Show that $\mathcal{N}$ is minimal:~any~$\hat{\FSM}$ 
that recognize the same language as~$\mathcal{N}$ 
has at least as many states as $\mathcal{N}$. 
\hint~follow \zcref{exer:SketchOfProofMinimizationWorks}. 
Then do an argument by induction that shows that the start states of the
two are indistinguishable, and if two states $q$ and~$r$ are indistinguishable
then so are the states they transition to on a single-character input.
This gives an association of single $r$'s with at least one $q$, and so there
are at least as many $q$'s as $r$'s. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The algorithm halts at a step when no class splits. 
Such a step must happen eventually
since these machines have only finitely many states.
\item If there is a $\sim_n$ class where some
character sends two states in that class to different $\sim_n$~classes then
the algorithm does not terminate at that step. 
Instead it splits that class and goes to the next step.
So the algorithm cannot end with an ill-defined transition function. 
\item Consider a string, $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
We will show that it is accepted by the machine~$\FSM$ 
if and only if it is accepted
by the other machine~$\mathcal{N}$.  
The argument is by induction, although we will present it informally.

The input machine starts in~$q_0$ while the output machine 
starts in a state $r_0=\eclass_0$ that contains~$q_0$.
The first character of~$\sigma$ moves~$\FSM$ from $q_0$ to some state~$q_{i_1}$
and moves~$\FSM[N]$ from a state that contains~$q_0$ 
to a state that contains~$q_{i_1}$.
The second character of~$\sigma$ moves~$\FSM$ from $q_{i_1}$
to some~$q_{i_2}$,
and moves~$\FSM[N]$ from a state that contains $q_{i_1}$ 
to a state that contains~$q_{i_2}$.
The machines proceed in this way until the string runs out.
At the end, $\FSM$ is in a final state if and only if 
$\FSM[N]$ is in a state that contains that final state, which is itself
a final state of~$\FSM[N]$.
\item As a preliminary, arrange that $\hat{\FSM}$ and $\FSM[N]$ 
don't share any states, by 
renaming so that the states of $\FSM$ are  
$q_0,q_1,\,\ldots$ and the states of $\mathcal{N}$ are
$r_0,r_1,\,\ldots$\sentencespace 
Then perform Moore's algorithm on the union of the two sets of states,
as in \zcref{ex:SketchOfProofMinimizationWorks}.
    
The two machines recognize the same language, so starting both with 
any string $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ on the tape results always in 
the same outcome:~either both machines end in an accepting state or
both end in a rejecting state.
Thus there is no distinguishing extension string between $q_0$ and~$r_0$, that
is, the start states are indistinguishable. 

For an induction argument, suppose that states $q$ and~$r$ are indistinguishable.
We claim that for all $x\in\Sigma$, the state 
$\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(q,x)$ is indistinguishable from $\Delta_{\hat{\FSM}}(r,x)$.
That's because if $\tau$ were a distinguishing string between these
two then $x\concat\tau$ would distinguish between $q$ and~$r$.

This implies that every state $r\in\mathcal{N}$ is indistinguishable
from at least one $q\in\FSM$.
The reason is that in a minimal machine all states must be reachable,
or else we could eliminate the state and get a smaller machine that 
recognizes the same language.
So there is some string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that
$\extended{\Delta}_{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)=r$.
Then $\sigma$ takes the machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ to some state:
$\extended{\Delta}_{\hat{\FSM}}(\sigma)$ is a state in~$\hat{\FSM}$, 
and by the prior 
paragraph it is indistinguishable from~$r$.

We then have a function where each domain state $r$ of $\FSM[N]$
is associated with at least one state $q$ of $\hat{\FSM}$.
Consequently, there are at least as many states in $\FSM$ as there
are in $\FSM[N]$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
There are ways to minimize Finite State machines 
other then the one given in this section.
One is Brzozowski's 
algorithm,\index{Brzozowski's algorithm}\index{minimization of a Finite State machine!Brzozowski's algorithm}
which has the advantage of being 
surprising and fun in that you perform some steps that seem
a bit wacky and unrelated to elimination of states and then at the
end it has worked.
(However, it has the disadvantage of taking worst-case exponential time.)
We will not go through why it works, but we will
walk through the algorithm using this Finite State machine, $\FSM$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min19.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Use Moore's algorithm to minimize it.
\item Instead, get a new machine by taking~$\FSM$, changing the state names
  to be $t_i$ instead of $q_i$, and reversing all the arrows (loops reverse to
  themselves).
  This gives a nondeterministic machine.
  Mark what used to be the initial state as an accepting state and mark 
  what used to be the accepting state as an initial state.
  (This may give a machine with more than one initial state.)
\item Convert this to a deterministic machine, 
  using the powerset method.
  Name the state of this machine~$u_i$.
  Omit unreachable states.
\item Repeat the second item by changing the state names
  from $u_i$ to $v_i$ and reversing all the arrows.
  Mark what used to be the initial state as an accepting state and mark 
  what used to be the accepting state as an initial state.
\item Convert to a deterministic machine and compare 
  with the one in the first item.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  So step~$0$ starts by checkmarking the $q_i,q_j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{3}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &      \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   \\
      \cline{1-2}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1}
   \end{equation*}

  Next, step~$0$ calculates whether any of those $\sim_0$ classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1\in\eclass_{0,1},\,q_1\in\eclass_{0,1}$
                 &$q_2\in\eclass_{0,0},\,q_2\in\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{3}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{$q_0$}   &    &      \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_1$}   &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{$q_2$}   \\
      \cline{1-2}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  There is no splitting.
  The $\sim_0$ classes are the $\sim$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_2}        
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1}
   \end{equation*}
  Here is the minimized machine.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min20.pdf}
  \end{display}
\item
  Here is the machine with the arrows reversed, the nodes renamed, and
  the start node made final, and the final one made a start.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min21.pdf}
  \end{display}
  It is nondeterministic; for instance, two edges leaving
  node~$t_1$ are labeled~\str{a}.
\item This is the table to convert that nondeterministic Finite State
  machine to a deterministic one.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|r|cc}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Node name}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Set of nodes}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}
    \\ \hline
    $u_{0}$  &$\emptyset$          &$u_{0}$  &$u_{0}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $u_{1}$  &$\set{t_0}$          &$u_{0}$  &$u_{0}$  \\  
    $u_{2}$  &$\set{t_1}$          &$u_{7}$  &$u_{2}$  \\  
    $u_{3}$  &$\set{t_2}$          &$u_{0}$  &$u_{5}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $u_{4}$  &$\set{t_0,t_1}$      &$u_{7}$  &$u_{2}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $u_{5}$  &$\set{t_0,t_2}$      &$u_{0}$  &$u_{5}$  \\  
    $u_{6}$  &$\set{t_1,t_2}$      &$u_{7}$  &$u_{7}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $u_{7}$  &$\set{t_0,t_1,t_2}$  &$u_{7}$  &$u_{7}$  \\  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  Here is the arrow diagram.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min22.pdf}
  \end{display}
  The only reachable states are $u_2$ and~$u_7$.
\item Here is the picture.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min23.pdf}
  \end{display}
\item Convert that nondeterministic Finite State
  machine to a deterministic one.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|r|cc}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Node name}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Set of nodes}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}
    \\ \hline
    $w_{0}$  &$\emptyset$          &$w_{0}$  &$w_{0}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $w_{1}$  &$\set{v_2}$          &$w_{0}$  &$w_{1}$  \\  
    $w_{2}$  &$\set{v_7}$          &$w_{3}$  &$w_{2}$  \\  
    \acceptingstate
    $w_{3}$  &$\set{v_2,v_7}$      &$w_{3}$  &$w_{3}$  \\  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  Here is the arrow diagram.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min24.pdf}
  \end{display}
  Once we omit unreachable states, 
  this machine is the same (except for state renaming) as the one in
  the first item.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language $\lang$ recognized by some Finite State machine, let
$\operatorname{rank}(\lang)$ be the smallest number~$n\in\N$
such that~$\lang$ is
accepted by a Finite State machine with~$n$ states.
Prove that for every~$n$ there is a language with that rank.
\begin{answer}\verified
The machine of \zcref{exer:MachineTakesNStepsToMinimize} 
\begin{display}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min06.pdf}
\end{display}
has five states and is minimal for the language described by 
\re{b*ab*ab*ab*a(a|b)*}.
Clearly this generalizes.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{minimization of a Finite State machine|)}\index{Finite State machine!minimization|)}
